# Caprebate — the questions

 **22 September 2026 — THIS IS NO LONGER WHERE QUESTIONS ARE ASKED OR ANSWERED.** The Word document **Caprebate_Questions.docx**, linked from the latest update on this document's board item, is the source of truth for questions and answers (Lakhbir, 22 September 2026). Write every answer there. This document is kept as background, because it has the longer reasoning behind each question. An answer written only here is copied into the Word document before anyone acts on it. The five questions added here on 21 and 22 September are now questions 186 to 190 in the Word document.

 **Every open question in the business, in one place, written so that whoever has to answer one can do it cold.**


 This replaces four separate documents: the tax adviser pack, the surveyor pack, the accountant questions that lived inside an HTML page, and the decision queue. Those all said the same things in different words and none of them was complete on its own.


---

## How to use this

 **Questions are identified by their title, not by a number.** "Can you value a building you have not stood in?" tells a surveyor what you are asking. "Question 1" does not. There are no reference numbers anywhere in this document, on purpose — if you need to talk about one, quote its title.


 **Every question below carries six things**, and if any of them is missing that is a defect in this document rather than a style choice:


|  **The question** |  In plain English, as a title |
| --- | --- |
|  **What we think** |  Our own reading, and how confident we are in it |
|  **An example** |  Real numbers wherever we have them |
|  **Our recommendation** |  Stated as a recommendation, not smuggled in as a fact |
|  **What it unblocks** |  Named by what the work actually is |
|  **An answer block** |  Somewhere to write the answer, on the question itself |

 **H****ow to answer.** Under every question there is a shaded ANSWER box. **Click into it and type between the dotted lines.** That is the whole method — you do not need to send anything back separately, and nobody has to transcribe it. The box is the same on every question so you learn it once.


 **Two lines in that box are doing real work** and are worth thirty seconds each. **H****ow confident are you** changes what we do with the answer: on a tax reading, "high" means we switch a branch on and "medium" means we build it and leave it off. A confident wrong answer is the expensive outcome, so please undersell rather than oversell. And **if you disagree with our recommendation, say so there** — that line exists because four answers so far have answered a slightly different question from the one we asked, and we would much rather be corrected than politely accommodated.


 **Five sections, one per person.** You only need to read your own.

 **What the three are, and where they came from.** A validation brief written on 21 June 2026 carried 31 numbered questions for the two professionals. It was superseded by these registers and by the eleven rulebooks, and archived — but nobody had ever checked, question by question, that all 31 had a home. **When that was finally done, four had none.** Three of them are now here: **“Has anybody actually checked that we are allowed to be called Caprebate?”** for Lakhbir, and **“Are our five sector percentages anywhere near right?”** and **“What counts as too far apart when we test ourselves against a real claim?”** for the surveyor. The fourth described a mechanism the engine no longer has, and folded into the sector-percentages question.


---

## The one rule behind all of this, and it matters most to the tax adviser

 **Where our reading of the law would make a claim bigger, we have deliberately not coded it.**


 We found the point, wrote it up, wrote the code that would implement it, and then left it switched off pending a professional opinion. **There are twenty-four of those switches** — ten until 7 September 2026, eleven until 10 September, twelve until 12 September, when five more were built, nineteen from 13 September, and twenty-four since 14 September, when Phase 132 built five more. Not every one would make a claim bigger if it were switched on: some can only make a claim smaller, and some move money either way. Three of them hold readings we had already shipped, set to keep them live. They sit in the system as prepared, tested, inactive branches. _Corrected 13 September 2__0__26 by Phase 131, before it started work:_ this paragraph said seventeen. The extraction verifier counted nineteen that day, all off. _Corrected again 15 September 2026 by Phase 132:_ the verifier counted twenty-four, all off. The count is taken by running it, not by reading this paragraph.


 We say this first because it tells you how we intend to work. **We are not looking for someone to bless a position we have already taken.** We have not taken it. That is what we are asking you to decide.

## Do we cap the fee, warn about it, or adjust the percentage?

 **This is the biggest open commercial question in the business and it was only discovered on ****4**** September.**


 **What we think.** Our fee is a flat 3% of the capital allowances identified (5% until 16 September 2026; the re-run on 3% is in the notes under this question). The client's tax relief arrives gradually — 18% or 14% a year on main-pool plant, 6% on integral features, on a reducing balance. So the fee falls due at once and the benefit trickles in. We already print a disclosure saying the fee "is not capped by, and may exceed, what you save in tax in a given year". Until 4 September nothing in the system computed what the client actually saves, so that sentence could never be checked against a real case.


 Now it can, and the answer is worse than the disclosure implies.


 **The example, and it is a real one.** A competitor's actual client file — allowances identified £73,740, a 40% taxpayer, first period 2022/23:


|   |  £ |
| --- | --- |
|  Our fee, 5% of £73,740 |  **3,687.00** |
|  Tax the client actually relieves in year one |  **3,405.36** |
|  **The client is out of pocket in year one by** |  **281.64** |
|  Tax relieved across five years |  12,552.20 |
|  Our fee as a share of the five-year benefit |  29% |

 And that is the flattering version. That client's actual letting profits were £2,525, so most of the year-one allowance could not be used at all and became a loss carried forward. Their real year-one cash was **£1,****0****10**. Against £1,010, our fee is **3.65 times** the benefit.


 **The part that is not about one invoice.** Year-one relief is (pool rate × tax rate) of the allowances. Our fee is a flat 5% of the same number. So whether the fee exceeds the benefit is decided almost entirely by _who the client is_:


|  Client |  Year-one relief as a % of allowances |  Against our 5% |
| --- | --- | --- |
|  Limited company, small profits (19%) |  3.42% |  **Fee is bigger** |
|  Limited company, marginal rate (26.5%) |  4.77% |  **Fee is bigger** |
|  Sole trader / partner, basic rate (20%) |  3.60% |  **Fee is bigger** |
|  Sole trader / partner, higher rate (40%) |  7.20% |  Fee is smaller |
|  Sole trader / partner, additional rate (45%) |  8.10% |  Fee is smaller |

 **A limited company's best possible year-one return is ****1****8% × 26.5% = ****4****.77%, and that is below our 5%. There is no mix of plant, at any tax rate, at which a company client's fee does not exceed their year****-****one benefit.** Dental practices, GP federations, care operators and private clinics are limited companies more often than not. And from April 2026 the main-pool rate falls from 18% to 14%, which makes this worse for everybody.


 **It is a timing problem, not a value problem.** Over five years the client is comfortably ahead on every case above. They are behind in year one — the year they pay us.


 **Our recommendation: warn, and adjust the percentage. Do not cap.**


 All three mechanisms are built and all are switched off.


-  **Warn** — a second, case-specific sentence next to the standing disclosure wherever the arithmetic says the fee exceeds year-one relief. It changes the amount by nothing. It is worth doing because the disclosure already promises this may happen, and a client told _on their own numbers_ cannot later say they were not told.


-  **Adjust the percentage** — you have already said the 5% is a variable sales discusses with the client. That needs no switch; it needs a number, and the system now computes one. For any client it will tell you the rate at which the fee stops exceeding their year-one benefit. On the competitor case above that number is **4****.62%**.


-  **Cap** — we would hold this back. On the table above it caps the fee on every company client you take, and that is a pricing policy decision rather than a build one.


 **The harder question underneath, which warning and adjusting do not answer:** is a flat 5% on allowances the right _shape_ of fee at all, when for a whole class of client it is arithmetically guaranteed to exceed what they get back in the year they pay us? That one is worth putting to the tax adviser alongside the technical questions.


 **What it unblocks.** Nothing may be quoted to a client until this is settled. It also decides whether the engagement letter's fee clause is right.

 **Added 9 September 2026 — this arithmetic is now on a screen, so you can answer it against real clients rather than against the competitor file.** The finance section of the back office shows, for every client we have invoiced or are about to: our fee, what they would relieve in their first year and across five, whether the fee exceeds the first year, and **the fee rate at which it would stop doing so**. It is computed at both ends of the marginal band for the entity type, because there is no single rate for a company — and where the two ends disagree it says so rather than picking one. **Nothing about the amount we charge has changed and neither switch has moved.** All this does is put the number in front of the person whose decision it is.

 **Added 11 September 2026 by CRM 7 of 10 — all three are now built into the invoice itself and switched off, and one thing about them matters before you choose.** Warn writes a sentence onto the invoice and changes nothing. **Cap and adjust-the-percentage act only where the client's first-year saving is a single figure, and that needs the client's marginal tax rate recorded — which nothing in the system records.** Switched on today, either would refuse to raise an invoice on every claim rather than pick a number out of a range. So if you choose cap or adjust, the system also needs to ask for, and record, the client's marginal rate.

```
ANSWER
This decision allows us to finalise our pricing policy, start issuing client quotations, and confirm the fee wording in the engagement letter.....................................................................
Your answer:


How confident are you?    High / Medium / Low

Answered by: SM                                    Date: 08.09.26

If you disagree with our recommendation, what should we do instead?


...................................................................... 
```

 **Read 15 September 2026 (Phase 132c).** The box is initialled SM and dated 8 September, and its words restate this question's own "what it unblocks" line — "allows us to finalise our pricing policy, start issuing client quotations, and confirm the fee wording" — without choosing. So it is not an answer, and nothing has changed. Asked again: warn, adjust the percentage, or cap? The flat 3% fee confirmed below changes the arithmetic: on main-pool plant a company's best first-year relief (14% × 26.5% = 3.71%) is no longer below the fee.

 **Re-run on the flat 3% fee, 16 September 2026 (Phase 132e).** Still not answered, and the arithmetic has moved in the client's favour without anyone deciding it. A company's year-one relief on writing-down allowances is 4.77% of the allowances on main-pool plant at 18%, 3.71% at 14%, and 1.59% on special-rate plant at 6%. At 5% the fee beat all three; at 3% it beats only the special-rate case. On the competitor file used as our benchmark (£73,740 of allowances, a 40% taxpayer) the client is now ahead by £1,193.16 in year one on headline figures, but on that client's real profits our fee is still 2.19 times the year-one cash. So the flat 3% narrows the problem to special-rate plant and to clients with low profits; it does not remove it. Asked again: warn, adjust, or cap — or is the move to 3% your answer? The engine's warning and cap stay built and switched off either way.

 **Phase 146, 22 Sep 2026 — the fee-versus-benefit model re-run at 3%, company by company.** The re-run above (16 September) gave the rate-level result. This one runs the engine's own benefit model (the one the back office uses) on £100,000 of allowances for every tax position the engine knows, on each kind of plant. As a check, it was run at 5% first and gave back this question's own figures: 4.77% for the best company case, and 3.42%, 3.60%, 7.20% and 8.10% in the table above. At 3% the fee is £3,000. Year one only, writing-down allowances only, with no first-year allowance established (which is how every client stands today):

|  Client |  Main pool, periods before April 2026 (18%) |  Main pool, from April 2026 (14%) |  Special-rate plant (6%) |  Mixed like the competitor file (46% main, 54% special), from April 2026 |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
|  Company, small profits (19%) |  £3,420 — client ahead by £420 |  **£2,660 — fee bigger by £340** |  **£1,140 — fee bigger by £1,860** |  **£1,842 — fee bigger by £1,158** |
|  Company, main rate (25%) |  £4,500 — ahead by £1,500 |  £3,500 — ahead by £500 |  **£1,500 — fee bigger by £1,500** |  **£2,424 — fee bigger by £576** |
|  Company, marginal band (26.5%) |  £4,770 — ahead by £1,770 |  £3,710 — ahead by £710 |  **£1,590 — fee bigger by £1,410** |  **£2,570 — fee bigger by £430** |
|  Sole trader or partner, basic rate (20%) |  £3,600 — ahead by £600 |  **£2,800 — fee bigger by £200** |  **£1,200 — fee bigger by £1,800** |  **£1,939 — fee bigger by £1,061** |
|  Sole trader or partner, higher rate (40%) |  £7,200 — ahead by £4,200 |  £5,600 — ahead by £2,600 |  **£2,400 — fee bigger by £600** |  £3,879 — ahead by £879 |
|  Sole trader or partner, additional rate (45%) |  £8,100 — ahead by £5,100 |  £6,300 — ahead by £3,300 |  **£2,700 — fee bigger by £300** |  £4,364 — ahead by £1,364 |
|  Scottish partner, top rate (48%) |  £8,640 — ahead by £5,640 |  £6,720 — ahead by £3,720 |  **£2,880 — fee bigger by £120** |  £4,654 — ahead by £1,654 |

 **What changed from 5%.** Across 44 combinations of tax position and plant (the table above, plus the Scottish starter, intermediate, higher and advanced rates), the fee was bigger than year-one relief in 34 at 5%. At 3% it is bigger in 21. **"The fee exceeds every company's year-one relief" is no longer true.** A company paying 25% or 26.5% on main-pool plant is now ahead in year one.

 **What did not change, and one thing the 16 September note missed.** That note said that at 3% the fee "beats only the special-rate case". The re-run shows three more cases:

-  **Special-rate plant.** The fee is bigger for every client at every tax rate. A 3% fee needs a 50% tax rate to be covered at 6%, and no rate in the engine reaches 50%.

-  **Small-profits companies (19%) and basic-rate traders (20%).** From April 2026 the fee is bigger even on pure main-pool plant, because 14% × 19% = 2.66% and 14% × 20% = 2.80%. A 3% fee needs a tax rate of at least 21.43% at 14%, or 16.67% at 18%. A small-profits company whose year starts on 1 January 2026 gets the blended rate of 14.99% and is also behind (£2,848).

-  **Mixed plant, which is what real buildings have.** From April 2026 a company at 25% falls behind once more than 25% of the allowances are special-rate, and a company at 26.5% once more than 33.5% are. For a higher-rate partner the point is 81%, and for an additional-rate partner 92%.

 **Worked example.** A dental company paying 25%, with £100,000 of allowances in its year from April 2026: 60% main pool, 40% special-rate. Year-one relief is 25% × (14% × £60,000 + 6% × £40,000) = £2,700. The fee is £3,000, so the company is £300 behind in year one. Over five years it relieves £10,604.55. On the competitor file (£73,740 of allowances, a 40% taxpayer), the fee is now £2,212.20 against £3,405.36 of headline year-one relief, so the client is ahead by £1,193.16. On that client's real profits of £2,525, the fee is still 2.19 times the £1,010 of year-one cash. At 5% it was 3.65 times.

 **By sector.** The model cannot give a sector-by-sector answer. Sector changes the result only through the mix of plant and the client's tax position. The engine holds no mix of plant by sector: the sector cost library is marked "not yet available", and no question captures a client's mix. So for every client today the system answers "undetermined". For a company at 26.5% that is a year-one range of 1.59% to 3.71%, which straddles 3%. Where a first-year allowance is established the picture changes completely: with Annual Investment Allowance on £100,000, a company at 19% gets £19,000 of relief in year one against a £3,000 fee.

 **Does the recommendation change?** Not by itself. The recommendation is still to warn, adjust the percentage and not cap. **But two sentences it rests on no longer hold at 3%.** First, "there is no mix of plant at which a company client's fee does not exceed their year-one benefit" is now wrong for companies at 25% or 26.5% on main-pool plant. Second, "a cap would cap the fee on every company client" is now too broad. At 3% a cap would bite on special-rate-heavy claims, small-profits companies and basic-rate traders from April 2026, and most companies whose claim is more than about a quarter to a third special-rate. A warning would fire on the same cases. Whether this narrower set changes your choice is your decision; nothing here decides it, and both switches stay off. The script that produced these figures is saved with the Phase 146 working files and re-runs the shipped engine.

## Did you send the two hosting questions, or were "yes and yes" the host's answers?

 **What we think.** On 3 September you replied "Yes and yes" to a message that asked you to send two questions to the website host. It reads equally as _"yes, I will send them"_ and as _"yes, the host said yes to both"_. We have not been able to tell which, and the difference is large.

 **The example.** The two questions were: can the client-document folders be excluded from the nightly whole-site backup, and can the host run Node. If those are _answers_, then the backup risk is closed and a Node service is available beside WordPress — which changes the developer's scope materially. If they are _an intention to ask_, both are still open and one of them is a live data-protection risk.

 **Our recommendation.** One line back: "sent" or "answered — here is what they said". If they were answered, forward the host's reply so it can go on file, because a data-protection control that rests on a remembered conversation is not a control.

 **What it unblocks.** The backup exclusion, the client-document upload page, and part of the developer's scope.

 **Note added 12 September 2026 by Phase 129.** This is asked on the task "❓ Ask the host: can client-document folders be left out of the backup?"; the separate "yes and yes" task was closed into it on 12 September 2026.

## Does the privacy mailbox actually exist and is somebody reading it?

 **What we think.** The domain is live on IONOS with email configured — that much has been verified independently by DNS. Whether `privacy@caprebate.com` has actually been created as a monitored shared mailbox has never been confirmed by anyone who could see it. From outside, a mail server accepting a message proves nothing: most accept mail for any address and bounce later, and some accept everything by design.

 **Why it matters more than it looks.** Since **19 June 2026** the law has required us to make it easy for people to complain about how we handle their data. Four separate duties sit on this one mailbox: complaints, the contact point named in a breach report to the regulator, the published contact details for the data protection officer, and one of our lawful bases for using people's data at all.

 **Our recommendation.** Create it as a **shared** mailbox that more than one person can read, not a forward to a personal inbox — a personal inbox stops working when that person is away, and a complaints channel that stops working is worse than one that never existed. Then send a test message from an outside address and confirm it arrives. That last step is the only proof.

 **What it unblocks.** A statutory duty that has been unmet since June, the breach-reporting route, and the data protection officer's appointment document, which currently has a blank where the address goes.

 **What you have already said about this, and it is the reason the question is narrower than it looks.** In the Word copy of these questions you wrote, against this item: _"This will be done by our internal team."_ That answers **who**, and it is recorded here so it is not asked again. It does not answer **whether it has been done** — which is the only part still open, and the part nobody outside the mailbox can verify. On the linked question about publishing the privacy notice you added: _"These will be completed once we have emails and ICO for final drafts"_, which sets the order — mailbox first, then the Information Commissioner registration, then the notice. We have not treated that sequence as a blocker on our side; everything behind it is built and switched off. _(Carried across from the Word copy on 5 September 2026, before that file was retired, so the only live list of questions is this one.)_

 **One thing has moved since this was written, and it is a separate question rather than an answer to this one.** The runbook that creates this mailbox assumes **Microsoft 365**, and the domain's mail is on **IONOS**. Before anybody creates anything, see **"Are the business mailboxes going on IONOS, where the mail already points, or on Microsoft 365, which the runbook assumes?"**, immediately below. **That one decides **_**where**_**; this one still asks **_**whether it has been done**_**, and it has not.**

## Are the business mailboxes going on IONOS, where the mail already points, or on Microsoft 365, which the runbook assumes?

 **Added 8 September 2026.** For Lakhbir. **This is the question sitting immediately behind the one above** — that one asks whether the privacy mailbox has been created; this one asks _where it gets created_, and nobody has recorded an answer. **Whoever picks up the mailbox runbook hits this at step 2 and there is currently no right way to proceed.**

 **The question.** The mailbox provisioning runbook tells you to verify the domain **in the Microsoft 365 admin centre**, then to add MX, SPF, DKIM and DMARC records. **But the mail is already on IONOS, and those records already exist.** Checked against live DNS on 8 September 2026: `caprebate.com` and `caprebate.co.uk` both answer with `mx00.ionos.co.uk` and `mx01.ionos.co.uk`, SPF is `include:_spf-eu.ionos.com`, and there is a DMARC record published through `dmarc.ionos.co.uk`. Nothing on this board records a decision to move any of that to Microsoft.

 **What we think.** Confidence: **high that there is a real fork here, low on which way you want it.** The runbook was written on 29 August, before the domains were bought, and it assumed Microsoft 365 because the rest of the business runs on Microsoft — OneDrive, Teams, the Word documents. **That is a reasonable assumption and it may still be what you want.** It is simply not what is configured, and the runbook does not know that.

 **Why it is not a tidy-up.** Step 3 says to add MX records. **Following it under a Microsoft answer would replace working mail routing**, and anything sent to the domain during the switch would bounce — including, in the worst case, a data-subject complaint, which is the one class of message we have a statutory duty not to lose. The runbook now carries warnings on both steps, but a warning is not a decision.

 **A worked example of the difference.** Five mailboxes are specified: `lakhbir@`, `dataprotection@`, `hello@`, `accounts@` and `noreply@`. **On IONOS** they exist already in the account that holds the domain: you create them in the control panel, they cost little or nothing beyond the hosting you are already paying for, and there is no DNS work at all. **On Microsoft 365** you need a licence for the personal mailbox, a tenant decision (the runbook already flags that a personal-tenant mailbox carries the same third-party-access and offboarding exposure the OneDrive migration plan raised), domain verification, and a DNS cutover. The shared mailboxes are free of licence on both.

 **Our recommendation. Create them on IONOS, now, and revisit only if something needs Microsoft.** Three reasons. It is where the mail already points, so there is no cutover and no bounce window. It is the fastest route to the one thing that is legally overdue — the data protection mailbox has been an unmet duty since 19 June 2026. And nothing that has been specified anywhere needs an Exchange feature: `dataprotection@` needs to be a **shared mailbox two people can read**, which IONOS does. **If you later want Microsoft for calendar or Teams integration, moving mailboxes then is a known job; leaving the duty unmet meanwhile is not recoverable.**

 **The one thing that would change our answer.** If the plan is for the whole business to live in one Microsoft tenant and you want a single sign-on and a single offboarding story from the start, do it once rather than twice. **Say so and we will re-point the runbook rather than leaving it warning about itself.**

 **What it unblocks.** Steps 2 and 3 of the mailbox provisioning runbook, which are unsafe to run until this is answered; and, behind them, the data protection mailbox at board item 12927874161, on which four separate duties wait.

```
ANSWER
......................................................................

        [ ] IONOS, where the mail already points
        [ ] Microsoft 365 — and the tenant is ..........................
        [ ] something else ...........................................
```

## Which email service should send our emails from contactus@caprebate.com?

 **Added 21 September 2026 by Phase 141.** For Lakhbir. **Staff can now type an email to a client on the client's page, and it says it is from Cap Rebate ****contactus@caprebate.com****.** Every email is saved and shown on the company's timeline. **None is sent**, and the screen says so in plain words: "Saved — emails are not being sent yet." Sending is built and switched off until three things are true: an email service is chosen (this question), that service is named in the privacy notice and in clause 7 of the engagement letter, and the contactus@caprebate.com mailbox exists with its email security records set. The mailbox is a separate task for you on the board, "Create the contactus@caprebate.com mailbox and check its email security records".

 **What we think.** Confidence: **high.** The simplest and safest choice is to send through **IONOS, the company that already runs our email.** Checked against live DNS on 21 September 2026: caprebate.com's mail already goes to IONOS (mx00 and mx01.ionos.co.uk), and its three email security records are already IONOS's — SPF (include:_spf-eu.ionos.com), DKIM (the s1-ionos and s2-ionos keys) and DMARC (set to p=none, which only watches). **And the privacy notice already names IONOS as "our email"**, so no new company would handle client data. The other kind of choice — a sending service such as Postmark, Amazon SES or SendGrid — is better at telling you whether an email arrived, but it is **a new processor**: it must be added to the privacy notice and clause 7 before any client email goes through it, and replies would still need a mailbox somewhere.

 **A worked example.** Sales writes to Dunmarrow's practice manager: "Your survey date". **With IONOS** the server logs in to the contactus@caprebate.com mailbox and sends it; the sent copy sits in that mailbox; the practice manager presses Reply and the reply lands in the same mailbox, where a later phase will read it onto Dunmarrow's timeline. Cost: nothing beyond the IONOS plan you already pay for. **With a sending service** the email goes out through that company's servers; you pay by volume (at our size, a few pounds a month or less); the reply still goes to contactus@, so you run two services instead of one. At a few hundred emails a month, IONOS's sending limits are not a concern.

 **Our recommendation. IONOS, sending from the ****contactus@caprebate.com**** mailbox.** It adds no processor, it is already set up for our domain, and replies arrive where the email came from. Reconsider only if you decide the business mailboxes move to Microsoft 365 (the question just above this one) — then send through Microsoft 365 instead, for the same reasons. **What we will do on your answer:** set the switch's provider, add the IONOS login to the server, and after one test email to you, switch sending on. If you choose a sending service instead, we add it to the privacy notice and clause 7 as a draft for the DPO and solicitor, and write a second adapter beside the one already built.

 **What it unblocks.** Real email from the back office: free-typed emails, template emails, booking invitations and password resets, all of which are saved and none of which is sent today. Also reading replies onto the company's chat and timeline — built by Phase 142 on 22 September 2026 and switched off until the mailbox exists; the same answer switches it on, as a read-only reader of the contactus@caprebate.com mailbox.

## Where do Microsoft, monday.com and IONOS actually hold our data — can you check our own accounts?

 **Added 15 September 2026 by Phase 132a. For Lakhbir.** Nothing is blocked. But every document that tells a client where their data is now says only what the providers allow, not where ours actually sits. It sits here, beside the mailbox question, because both are about the same three accounts.

 **The question.** The list of who holds client data, and the letters that answer a request for someone's own data, now say what Microsoft, monday.com and IONOS each publish about where they keep data. None of that says where our own accounts are, because each provider decides it by how and where the account was first set up. Only someone who holds the accounts can look. **Where are ours?**

 **What we think.** Confidence: **medium**. Microsoft keeps mailbox and file content in the UK for a tenant set up in the UK, and its admin centre shows the location. monday.com has no UK region: an account sits in its US or its EU region depending on where the first user was when it was opened, and a Pro account reaches the EU region only if it was opened on or after 23 January 2023; login and profile data are held in the US either way. IONOS says it processes in the UK or the European Economic Area unless a transfer becomes necessary, names no location for a UK customer's mailboxes, and lists a German company as the provider of its mail service. All three allow transfers out of the UK under standard contract terms.

 **An example.** A care-home client asks where their documents and emails are kept. Today the honest answer is "the UK or Europe for email and files, and the US or Europe for our task board". With three checks it becomes three named places.

 **Our recommendation.** Three short checks: in the Microsoft 365 admin centre, the data location shown under the organisation profile; in monday.com, the account region shown under Administration, then General; and a support request to IONOS asking in which country our mailboxes are stored. Write the three answers in the box.

 **What your answer unblocks.** Stating where client data actually is in the list of who holds it, in the letters that answer an access request, and in the privacy notice — instead of what the providers permit.

## Buy the Spon's price books, or ask the surveyor first?

 **What we think — and this changed on 3 September.** The £330 purchase has been sitting open for weeks. A real competitor's client report arrived and it prices an entire invoiced job on **BCIS elemental costs** — every line annotated "BCIS Element costs", in pounds per square metre. **Spon's does not appear anywhere in it.**

 **The example.** Their cost schedule reads: substructure £120/m², frame £110/m², external walls £190/m², space heating £106/m². All BCIS. We had also already established that the Spon's books cover hospitals and GP surgeries and contain **nothing for dental practices or care homes** — the two markets you intend to open with.

 **CORRECTION AND NEW FACTS — 6 September 2026.** Two things above are now wrong, and the situation has changed underneath the question.

 **One.** "Contains nothing for dental practices or care homes" is half wrong. The Architects' & Builders' 2024 edition prices **residential homes at £95,000–£135,000 per bed and nursing homes at £120,000–£170,000 per bed**, both at 40–60 m² per bedroom, in its Building Prices per Functional Unit section. Care homes **are** covered. Dental practices genuinely are not — every apparent match in the book is the word "incidental" or "accidental".

 **Two, and this is the bigger one. Three Spon's books have been sitting on the disk since 31 August** — Mechanical & Electrical 2021 and 2022, and Architects' & Builders' 2024 — roughly 83 MB, alongside a fourth file that was a byte-for-byte duplicate of the 2022 one. Nobody recorded where they came from. **They do not settle the £330 question and they must not be used until somebody establishes how we came by them.** See the separate question below.

 **Our recommendation. Do not buy anything yet.** On the free surveyor call, ask one question: _"Do you subscribe to BCIS, and would you price a capital allowances residual cost schedule off BCIS elemental rates?"_ If yes, the £330 decision disappears — and most cost surveyors do subscribe. Spon's becomes the fallback question, not the first one.

 **Ask this in the same breath, because it decides whether one of our numbers is wrong:** BCIS rates conventionally hold preliminaries _outside_ the unit rate; a Valuation Office replacement cost holds them _inside_. Which source we use therefore decides whether our own 18% preliminaries uplift is correct or is a double count that inflates every job we produce.

 **One honest limit.** This is one report on one furnished holiday let. It shows what one firm does. It does not prove BCIS is the profession's standard for healthcare property — which is exactly why it is a question for the surveyor rather than a conclusion.

 **What it unblocks.** A £330 purchase decision, and the preliminaries question that sits behind a known arithmetic error in every job.

## Where did the Spon's price books on our disk come from, and may we put their rates in a client report?

 **What we think.** Three Spon's PDFs have been sitting in the project folder since 31 August 2026 — Mechanical & Electrical 2021 and 2022, and Architects' & Builders' 2024. Nobody wrote down where they came from, and there are two separate problems with them.

 **The first is provenance, and it is the one that decides everything else.** Every page of the 2022 Mechanical & Electrical book carries, printed in its own header, the file path of the machine it was typeset on: a `OneDrive-Informaplc` folder belonging to a named user, under `SPON/MandE/dtp`. That appears **816 times** in the file. Informa plc is the parent company of Taylor & Francis, who publish Spon's. **That is the publisher's internal pre-press output, not a copy sold to a customer.** We do not know how it reached this business, and until somebody does, we cannot treat it as a licensed source. Both books also carry the ordinary all-rights-reserved notice, which specifically forbids reproduction "in any information storage or retrieval system" without written permission — and loading rate tables into our engine is exactly that.

 **The second is commercial use, and it applies even to a book we have lawfully bought.** Our own specification already states the position: a single-user reading licence permits **manual transcription of individual cited rates, not a data feed**. So even a legitimate £330 purchase would not let us load Spon's tables wholesale into the engine. What it would let us do is what the specification describes — quote a rate on a report line with its edition, section and page, for a surveyor to endorse.

 **An example of why this is not academic.** The engine has three empty tables waiting on exactly this data. `CB_LOCATION_FACTORS` wants the regional adjustment that turns a national rate into a Cardiff one. If we fill it from the Architects' & Builders' 2024 book we would be copying a table of fourteen numbers out of a copyrighted work into software we sell a service on. If we fill it from the free Valuation Office cost guide instead, there is no question to answer.

 **Our recommendation — three things, in this order.** **One: do not use these files, and do not delete them either.** They have been moved to `07-reference-and-benchmarks/price-books/` and left alone. **Two: tell us how they arrived** — if you or a colleague bought them, that closes the provenance point and only the licensing one remains. **Three: if we do need a published cost anchor, buy the current edition properly**, and treat it as a citation source rather than a database.

 **And one thing we would rather you did first.** Our own developer brief already recommends running at **£0 subscriptions**, seeding rates from the free Valuation Office and NHS cost guides plus the client's own invoices, and adding Spon's later only for credibility. The specification separately records that the **free Valuation Office rating cost guide already supplies location factors, the fee scale and contract-size adjustments** — which is most of what we wanted the books for. If that is right, the £330 buys presentation, not capability.

 **Note added 14 September 2026 by Phase 131: the free Valuation Office guide may not be free to use this way.** The paragraph above, and the example before it, assume the Valuation Office's rating cost guide can seed our rates with no question to answer. Its 2026 edition, read on 13 September 2026, sits on a GOV.UK page that offers the Open Government Licence "except where otherwise stated", and the guide itself says it "must not be used for any other purpose". So using it is a licensing question too, and it is now asked in the Valuation Office email. Until that reply arrives, do not seed any table from it. The Valuation Office became part of HMRC on 1 April 2026.

 **What it unblocks.** Whether we may hold and use these three files at all; and the three empty cost tables behind the two engine fixes that are currently tagged as blocked on this purchase.

## Is AML supervision legally required for this business?

 **What we think.** You asked this on 3 September. **The answer already existed on the board and had never been given to you**, which is our failure rather than an open question.

 **The short answer: probably yes, and it is worth an hour of the accountant's time to confirm.** Capital allowances advice provided by way of business is capable of falling within the definition of a "tax adviser" for money-laundering purposes, and a tax adviser must be supervised — by HMRC if not by a professional body. The registration is not expensive, but operating unsupervised where supervision is required is a criminal offence rather than a compliance gap.

 **Our recommendation.** Put it to the accountant as one of the twelve questions in their section, and to the tax adviser if they are engaged first. Do not act on our reading alone — this is a legal threshold question and we are not qualified to settle it.

 **What it unblocks.** Nothing technical. It is a "do not trade without checking" item rather than a blocker, and it sits with incorporation.

## How late may a client move their own call, and may they cancel it outright?

 **THIS IS ALREADY BUILT AND WORKING TO OUR RECOMMENDATION.** Nothing is waiting on you. It is here because both halves are business decisions rather than technical ones, and you should get the chance to overrule them cheaply. Changing the first is one number; changing the second is a small piece of work.

 **What we think.** A client can now change their own appointment from their own portal, instead of telephoning us — which is the one thing a portal exists to avoid. Two questions had to be answered before it could be built, and rather than stop, we answered them and built it.

 **Our recommendation on the first: two hours' notice.** A client may not move a call inside two hours of it, and may not move one _into_ the next two hours either — otherwise the rule buys nobody any notice, it only changes which call is the imminent one. Inside the window the control is not drawn at all and the screen says to telephone us instead.

 **The example.** A client with a call at eleven realises at nine that it will not work. They move it themselves and nobody is inconvenienced. The same client at ten to eleven cannot — by then the adviser has the file open and may be dialling — so they ring us and a person deals with it.

 **Our recommendation on the second: no self-service cancellation, and this is the more consequential of the two.** A cancel button that leaves a client with no appointment and no conversation is a lead going cold in one click, and it is worse for them as well: their claim simply stops. What we built instead is _give the time back_ — the slot goes into the pool for somebody else, the enquiry stays open with everything else about it unchanged, and the call reappears in the back office as one waiting for a time. The alternative times are shown **above** that option, so "none of these work" is something a person reaches after looking rather than instead of looking.

 **What it costs to change your mind.** The two hours is a single named number in the booking code; any other figure is a one-line change and the screens quote it automatically. A real cancellation is a small build, not a switch — and if you want one, say whether it should still offer a new time first.

 **What it unblocks.** Nothing. It is running. Answering only tells us whether to change it.

## At account creation, does "skip" mean skip the portal, or a genuinely manual product?

 **What we think.** The funnel offers an optional account at the end. Skipping cannot mean skipping the email address, because an engagement letter has to be delivered, the client has to receive a copy, and the audit trail has to show both. There is no version of that without an email.

 **The example.** A client who gives only a phone number can still be served — but that is a phone conversation and a posted letter, handled by an adviser. It is a manual product, not a funnel step.

 **Our recommendation.** "Skip" means **skip the password and the portal, not the email**. The claim is then handled by an adviser and the letter is sent rather than presented on screen. Keep the fully manual, no-email path as something you do for a client who asks, not as a route the website offers.

 **What it unblocks.** The account screen and the letter-delivery route behind it.

## What does Homedata actually cost per lookup?

 **Research note, 12 September 2026, Phase 129.** Prices read that day from homedata.co.uk/pricing and homedata.co.uk/docs/pricing. **Homedata sells tokens, not lookups:** one address lookup costs **7 tokens** (8 if the property record is filled in too). Free is 100 tokens a month — **about fourteen lookups, not a hundred** — and Starter is £49 for 4,500 tokens, about 7.6p a lookup if every token is used. The fuller note, with every plan, is under _"What does a Homedata address lookup cost us each time it runs?"_

 **Corrected 17 September 2026 (Phase 133) — the prices have changed again.** Homedata's pricing page, read on 17 September 2026, says "100 tokens = £1": pay as you go, with **no plans, no subscription and no free monthly allowance**. Tokens never expire, and the first top-up is matched (£10 buys 2,000 tokens). A search costs 2 tokens, the full address 5 and a property record 1, so **one lookup is 7p** (8p with the property record). A free playground tests the API by hand. The note above — a Free plan of 100 tokens a month, Starter at £49 — is out of date, and so is the recommendation below: there is no free trial to end, and Scotland is not in launch scope. Whether we need a paid lookup at all is now asked in "Do we need a paid address lookup at launch, or do we match the address the client types, as your last company did?"_ (Corrected 24 September 2026, Phase 147: "one lookup is 7p" is THE SUPPLIER'S find-then-retrieve, and is still right for that call. OUR lookup, as built on 23 September 2026, is 8 tokens, 8p a property: the postcode search 2, the address 5 and a check for a registered title 1.)_


 **Note added 10 September 2026 by the Phase 114 sweep — this question is asked twice in this section, and the two copies disagree.** Further down, **"What does a Homedata address lookup cost us each time it runs?"** asks for the same price. It was written on 8 September on the premise this question corrected that same day: that the address lookup fires at the front of the funnel, before anybody signs. **Under your own rule of 4 September a paid call fires only after a signed engagement and an operator's click** — so if the lookup costs anything at all, part (d) of that question is already decided by that rule, and the lookup sits in the paid tier. **Write the price once, in either box, and it answers both.** Neither copy is deleted, because the other carries the per-property and volume-band parts this one does not ask.

 **What we think.** Homedata is the address-lookup supplier. We do not have the rate card. **CORRECTED**** ****8 Sep****t****ember**** ****2026: th****is ****paragraph used to say Homedata was "****the only per-claim cost incurred before a client signs anything". ****It is not a pr****e****-signatu****r****e cost at all an****y**** more.** Your rule of 4 September — free calls once the client has been told, paid calls only after a signed engagement and only when someone clicks Run the research — makes the address lookup a paid call that fires after signature. Everything the funnel itself can reach (the energy certificate register, Companies House, the care regulator, price paid data) is free.


 **Why it matters, and it has changed si****n****ce**** ****this**** ****q****u****e****st****ion wa****s ****writt****e****n.** It used to be the number that decided whether paid traffic was viable, because an abandoned funnel cost one lookup and a five-property enquiry cost five. **Under your rule,**** an abandoned ****funnel ****now cost****s ****noth****i****ng —**** t****her****e**** i****s ****no p****a****id**** ****call in front of th****e**** ****si****gnature.** So the rate card no longer prices your wasted traffic. ## What replaces the paid address autocomplete in the funnel?

 **What we think.** Your rule of 4 September settled that paid calls only fire after a signed engagement. The address autocomplete is a paid call. So the thing that currently sits at question 2 of the funnel — type three characters, pick your address from a list, and we quietly look up everything else about the building — **cannot run there any more.**

 **This does not break the short funnel, and it is worth saying that first.** The four registers the front door leans on are all free, and every one of them can be searched on a typed postcode instead of on a bought address ID. What the paid lookup buys is **certainty that we matched the right building**, not permission to look. So the design still works: search free on a typed postcode, show the client what we found, and let them correct it. The paid lookup runs after signature and upgrades the match.

 **What is actually being asked.** Two things, and only the first needs money.

1.  **What does a visitor type into, at question 2, instead of a paid autocomplete?** A plain postcode-and-address box costs nothing and works. A cheaper or free autocomplete would be nicer. There are options — the Ordnance Survey and Royal Mail both sell address data, and some are free at low volume — and none has been priced.

2.  **How good is a postcode-only match?** We do not know, and we are not going to guess. The matching specification says to measure the failure rate on **200 real properties** and nobody has done it. That measurement decides how confident we can be before we show a client a figure.

 **An example.** A dental practice at a shared address — three units behind one postcode. On a bought address ID we know exactly which unit. On a postcode alone we may get the right building and the wrong unit, which means the wrong floor area, which moves the estimate. How often that happens is the thing nobody has counted.

 **Our recommendation.** Build the funnel against a **plain typed postcode and address** for now — it costs nothing, it is honest, and it does not block anything. Take the 200-property measurement before setting how confident the funnel has to be. Only buy an autocomplete if the measurement says typed addresses are losing you good enquiries.

 **What it unblocks.** The front door of the assessment — the developer needs to know what to build at question 2 before quoting for it. Nothing else waits on it.

## Is the small end of the market worth serving at all?

 **Note added 12 September 2026 by Phase 129.** The minimum is now asked **once**, at the end of this section, under _"Is our fee now a flat 3% of the allowances identified — with no minimum and no bands?"_ That question quotes your answer of 1 September — _"No minimum — smallest job is a £0 purchase price, and the fee is a set percentage"_ — and the fee table you wrote in the answer box below. Answer it there; the two readings set out below stand until you do.

 **What we think.** There is no minimum job size — that was decided on 1 September. So a very small claim can cost more to produce than it earns, and nothing currently stops one being taken.

 **The example.** A £40,000 allowance at a 5% fee earns £2,000. If that job needs a surveyor visit with a travel day, it loses money before anyone has written a report. If it can be done from a desk, it is profitable. **Which of those it is depends entirely on the surveyor's answer about desktop valuation** — which is the first question in their section.

 **Our recommendation.** Do not set a minimum until the surveyor has answered. Then set one, and set it in pounds of _allowances identified_ rather than property value, because that is what the fee is charged on.

 **What it unblocks.** Pricing, and the decision about whether there is a cheaper desk-only product for small jobs.

 **Rewritten 9 September 2026, and this question is now sharper and more urgent than it was.** The five pricing documents have been rebuilt on the decided fee base — 5% of the allowances identified rather than 5% of the tax saved — and that changes the answer to this question in two ways.

 **First, the arithmetic. The small end is much smaller than we thought.** A job now covers its fully loaded cost from a purchase price of about **£319,000** for dental, **£383,000** for a private hospital, **£417,000** for a care home and **£429,000** for a GP surgery, at a hundred jobs a year. At a realistic year-one volume of twenty-five jobs those figures are **£479,000 / £486,000 / £537,000 / £589,000**. Below them a job loses money. Above them it does not.

 **Second — and this is the part only you can settle — there are two live readings of what you decided on 1 September, and both papers on this board now price both of them.** You decided there is no minimum **job size**. The research distinguishes that expressly from a minimum **fee**, and both rebuilt papers still carry a recommended launch minimum of £5,000 for dental and GP and £7,500 for care homes and private hospitals. **The rate card cannot publish a floor that may have been retired, so it needs your word either way.**

|   |  **Reading 1 — the £5,000 / £7,500 minimum still stands** |  **Reading 2 — there is no minimum, only the 5%** |
| --- | --- | --- |
|  Fee on the plan's assumed book of a hundred jobs |  £18,325 a job |  **£18,325 a job — identical** |
|  Fee on a £300,000 dental practice |  £5,000 |  £3,375 |
|  Does that £300,000 job pay at a hundred jobs a year? |  **Yes**, margin £1,410 |  **No**, short by £215 |
|  What protects the small end |  The minimum |  Only the engagement gate — built 9 Sep 2026 and switched OFF
 |

 **The two readings price the whole assumed book identically**, because on the decided base the percentage overtakes the minimum at a purchase price of about **£444,000** for dental and GP, £500,000 for a care home and £429,000 for a private hospital — and every job in the assumed mix is above that. **So this is a decision about the smallest jobs and about how we describe ourselves, not about revenue.**

 **Our recommendation is unchanged in substance and now has numbers behind it.** Keep the £5,000 / £7,500 minimum as a floor until the surveyor has answered the desktop-valuation question, then re-set it. **And switch the engagement gate on**, because on the decided base the year-one loss band sits just ABOVE the crossover — between roughly £444,000 and £479,000 for dental — where no minimum can see it, and the gate is the only mechanism that reaches it. **This sentence said "and build the engagement gate" until 9 September 2026, when it was built. It is in the engine now and its switch is OFF, because whether it fires is your decision and not ours — see the question below.**


 **One thing we cannot tell you.** How much of the real book sits below £444,000 depends on a distribution of purchase prices nobody has measured. On the assumed mix the minimum never fires at all. **That is a question for the first twenty enquiries, not for a paper.**

## Below what size of claim do we advise a client not to claim at all?

 **Added 14 Se****p****tember 2026 b****y ****Phase 131. For Lakhb****i****r, a****n****d**** ****the tax advise****r**** th****e****n ****s****ee****s ****the wording.** Nothing is blocked. The no-claim report is drafted, and lists this as one of its open items.

 **The questi****o****n.** The report a client gets when there is no claim, the reasoned negative, fires on nine outcomes. One is "below the recommendation threshold": the relief is real but too small to be worth pursuing. The report says the threshold "is not yet set", that it should be expressed as a benefit rather than a percentage of price, and that it is a business decision; the client journey names the same open item. **Below what figure do we tell a client, in writing, that a real claim is not worth making?**

 **What we think.** Confidence: **low on the figure, medium on the sha****p****e**. This threshold could mean two different things, and only one of them is this question. **(a) The client's side:** the relief left after our fee and the cost of amending a return is too small to be worth the client's trouble. **(b) Our side:** the job costs us more to produce than the fee earns. (b) is the engagement gate, already asked under _"Do we __s__witch__ __the engagement gate on — and how many jobs are we actually going to do this year?"_; if the no-claim report were used for (b), it would quietly become a minimum job size, which you removed on 1 September. So we think this question is only about (a). At a 5% fee and a 25% tax rate our fee is a fifth of the relief, so on our fee alone the client always comes out ahead. What makes a small claim not worth it is the timing and the client's other costs.

 **An exam****p****le,**** ****w****i****th illustrative figures.** A claim identifies £8,000 of allowances, and our fee is £400. The relief at 25% is about £2,000 over the life of the pool, but through writing-down allowances at the 14% main rate it is £280 in the first year. Suppose the client's accountant charges £500 to amend the return: the client is **£620 worse off after the first year**, and about £1,100 better off over many years. With unused annual investment allowance, the whole £2,000 comes in the first year and the answer is plainly "claim". Separately, on our own costing that job costs us £2,645 from the desk at year-one volumes — the engagement gate's problem, not a reason to tell the client not to claim.

 **Our recommendation.** Set it on the client's side only, as **the tax rel****i****ef the clie****n****t would receive over the life of the claim, after our fee**, and have the report print the figures and leave the choice to the client rather than refusing the work. As a starting figure for you to change, we suggest **£****1****,****000**; it is not derived from anything. Then let the tax adviser read the wording before the report issues.

 **What your answer unblocks.** One of the four open items in the no-claim report; the "below the recommendation threshold" outcome, which today cannot fire; and the matching open item in the client journey.

The tax adviser reads the wording before any such report issues.

 **Built 16 September 2026 (Phase 132e).** The "below the recommendation threshold" outcome can now fire. When the client's tax relief minus our fee is below £1,000, the result prints the relief, our fee and the relief after our fee, says we would not normally recommend claiming, and says the choice is the client's. It refuses no work, withholds no quote and moves no figure. Where the relief is not known — a tax rate or pool split "not sure" or never given — it does not fire and never treats the relief as nil. An example: £20,000 of special-rate plant for a company at 25% gives £1,330.48 of relief over five years, our fee is £600, and £730.48 after the fee is below the line. The same wording is in the no-claim report and the client journey. **All of the wording is marked for the tax adviser.** **One choice was made in building it, and it is yours to confirm:** the engine measures five years of relief, while the documents say "over the life of the claim". ## One tax summary for the client, or two — the cash now and the relief to come?

 **Added 14 Se****p****tember 2026 b****y ****Phase 131. For Lakhb****i****r, a****n****d**** ****the tax adviser then reads the ****r****esult.** Nothing is blocked. The tax implication summary is drafted as one document and waits on the tax adviser either way.

 **The question.** A competitor's complete client deliverable, which we hold, carries **tw****o** tax implication summaries: one for the current years, showing the cash consequence — the repayment due, the revised payments on account, the loss reconciliation — and one for the future years, showing the pools running down. Ours is one document. When three features of that report were copied into the surveyor's template on 12 September, this one was deliberately left out, because it is "a decision about which documents a client receives", not about the template. **Do we issue one summary, or two?**

 **What we think.** Confidence: **medium**. The reason to split is not tidiness: it lets a client be told two different things as two different promises. Our own house rule already says relief and cash are different, and restricts "refund" to the routes that actually repay tax. A current-years summary is where that rule is easiest to keep, because it holds only what moves money now. Against that, two documents are two things to version, to have the tax adviser sign, and to keep consistent with each other and with the client report, which already carries a cash position and a forward schedule.

 **An example, from the c****o****m****p****etitor's**** ****ow****n ****re****p****o****r****t.** Its cover letter can say **"£1,010.00 repayment now"** and **"£23,184 over the next few years"** side by side, because each figure comes from its own summary. In one combined schedule the reader has to find the break between them, and a client who reads £24,194 as one number has been told something that is not true.

 **Our recommendation.** **One document in tw****o**** clearly se****p****arate parts**, "what changes this year and last" and "what the pools do from here". That gives the client the two promises without a second document to keep in step. If you would rather match the competitor exactly, two documents is a legitimate answer.

 **What your answer unblocks.** The layout of the tax implication summary the tax adviser is being asked to sign, and of its sole-trader and partnership versions; and the cover letter's headline, which can only state the two figures separately once the summary does.

## May we use Scottish energy certificate data at all, and only from the published quarterly file?

 **Added 14 Se****p****tember 2026 b****y ****Phase 131. For Lakhb****i****r, to**** p****ut to the solicito****r****.** Nothing is blocked. Scotland is not served, and this matters only on the day the question about Scotland's trigger is answered.

 **The questi****o****n.** The register licensing review read the Scottish energy certificate register's terms on 26 August 2026, and asked the solicitor whether we can use Scottish certificate data for fee-earning work at all — one of its matters for the solicitor, never written into this document. **The terms were re-read on ****1****3**** September 2026****, at version 11****.****7.0.0, and have not moved.** They still say that commercially exploiting the information "in any way" is "strictly prohibited", and that all access "via any automated software agent is prohibited". **They give no route to apply for permission.** **May we use the data, and by what route?**

 **What we think.** Confidence: **high that the register's own website is closed to us, low on the alternative**. Automated lookups against it are prohibited in terms, there is nobody to ask for an exception, and we would not build them. There is a second route the review did not consider. The Scottish Government publishes the non-domestic certificate data as a dataset on statistics.gov.scot, read on 13 September 2026: current certificates from 2016 to the first quarter of 2026, next update due in the third quarter of 2026. Its page says the non-address data is under the **Open Government Licence v3.0**, and the address data is subject to **Royal Mail copyright restrictions**, with their own list of permitted uses. The jurisdiction matrix already assumed Scotland would be a quarterly bulk file rather than a lookup. Whether that page's licence governs our use of the same underlying data, whatever the register's portal terms say, is exactly what we are not qualified to say. The page did not say whether floor area is included.

 **An exam****p****le.** An Edinburgh care home asks for a claim once Scotland is served. **Th****ro****u****g****h the ****re****gi****s****ter'****s ****website**, each lookup by our system is an automated access the terms forbid, with no way to ask for one to be allowed. **Through the published file**, we download it once a quarter, find the certificate by postcode on our own server, use the non-address fields under the Open Government Licence, and use the address only within what the Royal Mail terms permit.

 **Our recommendati****o****n.** Never automate against the register's website, and do not spend time seeking a permission its terms offer no route to. Plan Scotland on the statistics.gov.scot quarterly file, and ask the solicitor to confirm three things together: that its licence applies to our use; which uses the Royal Mail address terms permit; and that nothing in the register's portal terms reaches data obtained that way.

 **What your answer unbl****o****cks.** One of the four new sources Scotland needs, in the jurisdiction matrix's sizing; and one of the solicitor's matters in the register licensing review. **Added**** 15 September 2026**** by Phase 132a — the published file has now been read at source****.** The Scottish Government's file of non-domestic energy certificates on statistics.gov.scot gives its non-address fields under the Open Government Licence v3.0 and its addresses under a Royal Mail notice limited to listed purposes. The page itself says the licence is "Not supplied", describes it as restrictive or unrecognised, states a research purpose and says the site is no longer updated. Finding a client's record means using the address fields, so the Royal Mail purpose list would apply to us. The register licensing review now records this beside the Scottish register's own terms, and its fourth matter for the solicitor asks it. The answer written for this question reads "Not targeting Scotland", so nothing waits on this today; it goes to the solicitor when Scotland is planned.

## How far does our free support go if HMRC opens an enquiry?

 **Added**** ****14**** ****Se****p****tembe****r**** 2026**** ****by**** Phase 13****1. For Lakhbir****, ****and then the insurer.** Nothing is blocked. But the promise below is already written into the drafted cover note an accountant receives, and it has no limit.

 **The question.** The accountant's cover note says: _"If HMR__C__ asks a questi__o__n, call us first. We will su__p__port __y__ou__ __on anyth__in__g in the__ __valuation or the a__p__po__r__ti__o__nment at no char__g__e, fo__r__ as long as th__e__ claim i__s__ capable of enquiry."_ The cover's own open items say that "for as long as the claim is capable of enquiry" is open-ended and needs "a defined boundary before it is made in writing. Business decision, then insurance." The governance policies make the same promise, and now mark it as pending this question. The engagement letter the client signs says nothing about enquiry support at all. **What exactly do we promise, for how long, and how much of it is free?**

 **What we think.** Confidence: **medium**. It is the right promise commercially: an accountant who refers to us has to know what happens when HMRC writes, and the cover note exists to tell them. But three things are unbounded. **How lon****g****:** the cover itself says an enquiry may land "this year or in six years". **How much:** one enquiry can take an hour, or several days of surveyor and adviser time. **What it covers:** "anything in the valuation or the apportionment" is a scope, but it does not say whether it runs to a tribunal. It also sits next to clause 13: if the enquiry reduces the claim, we refund part of the fee as well as paying for the support.

 **An exam****p****le, with**** i****llustrative hours.** A dental practice's claim identifies £300,000 of allowances, so our fee is £15,000. Three years later HMRC opens an enquiry and reduces the allowances to £240,000, so under clause 13 we credit £3,000. If answering the enquiry takes twelve hours of specialist time at the £100–£250 an hour our own costing uses, that is **£1,200 to £3,000 mor****e**, unpaid. On the same claim we have given back up to £6,000 of a £15,000 fee, and nothing in writing today stops a second enquiry on the same claim costing the same again.

 **Our rec****o****mmendation.** Keep the promise and bound it three ways, in the same words in the cover note, the governance policies and the engagement letter: **(1) ****s****cope** — the valuation and the apportionment only, supporting the client's accountant, and not representation at a tribunal; **(2) duration** — any enquiry opened within six years of our report, matching the six years the claim's records are kept; **(3) volume** — free up to a stated number of hours per claim, then at a stated hourly rate. Before it goes into the letter, confirm that the professional indemnity policy, once there is one, covers it.

 **What your answer unblocks.** The last open item in the accountant's cover note, so it can be issued; a sentence in the engagement letter, which today is silent; and the pending line in the governance policies.

## What do we charge for work on a disposal, where no allowances are identified?

 **Added 14 Se****p****tember 2026 b****y ****Phase 131. For Lakhbir.** Nothing is blocked today. But the disposal note states no fee, and says only that the fee is agreed separately, so no client can be quoted for this work until it is answered.

 **The questi****o****n.** Our fee is a percentage of the capital allowances identified. The disposal note is different work: the election a client signs when they sell a property whose fixtures they have claimed on. On a disposal no new allowances are identified for the seller, so the percentage comes to nothing. The note flags this as a business decision for you, to settle alongside the minimum fee, and it has never been asked. **What is the fee on a disposal?**

 **What we think.** Confidence: **medium**. What a seller gets from disposal work is one of four things: a balancing charge avoided or reduced; a better election figure negotiated; a valid election instead of a defective one; or nothing that can be measured, where the answer is "sign nothing until this clause changes" and the clause then changes. A percentage of the allowances identified fits none of the four. The nearest thing to a base is the figure fixed by the election, and that is the number buyer and seller are negotiating, so a fee on it would give us an interest in how the negotiation comes out. This does not depend on whether the claim fee is 5% or 3%, which is asked under _"Is our fee now a flat 3% of the allowances identified — with no minimum and no __b__ands?"_: neither reaches a disposal.

 **An example, with illustrative figures.** A company sells a care home for £2,400,000. It had claimed £400,000 on the fixtures; suppose its pool now stands at £190,000 and holds nothing else. The buyer's solicitor asks for an election at £400,000. That brings £400,000 into the seller's pool against £190,000: a balancing charge of £210,000, which is £52,500 of tax at 25%. An election at £190,000 brings no charge. A 5% fee on the election figure would be £20,000 on the first and £9,500 on the second, so **we would earn £****1****0,****5****00**** ****mor****e**** for ****t****h****e**** outco****m****e**** that costs ou****r ****client £5****2****,50****0**. A 5% fee on allowances identified is £0 on both.

 **Our rec****o****mmendation.** A **fixed fee for the review and the drafted election**, costed from the job-economics model the way the break fee was, not guessed — and no percentage of the election figure under any reading. The disposal note's own suggestion is a fixed fee plus a separate basis where a balancing charge is genuinely avoided; if you want that, say what the separate basis is, because a share of the charge avoided rewards us for the seller's side of a negotiation the buyer is also relying on.

 **What y****o****ur answer unblocks.** The fee sentence in the disposal note, which today says only that the fee "is agreed separately"; an engagement letter for disposal work, which does not exist; and the disposal note's own open item, which points at the minimum-fee task on the board.

```
ANSWER
............?..........................................................
The fee on a disposal:
   A fixed fee only  /  A fixed fee plus a separate basis where a charge is avoided  /  Something else

If a separate basis, what is it?


Should we cost the fixed fee from the job-economics model?    Yes / No - use £..........

How confident are you?    High / Medium / Low

Answered by:                                     Date:

If you disagree with our recommendation, what should we do instead?


......................................................................
```

 **Read 15 September 2026 (Phase 132c).** The box holds only "?", so it is not an answer. The disposal note still says the fee "is agreed separately", and no client can be quoted for disposal work. Asked again: a fixed fee only, or a fixed fee plus a separate basis where a charge is avoided — and should we cost the fixed fee from the job-economics model? A percentage of allowances identified, now 3%, still comes to nothing on a disposal.

 **16 September 2026 (Phase 132e).** Still not answered. The flat 3% fee does not help here: a percentage of allowances identified is still nothing on a disposal, and the retired £5,000 and £7,500 minimum fees, which some papers had assumed might cover a small job, are gone. So a disposal fee now has to be set on its own.

## Who handles an ordinary complaint about our service, and who handles one about you?

 **Added 14 September 2026 by Phase 131. For Lakhbir.** Nothing is blocked today. But the complaints process now marks this open, and until it is answered "the handler" in its steps names nobody.

 **The question.** The complaints process was drafted to give first contact to a Client & Referral Coordinator. That role comes from the HR framework, which is parked until a first hire, so nobody holds it. The draft Complaints Policy says you investigate a complaint in the first instance, and that a complaint about you personally goes to the firm's solicitor for an independent view — and no solicitor has been instructed. A complaint about how someone's personal data was handled is not part of this question: it goes to the data protection officer, with you countersigning. **Who handles a service complaint, and who reviews one about you?**

 **What we think.** Confidence: **high that you handle service com****p****laints until the first hire, low on the complaint about ****y****ou**. Until someone is hired, every step falls to you: log the complaint the same day; decide on the day whether it is a service complaint or a data protection one; acknowledge within **2 business days** (the policy allows 3 working days; the process aims faster); and answer within **14 c****a****lendar day****s**** of receipt**. The policy's answer for a complaint about you rests on a solicitor who is not instructed, so today a complaint about your own conduct would be decided by you. The breach deputy has the same shape, and there it is recorded as a known limitation rather than presented as a check. The policy also says plainly that no ombudsman or regulator covers our own conduct, so a client must be told what independent routes actually exist; the only outside professional route is the surveyor's or the tax adviser's own body, where their judgement is what is complained about.

 **An example.** A care home is invoiced **£40,500** — 5% of **£810,000** of allowances identified. It complains that the fee is more than its first-year tax saving of about **£36,450** (£810,000 at the 18% main rate, for a period before April 2026, at 25%), and that the fee was badly explained. You acknowledge within two business days and answer within fourteen days of receipt. The fee half is answered from clause 4 of the signed letter, which discloses in terms that the fee may exceed a year's saving. **The "****b****adly explained" half is a complaint about how you explained it, and today the p****er****son**** ****deciding it is you****.**

 **Our recommendation.** **Y****o****u handle service com****p****laints for now**: acknowledge within 2 business days, answer within 14 calendar days of receipt. **Name one independent ****r****eviewer in advance f****o****r complaints about you****r**** own conduct** — the solicitor once instructed, or an external adviser chosen per case. **Until one is named, tell the ****c****lient in writing that there is no independent reviewer yet**, and point them to mediation and the courts. Data protection complaints stay with the officer. **Revisit at th****e**** fi****r****st**** ****hire**, when the coordinator role can take intake.

 **What y****o****ur answer unblocks.** The handler in the complaints process's steps, so the process can come to you for approval once the data protection officer has checked the hand-off; the stop-gap and the open question in the Complaints Policy's "who investigates" section; and the name the acknowledgement letter gives.
