## Do we switch the engagement gate on — and how many jobs are we actually going to do this year?

 **Added 9 September 2026, the day the gate was built. It is one question with two halves, and the first half is worth nothing without the second.**

 **What we think.** The question above — _is the small end of the market worth serving at all?_ — ends by saying the engagement gate is the only thing that protects the loss-making band. That gate now exists. It sits in the assessment, and where the fee we would quote does not cover what the job costs us plus a 25% margin, it stops the automatic quote and says _"we will come back to you with a price"_ instead. **It is switched off. Turning it on is a commercial decision and it is yours, not ours.**

 **The worked example, on our own cost estimates.**

|  A dental job with £100,000 of allowances identified |   |
| --- | --- |
|  Our fee at 5% |  **£5,000** |
|  What that job costs us, at 25 jobs in the year |  £5,390 |
|  Plus the 25% margin the papers recommend |  **£6,737** |
|  **So we would quote £5,000 for work costing us £5,390** |  **short by £1,737 against the target, and losing £390 in cash** |
|  The same job, if we were doing 100 jobs a year |  costs £3,590 — **comfortably profitable** |

 **That last row is the whole of the second half of this question.** The same job is a loss at a realistic year-one volume and a good job at a mature one. **So the gate cannot work without knowing how many jobs we are actually going to do**, and there is no safe number to assume: assuming a hundred would wave through every job that loses money for the whole of the first year, which is the exact failure the gate exists to prevent. **We have therefore left it blank rather than guessing, and while it is blank the gate refuses nothing even if you switch it on.**

 **What we recommend.**

-  **Switch it on** — but only once you have given us the second half. The business plan's own ramp reaches sixteen jobs in year one and the papers work to twenty-five. **Either is fine as an answer; "about twenty" is fine as an answer. No answer is the only one that does not work.**

-  **And read one caveat before you do.** The cost side is still built on a £60,000 annual overhead that was a placeholder somebody wrote down, and on estimated rather than quoted surveyor fees. **The gate will draw a line, and the line will be as good as those two numbers are.** It says so on every answer it gives rather than presenting them as measured, but you should know it before it starts refusing work.

 **What it unblocks.** The last floor on the rate card that the engine could apply by itself. Today the rule _"never issue a quote that fails the internal gate"_ is enforced by whoever sends the quote and by nothing else.

 **What happens if you say no.** Nothing breaks and nothing needs undoing. The gate stays built and inert, exactly as the fee cap and the fee warning do, and the answer can change later without anybody rebuilding anything.

```
ANSWER
Switching this on stops the assessment quoting jobs we lose money on. It needs BOTH answers to do anything.

(a) Switch the engagement gate on?  Yes      YES  /  NO

(b) How many jobs do you expect us to do in the current year?   ......120.............


How confident are you?    High / Medium / Low

Answered by:                                     Date:

If you disagree with our recommendation, what should we do instead?


...................................................................... 
```

 **Read 15 September 2026 (Phase 132c).** A decision: switch the engagement gate on, with 120 jobs expected this year. That is well above the 16 to 25 the business plan and papers assume, and the gate lets through more jobs at 120 than at 25. A build phase sets 120, re-works the gate on the flat 3% fee, shows under this question which quotes it would stop at 120 and at 25, and then switches it on. It moves no client tax figure, and every answer it gives still says its overhead and surveyor costs are estimates.

 **Built 16 September 2026 (Phase 132e) — and first, which quotes it would stop.** This was worked out on the 3% fee, before the switch was set, by running the job-economics model. The gate stops an automatic quote when our fee is below the job's loaded cost plus 25%; the client is told we will come back with a price. It looks at the fee, the sector and the year's job volume, never the purchase price. The costs are estimates: the overhead is a £60,000 placeholder, and the surveyor and tax adviser fees are estimates, not quotes, and every answer the gate gives says so. **120 is about five to seven times the business plan** (the plan's ramp reaches 16 jobs in year one; the papers use 25), and more jobs a year lowers the line, so the gate lets more small jobs through at 120 than at 25. If 120 is not reached, it passes jobs that lose money at the volume actually done.

|  On the surveyor-visit route |  Stopped below, at 120 jobs a year |  Stopped below, at 25 jobs a year |
| --- | --- | --- |
|  Dental |  £145,417 of allowances (fee £4,362.50) |  £224,583 (fee £6,737.50) |
|  GP surgery |  £197,083 (fee £5,912.50) |  £276,250 (fee £8,287.50) |
|  Care home |  £256,250 (fee £7,687.50) |  £335,417 (fee £10,062.50) |
|  Private hospital |  £275,417 (fee £8,262.50) |  £354,583 (fee £10,637.50) |
|  Supported living |  never: no cost row, so the gate cannot decide |  never |

 Examples at the yield midpoints: a £500,000 dental purchase is stopped at both volumes; a £750,000 dental purchase and a £1,100,000 GP purchase are quoted at 120 but stopped at 25; a £1,000,000 care home is quoted at 120 and stopped at 25; the plan's typical care home (£1,800,000) and private hospital (£2,500,000) are quoted at both.

 **Then switched on:** the engine's gate is on, with 120 jobs this year, and its setting records your decision and the estimate caveat. It is a funnel switch, not a tax switch: all 24 tax switches are still off. **One thing it does not do yet, and it matters.** The gate needs the job-economics costs handed to it by whatever runs it. The public site cannot load that model, and the back end does not hand it over yet, so today it comes back undetermined on every quote and stops nothing. Connecting it in the back end is written into the next build phase. The test harness does connect it, and proves it stops a fee below the line and quotes one above it, and that a volume or fee of "not sure" and one never given behave identically.

## The multi-site discount was capped at 15% because there was no room. There is now. What should it be?

 **Added 9 September 2026, when the rate card was rebuilt on the decided fee base. This is a case of an argument losing its support, and it is better that you hear it from us than from a client.**

 **What we think.** The rate card offers 5% off for two or three sites, 10% for four to nine and 15% for ten or more, and its stated reason is that most of our cost is per-site — six care homes means six surveys — so a bigger discount would price the work below what it costs. **That reason was true on the old fee base and it is not true now.**

 **The example, which is the card's own.** Six care homes at £1.8m each, held in a company.

|   |  On the superseded base |  **On the decided base** |
| --- | --- | --- |
|  Fee per home after the 10% discount |  £6,750 |  **£24,300** |
|  Our loaded cost per home, at a hundred jobs a year |  £6,250 |  £6,250 |
|  Margin per home |  £500 — and a loss of £1,300 in year one |  **£18,050** |
|  What a 40% discount would leave |  Well below cost |  **£16,200 a home** |

 **So a client who opens at 40% — and the card predicts they will — can now be given it and we would still make five and a half times our cost on every home.** The card can no longer say the cost base is what stops us.

 **Our recommendation: hold the discount at 5–15%, and change the argument rather than the number.** Two reasons, and neither is about cost. **A discount sets the reference price for every deal after it** — the second multi-site client will hear about the first. And **the per-site cost point is still the honest description of what a portfolio actually saves us**, which is what makes 5–15% defensible in the room even though it is no longer forced.

 **We have rewritten the card's own question to Saddique to match** — it now asks whether that conversation can be won on those grounds, rather than asking whether 5–15% is saleable. **But the discount table is yours, not his**, and if you would rather widen it deliberately now than have it negotiated away deal by deal, that is a legitimate answer and the card will follow it.

 **What it unblocks.** The rate card's discount table, and the point at which a deal escalates to you.

```
ANSWER
Looking to offer a flat fee of 3% for 1 or more sites - avg market rate is 5% - 7%......................................................................
Your answer:    Hold at 5–15%  /  Widen it to ____%  /  something else


How confident are you?    High / Medium / Low

Answered by:                                     Date:

If you disagree with our recommendation, what should we do instead?


...................................................................... 
```

 **Read 15 September 2026 (Phase 132c).** "Looking to offer a flat fee of 3% for 1 or more sites" was read by Phase 129 as a flat 3% fee and asked once, under "Is our fee now a flat 3%…". That box now says yes, and adds that "a further multi-site discount can be applied depending on site/opportunities". So the 5–15% table goes; what replaces it is asked there — the largest discount, and who approves it. The rebuild goes to a build phase.

 **Answered and closed 16 September 2026 (Phase 132e).** There is no longer a capped table to widen. The fee is a flat 3% with no bands, and the 5%, 10% and 15% multi-site discounts are withdrawn from the rate card, the engine and the back end. What remains open is narrower and is asked afresh: how large a discretionary multi-site discount may be, and who approves it — under "Now the fee is a flat 3%, how large may a multi-site discount be, who approves it, and do the old uplifts survive?" in this section. Until that is answered no discount is quoted.

## The rate card's fee bands were written against tax saved. Where should the breakpoints sit now?

 **Added 9 September 2026, when the rate card was rebuilt on the decided fee base. It is the one number in that card nobody can derive — it has to be chosen.**

 **What we think.** The rate card charges a marginal percentage, like income tax: 5% on the first slice, 4% on the next, 3% above that, so bigger jobs pay a lower average rate and there are no cliff edges. The breakpoints were written as **£250,000 and £1,000,000 of RELIEF**. We now charge on **allowances**, which are two and a half to four times larger for the same job. So the published breakpoints, left where they are, bite on jobs far smaller than the card was designed for.

 **The example, on the card's own largest worked job — a £10m private hospital, £3.5m of allowances.**

|  Reading |  Breakpoints |  Fee |  Effective rate |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
|  **A — rescaled, and what the card is priced on today** |  £625,000 / £2,500,000 of allowances |  **£139,375** |  3.98% |
|  B — the published numbers left where they are |  £250,000 / £1,000,000 of allowances |  £118,750 |  3.40% |
|  _No banding at all, for reference_ |  _flat 5%_ |  _£175,000_ |  _5.00%_ |

 **The two readings are identical on every job below £625,000 of allowances**, which is a purchase price of about £2.8m for dental and £1.8m for a private hospital — so on ordinary work the choice is invisible. **It only shows on the deals that come to you anyway**, and there it is worth £20,625 on the single job above.

 **Our recommendation: Reading A.** Dividing the old breakpoints by 40% keeps the same physical jobs in the same bands as the card intended, so it changes the base without changing the policy. **Reading B is a real price cut on large work that nobody decided to make** — it would arrive as a side effect of a definition, which is exactly how the mistake this rebuild corrected happened in the first place.

 **What it unblocks.** The rate card can go to Saddique either way — it is priced on Reading A and says so — but it cannot come into force until you have chosen, because the bands are the card.

```
ANSWER
.......no banding - flat fee of 3%...............................................................
Your answer:    Reading A  /  Reading B  /  something else


How confident are you?    High / Medium / Low

Answered by:                                     Date:

If you disagree with our recommendation, what should we do instead?


...................................................................... 
```

 **Read 15 September 2026 (Phase 132c).** "no banding - flat fee of 3%" was read by Phase 129 as a flat 3% fee and asked once, under "Is our fee now a flat 3%…". That box now says yes, so this question closes as answered: neither Reading A nor Reading B, because there are no bands. The rate card is rebuilt without them in the build phase that moves the fee to 3%.

 **Answered and closed 16 September 2026 (Phase 132e).** Neither Reading A nor Reading B: there are no bands, so there are no breakpoints. The rate card is rebuilt on a flat 3% of the allowances identified, with no bands and no minimum, and its six-home example reads £97,200 on £3.24 million of allowances (£16,200 a home). The complexity uplifts and the £1,500 manual-handling supplement were designed for the banded card and are suspended, not applied; whether a flat fee carries them is asked under "Now the fee is a flat 3%, how large may a multi-site discount be, who approves it, and do the old uplifts survive?".

## Has anybody actually checked that we are allowed to be called Caprebate?

 **Research note, 12 September 2026, Phase 129.** The results of the two searches this question asked for, with what was tried.

 **Companies House**, searched 12 September 2026 for "Caprebate", "Caprebate Limited", "Cap-Rebate" and "Caprebates", and on the register's general search. Every search except "Caprebates" returned the same company: **CAP REBATE LTD, company number 17307313. Active. Incorporated 29 June 2026. Registered office in Hinckley. Nature of business: 69203 Tax consultancy.** This is the company your answer box names. "Caprebates" returned no company. **Why it matters:** Companies House's own guidance ("Incorporation and names", GOV.UK, section 6.4) ignores spaces between characters and a closing "Ltd" or "Limited" when it decides whether two names are the "same as" each other. **So "Caprebate Limited" would read as the same name as an existing company in our own trade.** Our reading, which a solicitor or company formation agent should confirm before any application: Companies House is likely to refuse the name.

 **UK trade marks.** This could not be run headlessly: the UK IPO's trade mark search pages each returned a captcha page (HTTP 403), which was not bypassed; the WIPO Global Brand Database returned a bot check; TMview did not respond within 60 seconds. Two web searches found no mark called Caprebate — that is not a register search. **Your answer box says a search found nothing: please add the date and the classes searched.**

 **Domains**, checked by DNS lookup the same day. caprebate.com and caprebate.co.uk both point to the same IONOS address — probably both ours, not confirmed from outside. caprebate.uk, .org, .org.uk, .net and .io have no DNS records.

 **What this changes.** The recommendation stands: record the date of each search. But **there is now a live conflict at Companies House, in our own trade**, so the name decision needs to come back to you before incorporation. A clearance opinion, and any conversation with the other company, is for the solicitor.

 **Added later on 12 September 2026.** The UK trade mark register was searched a second way the same day: TMview, querying the UK register, returned UK marks for the control words "tesco" and "rebate" and none for "caprebate" or "cap rebate". A text search like this is not a clearance.

 **Note added 14 September 2026 by Phase 131.** The brief to the solicitor is now ready to send, and it deliberately does not ask for a name-clearance opinion. That is a separate piece of work with its own fee, and adding it would change what the solicitor is being paid for. If you want the solicitor to clear the name against CAP REBATE LTD and the trade mark register, say so in the answer box below, and say whether it should be added to the same brief or instructed separately.

 **Added 9 September 2026.** Found while reconciling a document from June that nobody had read in ten weeks. **Nothing on the board is blocked on this, and that is the problem — it has been quietly outstanding since before the name was even chosen.**

 **What we think. Confidence: high that it has not been done, and we cannot tell you what the answer would be.**

 **Deciding a name and clearing a name are two different jobs, and only the first one has happened.** The name was settled on 4 September 2026, caprebate.com is registered, live, and configured for mail, and every document in the business now says Caprebate. **What we have found no record of anywhere is a Companies House search or a UK trade-mark search against it.**

 **How we know it was meant to happen.** Our own brand note sets out a six-step sequence. Step 1 is _settle the name_ — done. **Step 2 is “check what sits at the matching domains, and run a UK trade mark search on the chosen name”** — and it calls that _“a twenty-minute job and the cheapest insurance available at this stage”_. Steps 3 to 6 (register the domain, set up mail, fill the policy placeholders, brief a designer) have all been done or started. **Step 2 is the only one in the middle of that sequence that was skipped.**

 **And the note that carries it is now about the wrong name.** It was written when the business was going to be called Ledgerstone, so it recommends a search on _“Ledgerstone”_, prices _ledgerstone.uk_, and reports which _ledgerstone_ domains were taken. **It closes by asking whether the trade-mark question should be raised as its own board item, and observes that “it is a real risk that this note surfaces and does not close”. It did not close.** The advice in it is still sound; every proper noun in it is stale.

 **A worked example of what we are exposed to.** Suppose a firm in accountancy, tax or property already trades as Caprebate, or holds a UK trade mark in the relevant classes. **Today that costs a domain, some stationery and an afternoon.** After launch it costs the website, the eight policy documents, the engagement letter, the client-facing report templates, every exported governance document, the email addresses that clients have written down, and any search ranking built in the meantime — and this project has already done one full corpus-wide rename, from Ledgerstone to Caprebate, so we know exactly what that costs. **It also has a shape nobody enjoys: the first you hear of it is usually a letter.**

 **Our recommendation. Run both searches before the site goes live, and record the date you ran them.** A free Companies House name search and a free UK Intellectual Property Office trade-mark search, in the classes that cover tax, accountancy, surveying and business advice. **If both come back clear, we want the date on file** — the value is as much in being able to show it was checked as in the answer. **If either shows a conflict, tell us immediately**, because the cost of changing the name rises every week from here and rises sharply the day the site is published.

 **This is not us asking you to take legal advice.** A clearance opinion is a solicitor's, and if a search turns something up that is exactly where it goes — the solicitor register already carries three matters for whoever we appoint. **What we are asking for is the search itself, which nobody has done.**

 **What it unblocks.** Nothing formally. What it protects is the site going live, the notices page at caprebate.com/notices/, the engagement letter, and every document that now carries the name — which is all of them.

## Incorporate — and the chain that is sitting behind it

 **What we think.** Incorporation is deferred indefinitely, which is a legitimate choice, but a number of things are queued behind it and that queue is not visible from the decision itself.

 **The example.** Incorporating starts a chain: company number, then registration with the Information Commissioner, corporation tax, the VAT position, a bank account, professional indemnity insurance, and the Google business listing. **Two things sit specifically behind it.** Professional indemnity insurance cannot be bought until the company exists, so our breach paperwork currently has to record "no policy in force". And the data protection officer's appointment cannot be signed until the company is live — which holds thirteen finished documents out of force, including the privacy notice.

 **Our recommendation.** If incorporation is genuinely deferred, then **say so on the thirteen governance documents** rather than leaving them reading "DRAFT — NOT APPROVED" indefinitely. Either the deferral is short and they wait, or it is long and they need an interim owner. The gap should be closed by a decision, not by editing headers.

 **One limitation worth writing down rather than glossing over.** You are the deputy who decides a breach if the data protection officer cannot be reached. The design assumes one person proposes and a second approves. With you as deputy, that is the same person doing both. It is defensible for a business this size — but record it as a known limitation rather than presenting it as a two-person check.

 **What it unblocks.** Around twelve tasks, thirteen governance documents, and three live duties.

How confident are you?    High / Medium / Low

...................................................................... 
```

## Buy the two British Standards the quantity model depends on?

 **Note added 14 September 2026 by Phase 131.** The question below predates the work that answers most of it, and your answer box says "Surveyor", which still stands as the first step. On 31 August the two standards were read against the model, and the finding was **buy one, not both**. **BS EN 50173-2**, the structured-cabling standard for office-type space, is the only purchase that closes a gap in the quantity model outright: the count of data and communications outlets, since split into a count per enclosed room and a density for open-plan floor. Both are still blank, both wait on this standard, and until one is filled the rows that depend on them stay unresolved and add nothing to any total. **BS 5839-1**, the fire detection standard, is not worth buying. On a real 268 m² dental fit-out a room-by-room count gives 15 to 19 detection points, against 2.4 or 4.8 from the floor-area readings the standard would settle, so the model now counts detectors room by room. **So: ask the surveyor first, and if they do not hold BS EN 50173-2, buy that one alone.**

 **What we think.** Two standards sit behind coefficients in the model that estimates how much equipment is in a building: one covering structured cabling, one covering fire detection and alarm systems. Without them, two coefficients are estimates rather than derived figures.

 **Our recommendation.** Ask the surveyor first, exactly as with the price books. A practising surveyor will either have them or will tell you the coefficients do not need them. Buying standards you have not been told you need is the most avoidable spend on this list.

 **What it unblocks.** Two coefficients in the quantity model, and nothing else.

## What is the break fee, and is there ever an abort fee or a fixed fee?

 **Updated 12 September 2026 by Phase 129, which worked out the break fee from what a job costs us.** Your answer box already says "£995 per property?" for the break fee and nil for the abort fee. This update tests those answers.

 **What we think.** Three commercial figures in the engagement letter are still blank:

-  **Clause 5, the break fee** (£[X]).

-  **Clause 4, the abort fee** ([no fee is payable / an abort fee of £[amount] is payable]).

-  **Clause 4, the fixed-fee alternative** (or a fixed fee of £[amount]).

 **The earlier version of this question pointed at the wrong model.** The residual cost model costs the _client's building_. What _our job_ costs is in the job-economics model. The figures below come from running the new break-fee derivation, which reads that model and the engine's 5% rate. They are estimates, not quotes.

 What we have spent on one property if the client leaves, on the route where the surveyor attends:

|  Sector |  Research run, surveyor not instructed |  Surveyor instructed, and we owe the surveyor |  Whole job |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
|  Dental |  £50 to £530 |  £2,030 |  £2,630 |
|  GP surgery |  £50 to £770 |  £2,970 |  £3,870 |
|  Care home |  £50 to £890 |  £4,090 |  £5,290 |
|  Private hospital |  £50 to £950 |  £4,450 |  £5,750 |

 **Why it matters.** Clause 13 makes the client agree to the break fee by name. Clause 8 lets it apply inside the 14-day window once work has started. Clause 5 calls it "a genuine pre-estimate of our losses on early termination and is not a penalty" — and that sentence is only true of a figure somebody has estimated. The letter cannot go to a client with these blanks in it.

 **An example.** A dental practice buying for £500,000 signs on Monday. Its allowances are £112,500 and our fee is £5,625.

-  **It leaves on Tuesday, before anything has started:** nothing is owed, and it has cost us nothing.

-  **It leaves after the research has run:** we have spent up to £530, and £995 covers it.

-  **It leaves after we have instructed the surveyor:** we have spent £2,030. A flat £995 leaves us £1,035 down. £995 plus the surveyor's £1,500 recovers £2,495.

-  **With a nil abort fee, a survey that finds nothing costs us £2,030.** That is the profit on one completed job at 100 jobs a year, and on 8.6 completed jobs at 25 jobs a year.

 **Our recommendation.**

-  **(a) Break fee: £995 per property, plus, once a surveyor is instructed, the surveyor's charges we are liable for under that instruction, at cost.** £995 covers the most we can have spent before survey in every sector (the highest is £950). The surveyor pass-through covers the one large cost we cannot recover after survey.

-  **(b) Abort fee: nil,** as you answered — with the cost above in view. It means the research before survey has to screen out claims with nothing in them.

-  **(c) Take the fixed-fee option out of clause 4.** The engine has one rate and no fixed-fee path, so we cannot quote a fixed fee or check it against the client's benefit.

-  **For the solicitor:** whether £995 is still a genuine pre-estimate if a client leaves when we have spent as little as £50. If not, use a smaller fee before survey, equal to each sector's research figure.

 **One thing that may move these figures.** They are costed against a 5% fee. If the fee is now a flat 3% — asked at the end of this section — our costs do not change but the profit that pays for a failed job does, so the abort-fee example gets worse.

 **What it unblocks.** Three of the eleven blanks in the letter, and two of the four questions for the solicitor, which need these numbers first. The full paper is attached to the task: _Caprebate Break Fee and Abort Fee Derivation v1_.

How confident are you?    High / Medium / Low

## If a claim is reduced after we have been paid, do we give the money back?

 **Two of our own documents say we will. The contract the client actually signs says nothing about it.** Found by the sweep on 6 September 2026, on reading the letter clause by clause.

 **What we think.** Our fee is a flat 3% of the capital allowances identified (5% until 16 September 2026), invoiced once the claim is agreed. If HMRC later opens an enquiry and the allowances come down, the client has paid us against a figure that turned out to be too big. The business plan already recommends the remedy, under the risk headed "a claim collapses on enquiry after we have been paid" — a clawback that is "proportionate and computable: fee is a stated share of a stated figure, so a reduction produces a proportionate credit anyone can calculate". The rules-defect and client correction plan states it harder still, as a rule rather than an intention: "the fee follows the corrected figure, always… the difference is credited or refunded. This is not discretionary and is not a goodwill gesture."


 **The engagement letter contains none of it.** Clause 4 sets the fee and never revisits it. Clause 5 runs the other way, charging the client a break fee if they walk away. Clause 10 caps our liability at the fees paid — which reaches the same money only if a client sues us and proves a claim, and no client would experience that as a refund.

 **Corrected 11 September 2026 by CRM 6 of 10 — the paragraph above is no longer true of the letter, and the question still stands.** The engagement letter now carries **clause 13, Fee Adjustment Where a Claim is Reduced**: the fee is recalculated on the corrected figure and the difference is credited, or refunded where it has been paid, with the £73,740-to-£50,000 worked example written into it. **Its three terms are still in square brackets** — whether it applies to any reduction or only a material one, whether it applies to a settlement the client accepted, and its time limit — which are exactly (b), (c) and (d) in the answer box below. So (a) is drafted, waiting on your yes, and the other three are yours. The build reads those blanks from the letter itself: the clawback is switched off, and even switched on it raises nothing while any of the three is blank.

 **An example, on the competitor case we already hold.** Allowances identified £73,740, our fee £3,687. HMRC reduces the allowances to £50,000 on enquiry. Under a proportionate clawback the fee becomes £2,500 and we credit £1,187 — a figure the client can check on the back of an envelope. Under the letter as written, we keep the £3,687 and the client's only route to that £1,187 is to sue us.

 **Why it is not only about fairness.** The correction plan makes the point that a downward correction reduces our own fee, so the firm has a direct financial interest in not finding, not classifying and not disclosing a defect in its own rules. The credit rule is the control that removes that interest. Without it in the contract, the control is a promise we made to ourselves.

 **Our recommendation: put a clawback in, and make it automatic rather than discretionary.** The shape writes itself, because the fee is a percentage of a stated number — recompute 5% against the corrected figure and credit the difference. Three things are yours to decide rather than ours: whether it fires on any reduction or only a material one; whether it survives a settlement the client chose to accept rather than contest; and whether it is time-limited, or open for as long as HMRC can enquire. Our own preference is any reduction, no materiality floor, no time limit short of the enquiry window — because a floor is exactly the discretion the control exists to remove.

 **What it unblocks.** The clause can be drafted with the trigger in square brackets, in the same way clauses 4 and 5 already carry bracketed commercial terms, so this does not stop the letter being finished. What it does stop is the letter being sent: a client who signs today gets no clawback at all. It also settles one of the questions we would put to a solicitor about the letter, and it is the missing half of the fee-versus-benefit disclosure we already print.

```

......................................................................
Your answer:

(b) Trigger — any reduction, or only a material one?  Any / Material over £...... ?

(c) Does it apply where the client accepted a settlement rather than contesting it?   Yes / No ?

(d) Time limit —  none / ...... years from invoice - no time limit as HMRC can open case at any time


How confident are you?    High / Medium / Low

Answered by:                                     Date:

If you disagree with our recommendation, what should we do instead?


...................................................................... 
```

 **Read 15 September 2026 (Phase 132c).** Partly answered. (a) says we refund after we have been paid — yes to a clawback. (d) says no time limit "as HMRC can open case at any time"; HMRC's windows are in fact 4, 6 or 20 years, and clause 13's own option "for so long as the claim remains open to enquiry" gives that result. Both go into the letter in a build phase. (b) and (c) are "?", and while they are blank the clawback raises nothing. Asked again: any reduction or only a material one, and does it apply to a settlement the client accepted?

 **Built 16 September 2026 (Phase 132e).** (a) is recorded: yes, we refund. Clause 13's time-limit bracket is completed as "this clause applies for so long as the claim remains open to enquiry", which is the nearest the letter's own options come to "no time limit", because a closed claim cannot be reduced. Its worked example is now at 3%: allowances falling from £73,740 to £50,000 take the fee from £2,212.20 to £1,500, and £712.20 is credited or refunded. **Still open, and asked again:** (b) the trigger — any reduction, or only a material one — and (c) whether it applies where you accept a settlement with HMRC. While those two brackets are blank the back end's clawback raises nothing, even switched on, and that is tested both ways.

## What does a Homedata address lookup cost us each time it runs?

 **Research note, 12 September 2026, Phase 129.** The prices below are from homedata.co.uk/pricing and homedata.co.uk/docs/pricing, read that day. **Homedata sells tokens, not lookups.** An address search costs 2 tokens and retrieving the full address costs 5, so one lookup costs **7 tokens**; filling in the property record as well costs 1 more, **8 in all**. Plans: Free, 100 tokens a month; Starter, £49 a month for 4,500 tokens; Growth, £149 for 18,000; £349 for 70,000; Scale, £699 for 250,000. **So the free plan covers about fourteen lookups a month, not the hundred recorded when Homedata was chosen on 26 August.** On Starter a lookup costs about 7.6p if every token is used — a rounding error next to the £50 data line. The pricing page also says "Currently England and Wales only". This note is not an answer to which tier the lookup belongs in, or whether it is used at all; those remain your decisions, and your own note says your previous company matched typed addresses against public sources instead.

 **Corrected 17 September 2026 (Phase 133) — the prices have changed again.** Homedata's pricing page, read on 17 September 2026, says "100 tokens = £1": pay as you go, with **no plans, no subscription and no free monthly allowance** — the Free, Starter, Growth and Scale plans in the note above no longer exist. Tokens never expire, and the first top-up is matched (£10 buys 2,000 tokens). A search costs 2 tokens, the full address 5 and a property record 1, so **one lookup is 7p**, and a five-property claim costs **35p** in lookups. Sold-price and energy-certificate data are England and Wales only. Your answer below says your last company never used a paid lookup; the question that follows this one asks whether we need one at all._ (Corrected 24 September 2026, Phase 147. This sentence is about OUR lookup, so it is corrected: the lookup built on 23 September 2026 is 8 tokens, 8p a property (postcode search 2, address 5, registered-title check 1), and a five-property claim costs 40p, not 35p. 7p is the supplier's find-then-retrieve, which we do not call.)_


 **Added 8 September 2026 by Phase 100, while costing the small end. Board item 12955448913.**

 **Why this one was thought different from every other cost in the business.** **Correc****t****ed**** ****17 September 2026 (Phase 133).** Written on 8 September 2026, this paragraph said the address lookup is "the only thing we pay for before a client has signed anything", because it "fires at the front of the funnel, on people who may never come back". That was already overturned by your rule of 4 September: a paid call fires only after a signed engagement, when someone clicks Run the research. So if we use Homedata at all, it is paid for on clients who have signed, and an abandoned funnel costs nothing. On 15 September you also agreed a typed postcode and address in the funnel.


 **It still fires once per property, not once per enquiry** — but after signature. Corrected 17 September 2026 (Phase 133): this said somebody with eleven care homes triggers eleven lookups "before we have any commitment from them at all". Under the 4 September rule the eleven lookups run only once that client has signed; at today's price of 7p a lookup they cost 77p._ (Corrected 24 September 2026, Phase 147. This is OUR lookup: at 8p a property the eleven cost 88p, not 77p.)_


 **What we think.** We have deliberately **not guessed this number.** The cost model carries it as a named, empty parameter, and every figure it produces is flagged as excluding it rather than quietly assuming it is free — because a guessed cost would be indistinguishable from a real one six months from now.

 **A worked example.** Three enquiries fail for every one that converts. So at **50p a lookup**, a single-property enquirer costs us about £2 per job that actually lands — noise against a £1,220 delivery cost. At **£5 a lookup** it is £20 per landed job, still small. But an eleven-property enquirer at £5 is **£55 spent before anybody signs**, and if that pattern is common it stops being a rounding error and starts being a reason to cap how many properties the free tier will look up._ (Withdrawn 24 September 2026, Phase 147. This example charged the lookup to enquiries that never sign, and spoke of money "spent before anybody signs". Under your rule of 4 September a paid lookup runs only after signature, so a failed enquiry costs no lookup at all, and there is nothing to cap on the free tier. The real cost is per property of a signed job: OUR lookup is 8p, so eleven care homes cost 88p. The job cost model no longer carries the four-times figure.)_


 **Our recommendation.** Tell us the per-lookup price and whether it is banded by volume. If Homedata price it as a bundle or a subscription rather than per call, say that instead — it changes the shape of the answer, not just the number.

 **What your answer unblocks.** Three things. It completes the cost of a job. It tells us whether we need a property-count limit on free enquiries. And it may **move the lookup from one tier to the other**: we have settled that free lookups fire once a client gives permission, and paid ones fire only after signature on an operator's click. **If the address lookup is meaningfully paid, it does not belong in the free tier at all** — and that is a change to how the funnel works, not just to a spreadsheet._ (Answered and closed 24 September 2026, Phase 147. The tier part is settled: the address lookup is a paid call. It runs only after signature, when a member of staff clicks, and it is built that way and switched off (Phase 146d). There is no property-count limit to set on free enquiries, because a free enquiry spends nothing on it. Only the confirmed price is still open, in the Word questions document: "How much does a Homedata address lookup cost us each time?")_

## Do we need a paid address lookup at launch, or do we match the address the client types, as your last company did?

 **Added 17 September 2026 by Phase 133. For Lakhbir.** A decision, not a price. It is asked because the Homedata task on the board was still a trial plan for a paid lookup, while your own answer under "What does a Homedata address lookup cost us each time it runs?" says your last company never used one: it took the address from the customer and matched it against Companies House, Google and similar sources.

 **What we think.** Cost does not decide this, and it never really did. Homedata's pricing page, read on 17 September 2026, says "100 tokens = £1": pay as you go, no plans, no subscription and no free monthly allowance; tokens never expire, and the first top-up is matched. A search costs 2 tokens and the full address 5, so one lookup is 7p. **What a paid lookup buys is match certainty, not access.** The free registers (energy certificates, the care regulator, Companies House, price paid) can all be searched on a typed postcode or name; the paid lookup's property identifier, the UPRN, just makes the match exact instead of fuzzy. And under your rule of 4 September a paid call only fires after the engagement letter is signed, when someone clicks Run the research, so it cannot help the funnel in front of the signature at all. On 15 September you agreed a typed postcode and address in the funnel. **Confidence: high on the price and the rule; none on how often a typed address fails to match** — the matching specification says to measure that on 200 real properties, and nobody has._ (Corrected 24 September 2026, Phase 147: "one lookup is 7p" is THE SUPPLIER'S find-then-retrieve. OUR lookup, as built on 23 September 2026, is 8p a property, because it adds a 1p check for a registered title.)_


 **A worked example, on today's prices.** A client signs for a five-property claim. With Homedata, five lookups at 7p cost **35p**, and each property carries an exact identifier into the research. Without it, the cost is **nothing**, and each typed address is matched against the free registers with a confidence score; any that match weakly go to a person to check. If one address in ten needed checking at five minutes each, that is about half a minute of staff time per five-property claim on average. That one-in-ten is an illustration, not a measurement._ (Corrected 24 September 2026, Phase 147. This is OUR lookup: five lookups at 8p cost 40p, not 35p.)_


 **Our recommendation.** Launch without a paid address lookup, as your last company did: match the typed address, and send weak matches to a person. Measure the match rate on the first 200 properties, as the matching specification already says. Buy a paid lookup only if that measurement shows typed addresses failing often enough to cost more than 7p a property in staff time or lost claims. Do not run the Homedata trial plan now._ (Corrected 24 September 2026, Phase 147. This threshold is OUR lookup, so read it as more than 8p a property, not 7p; 7p is the supplier's find-then-retrieve. Overtaken in any case: you answered on 22 September 2026, "yes, use Homedata", recorded in the Word questions document.)_


 **What it unblocks.** The Homedata task on the board, which can close as not needed at launch or move to the measurement; the address lookup's line in the job cost model, which stays empty until this is answered; and which data connectors the developer prices in the research step.

## Who publishes the address lookup's data, under what licence, and how long may we keep it?

 **Research note, 12 September 2026, Phase 129.** We read the supplier's own published pages that day: its Terms of Service at homedata.co.uk/terms (marked "Last updated: November 2024"), and its about, pricing, documentation and coverage pages. The full list is in the research record attached to the task.

 **What the supplier publishes.**

-  **(a) Who publishes the data.** The supplier is Homedata. The underlying publishers are **Ordnance Survey (AddressBase, which is where the property identifier comes from) and Royal Mail (PAF)**. The supplier's pricing documentation says "Address endpoints carry Royal Mail PAF / Ordnance Survey licensing".

-  **(b) The licence.** There is no licence name. The only licence is the Terms of Service: "a limited, non-exclusive licence to use the data in accordance with your subscription plan". **Attribution wording: NOT PUBLISHED.**

-  **(c) How long we may keep it: NOT PUBLISHED.** No page states a cache window.

-  **(d) Client report, accountant's pack and HMRC pack: NOT PUBLISHED.** The terms say only that we may not "Resell or redistribute our data without authorisation".

 **Also found.** Coverage is published two ways. The pricing page says England and Wales only; the postcode page says Scotland is covered.

 **What was built.** The register row is written, with the cache window held at 0 days (a held address counts as stale until it is fetched again) and every outward channel closed. Zero and closed are our refusals, not the supplier's figures. A draft email asking the supplier for the four missing facts is written and has not been sent.

 **Proposed answer, for you to confirm.** (a) Supplier: Homedata; underlying publishers: Ordnance Survey AddressBase and Royal Mail PAF. (b) Licence: Homedata Terms of Service; attribution to be confirmed by the supplier. (c) To be confirmed by the supplier; until then, 0 days. (d) No, no and no, until the supplier authorises them.

 **What is still needed from you.** Send the draft email when you open the account. **Or,** if the address lookup will not be used at launch — your answers under the two Homedata questions point that way — say so, and this question closes with a note that it is not used.

 **Added 9 September 2026 by Phase 106, while building the panel that shows what has fired. Board item 12984650344.**

 **Correction, 10 September 2026, Phase 114 sweep.** The board item named in the line above is the back-end build item that found this gap, and it is Done. The item that tracks this question is **"Address lookup has no source-register entry — no publisher, no licence, no cache window for the one field every other lookup is matched on"**, in 01 FOR LAKHBIR, at Blocked — question.

 **This is not the cost question above it, and answering that one does not answer this one.** That question asks what a lookup costs and which tier it belongs in. This one asks what we are allowed to record when one runs.

 **What we found.** Every fact this system stores carries three things with it — **the publisher, the licence, and the moment the value was read.** That is not a preference; it is the shape the source register defines, it is what an evidence trail is built from, and the new sources panel reports it for every source. There are sixteen sources in that register and **the paid address lookup is not one of them.** The Provenance Map lists it first and the Build Order tiers it, but neither says who publishes the data, under what licence we hold it, or how long a cached value stays usable.

 **A worked example of why it matters.** A claim is defended two years from now. Every figure in it can name its publisher and the day it was read — except the address and the property identifier that every other lookup was matched on, which can name nothing. That is the one field an inspector is most likely to ask about, because it is what ties the claim to the building.

 **Our recommendation. Get three facts from the supplier when you contract them, and send us those rather than a contract.** The publisher of the underlying data (it is very likely Ordnance Survey and Royal Mail beneath a reseller, and if so the attribution wording is prescribed and must appear verbatim); the licence name; and how long we may keep a returned address and identifier before we have to look it up again. **We have deliberately not written a register entry from guesswork** — an invented licence reads exactly like a checked one six months later, and this is the field a claim is defended on.

 **What your answer unblocks.** The address lookup gets a row on the sources panel instead of a stated gap, a fact it supplies can carry provenance like every other fact, and the retention rule for it stops being unwritten.

## Who refreshes the postcode table every quarter, and what happens on the day nobody does?

 **Added 9 September 2026.** For Lakhbir. **Nothing is blocked on this** — the refresh is written up, the software already handles being out of date, and this is about naming a person.

 **The question.** We hold a copy of the national postcode-to-council table. The Office for National Statistics republishes it **four times a year** and our copy does not update itself — by design, because nothing in the engine reaches out to the internet. Somebody has to download a free file, run one command, check two lines of its output and swap a file on the server. **It takes under an hour, costs nothing, and needs no licence, no account and no purchase order. Whose job is it?**

 **Why we are asking rather than deciding.** This is the first genuinely recurring operational duty in the whole build. Everything else is either a one-off or driven by a client. Getting the owner right matters more than the task does.

 **What we think.** **Whoever holds the back-office Admin role.** Today that is you, because there is no operations team and no company yet. When there is one it should move with the role and not with the person. And it should sit **on the operations dashboard as a recurring task**, not in somebody's calendar — a calendar reminder leaves with the person who set it.

 **What happens if it slips, which we have built rather than hoped.** A few weeks late: nothing at all, there is a month of grace built in. A quarter or two late: answers still come but each one is flagged as coming from an old table. **Over a year late: it refuses**, because by then it has been through an April and councils merge in April — an out-of-date table would name a council that no longer exists, silently, in a surveyor's report. That is the failure worth refusing over.

 **The one thing that must not be skipped, whoever does it.** The Office for National Statistics **renames a column in the file every year**. The command does not fail when that happens — it copes and then prints what it actually used. **Those two printed lines have to be read by a person and written in the log**, and that is the whole reason this is a procedure rather than a script.

 **What your answer unblocks.** A named owner and a recurring task. Nothing technical.

Does it go on the operations dashboard as a recurring task?    Yes / No

...................................................................... 
```

## Do we need a login for anyone beyond the five roles — a manager, a technical reviewer, or a read-only viewer?

 **Added 10 September 2026 by CRM 1 of 10.** For Lakhbir. **Nothing is blocked on this** — the build works with five roles, and this is about whether it ever needs more.

 **The question.** The two CRM requirements documents ask for seven kinds of login: Administrator, Management, Sales, Surveyor, Technical or Tax Team, Finance, and Read-only. The application has five — Admin, Sales, Accounting, Surveying and Client — and adding a sixth is your decision, not a build decision. **Should there be a login for a manager, for a technical reviewer, or for somebody who may only look?**

 **Why it matters more than it sounds.** A role is not a label on a person. Every one of the application's 44 rules says which roles may do one thing — read a claim's value, read a contact, change a property — and a new role needs a yes or a no on every one of them. A wrong yes on a single rule shows somebody a client's figures, or a surveyor our estimate, and nothing else on the screen would look wrong. (44 is the count of registered actions in the application's rule table on 10 September 2026, counted by running it; count again rather than quoting this.)


 **What we think.** **None is needed today.** A manager is you, and you already hold Admin. The technical and tax reviewer is the chartered tax adviser, an outside professional: their sign-off is built as a record of who signed what and when, not as a login, and that keeps them out of every other client's file. Read-only is the one that could earn its place later — an investor, an auditor, or a second person checking the pipeline — and it is the most dangerous of the three, because "only reading" still means reading client data.

 **An example.** Suppose a part-time operations manager joins and should see every claim's stage, owner and next action, but not the fee ledger and not the audit log. Of today's 44 rules they need perhaps 20 — and somebody has to decide, for instance, whether they may read a client's contacts and whether they may see the claim value. Each of those is a yes or no we would build and test; neither is something the build should guess.


 **Our recommendation.** Keep the five. Revisit when the first person who is not a founder joins, or when an outside reviewer needs to see a claim. At that point we would build the new role with a refusal test on every rule it is not given, exactly as each of the five has today.

 **What your answer unblocks.** No work waits on it. **A "no" closes four open lines of the CRM scope** (the technical owner on a claim, and the Management, Technical and Read-only roles). A "yes" to any of the three becomes one build phase that adds that role.

Do we need a technical or tax reviewer login?                Yes / No / Later

Do we need a read-only login, and for whom?                  Yes / No / Later


How confident are you?                                       High / Medium / Low

If you disagree with our recommendation, say so here:


Answered by:                                     Date:

......................................................................
```

It has an explicit yes or no on every one of the 114 rules — yes on 32, no on 82 — and a test fails if a new rule is ever added without a decision for it, and every no is tested as refused. ## Should Accounting see the Clients list?

 **Added 21 September 2026 by Phase 135. For Lakhbir.** Nothing is blocked. Accounting's menu works today with Claims in the place the brief gave Clients.

 **The question.** Your target menu for Accounting was Home, Clients, Finance, Tasks. But Accounting is not allowed to read a client's record, the Clients list included. That was settled on 6 September 2026: Accounting may know a client's name for an invoice, and nothing more. Phase 135 was told not to change who may see what, so Accounting's menu has **Claims** where you wrote **Clients**. Should Accounting be allowed to open the Clients list?

 **What we think.** Confidence: medium. Accounting's daily work is invoices, fees and VAT. It reaches every company it bills through the claim and the invoice, and it already sees the company's name there. The Clients list would add the company's status, its owner and how many claims it has. None of that is money, and none of it is the research or the notes. But opening it means changing the access rules, and the rules were set on purpose.

 **An example.** Accounting raises our fee on Dunmarrow Medical Practice Limited's claim. Today it opens **Claims**, finds "Dunmarrow Medical Practice Limited — Invoice" and raises the fee. With the Clients list it could also see that Dunmarrow is Active, owned by Sales User, with 2 claims. It still could not open Dunmarrow's client page, the research or the notes unless that were changed as well.

 **Our recommendation.** Keep it as it is: Claims, not Clients, for Accounting. If Accounting needs a company-level view later, give it a list of the companies it has invoiced, built from the invoices, rather than the full Clients list.

 **What your answer unblocks.** One line in the menu table for Accounting and, if you say yes, a change to the access rules with its own test. Nothing else waits on it.

## When is our fee earned — when the client accepts the report, when their accountant files the amended return, or when HMRC processes the relief?

 **Added 11 September 2026 by CRM 2 of 10.** For Lakhbir. **Nothing is blocked on this** — the build ships at the most cautious answer, and your answer is a one-line change to it.

 **The question.** A claim now moves through the operating procedure's ten stages, and it cannot move past the invoice stage until our fee has been earned. The procedure leaves open what earns it — it is the procedure's own first open question — and no engagement letter drafted so far settles it either. **Which of these three moments earns our fee?**

 **What we think.** Confidence: **medium**. There are three honest readings. **(a) The client accepts the report.** The earliest cash, but we would invoice before the relief has been claimed at all, and before the client's accountant has checked that it fits the return. **(b) The client's accountant files the amended return.** The claim is then in HMRC's hands and our work is over; the risk left is an enquiry, which a separate question already covers. **(c) HMRC processes the relief.** The latest cash, and we never invoice for relief the client has not received — but we would depend on the client or their accountant telling us that it happened, and on HMRC's timetable.

 **An example.** A dental practice's claim identifies £300,000 of allowances, so our fee is £15,000. The client accepts the report on 1 March, their accountant files the amended return on 20 March, and HMRC processes the relief on 30 June. Under (a) we invoice £15,000 on 1 March; under (b) on 20 March; under (c) on 30 June, three months later, with nothing left for us to do in between. Now suppose HMRC opens an enquiry in May and reduces the allowances to £240,000. Under (a) and (b) we have already invoiced £15,000, and whether £3,000 goes back to the client is the question "If a claim is reduced after we have been paid, do we give the money back?". Under (c) we invoice £12,000 once, and nothing needs crediting.

 **Our recommendation.** **(b), the amended return filed** — written into the engagement letter in those words. It is the point at which our work is finished and the claim is in the right hands, and the question about reduced claims already deals with what can happen afterwards. **The build ships at (c) until you answer**, because it is the most cautious of the three: nothing is invoiced early, and moving to (b) is one line.

 **What your answer unblocks.** No work waits on it. It settles the gate on the invoice stage of the claim pipeline, the fee trigger clause in the engagement letter, and when the finance view should expect a fee — and it closes the operating procedure's first open question.

```

..Fee is due when we either complete the report and submit on clients behalf, or when we send to their client to submit
which we will have no confirmation of that so that is deemed as successfull and then invoice is sent out to be paid. 

Engagement letter should state this

....................................................................
Your answer:

When is our fee earned?
   The client accepts the report / The amended return is filed / HMRC processes the relief

Should the engagement letter say so in those words?          Yes / No


How confident are you?                                       High / Medium / Low

If you disagree with our recommendation, say so here:


Answered by:                                     Date:

......................................................................
```

 **Read 15 September 2026 (Phase 132c).** The box says the fee is due when we complete the report and either submit it for the client or send it to them to submit, and that the engagement letter should say so. That is earlier than our recommendation (the amended return filed) and than the build today (HMRC processing the relief). A build phase moves the invoice stage, the finance view and the letter's fee clause. One point goes to the solicitor instead: clause 1 says we do not sign the filing position, so "submit on clients behalf" needs checking.

 **Built 16 September 2026 (Phase 132e).** The engagement letter now says: "Our fee is earned when we deliver our report for filing, whether to the Client or to the Client's accountant or tax adviser, and it is invoiced on that delivery. It does not depend on the amended return being filed or on HM Revenue and Customs processing the relief." The claim pipeline's invoice stage now opens on the report being delivered for filing, not on HMRC processing the relief. The finance view expects the fee on the delivery date, and where the delivery is not recorded it shows no expected date, never today's. The operating procedure's first open question is closed. **One conflict is not ours to settle:** your answer speaks of us submitting on the client's behalf, while clause 1 says we do not sign the filing position. The letter says only "deliver", and the point is on the solicitor's register.

## Is anything paid to the accountant?

 **Added 11 September 2026 by CRM 5 of 10.** For Lakhbir. **Nothing is blocked on this** — both answers are built and switched off, and your answer turns one of them on.

 **The question.** An accountant who refers a client to us gets something back, or nothing. The referral network paper sets out two ways, and both are now built in the back end and switched off. **Do we give the introducing firm anything — and if so, what?**

 **What we think.** Confidence: medium. **A, no cash.** Firm-level reciprocity: a free CPD session for the practice on capital allowances, a co-branded one-pager for its client newsletter, and priority turnaround on the cases it refers. Nothing is paid, so the accountant has nothing to disclose to their client and their compliance officer has nothing to object to. **B, a fixed fee per successful claim, paid to the firm** and never to a person, and disclosed to the client: members of the accountancy bodies must disclose a referral fee where it could be seen to affect their objectivity, so the system refuses to treat any fee as payable until the disclosure is recorded as given. B may win firms that expect a commercial arrangement, and it adds a referral agreement to sign first. The paper recommends A.

 **One thing that is not what it looks like.** The build refuses to switch either model on while clause 1 of the engagement letter is blank. Clause 1's blank is **the fee for our own tax adviser**, introduced to sign the filing position for a client who has no accountant — **it is not a referral fee**, and the letter says nothing about paying an introducer at all. So completing clause 1 lets the switch move, but does not answer this question: under B the disclosure belongs in a short referral agreement with the firm, and your answer should say whether the engagement letter should mention it as well.

 **An example.** Ledger & Co refers four dental practices in a year. Two claims succeed, identifying £300,000 and £180,000 of allowances, so our fees are £15,000 and £9,000. One ends in a reasoned negative and one does not proceed. Under A, the firm gets a CPD session in the spring and priority turnaround on all four; nothing is paid. Under B at a £500 fixed fee, the firm is owed £1,000 for the two successful claims — each payable only once that client has been told — and nothing for the other two. £1,000 is about 4% of the £24,000 in fees, and it is the same £500 whether a claim is £180,000 or £3,000,000 of allowances.

 **Our recommendation.** **A** — launch with no cash, as the paper recommends. Revisit B only when a particular firm asks for a commercial arrangement, and then as a fixed fee paid to the firm, with the disclosure written into the referral agreement.

 **What your answer unblocks.** No work waits on it. It decides which model is switched on once clause 1 of the engagement letter is complete; what the accountant handoff pack says, which today is that the referral-fee model is not to be discussed with a practice until it is decided; and whether a referral agreement needs drafting.

## How long does a client have to pay our invoice?

 **Added 11 September 2026 by CRM 7 of 10.** For Lakhbir. **Nothing is blocked** — the build ships with no due date on any invoice, and your answer is one edit to the engagement letter.

 **The question.** Clause 4 of the engagement letter says our fee is payable "[payment terms — e.g. on completion / within 14 days of invoice]". That blank is the only place payment terms are written anywhere, and no question has ever asked it. **How long does a client have to pay our invoice?**

 **What we think.** Confidence: **medium**. Under the fee trigger the build uses until you answer the question about when our fee is earned, we invoice only after HMRC has processed the relief, so the client has had the benefit before our invoice arrives. A short term is fair on those facts. Fourteen days is the letter's own example; thirty is common for professional fees.

 **An example.** A dental practice is invoiced £15,000 on 1 July. On fourteen days it is due on 15 July and shows as overdue on 16 July; on thirty days it is due on 31 July. **Today that invoice shows no due date, is never overdue, and nothing flags it** — the honest position while the letter is blank, and a gap the day it is sent.

 **Our recommendation.** **Within 30 days of the date of the invoice**, written into clause 4 in those words. The finance view reads that sentence from the letter and sets each invoice's due date itself — no code changes.

 **What your answer unblocks.** The due date on every invoice we raise, and the overdue flag and total on the finance view. It also fills one of the blanks that stops the letter being issued.

How confident are you?                   High / Medium / Low

If you disagree with our recommendation, say so here:


Answered by:                                     Date:
......................................................................
```

 **Read 15 September 2026 (Phase 132c).** The box says the client must pay within 14 days once we submit to HMRC or send the report on. That is a decision: 14 days, not the 30 we recommended. It goes into clause 4 of the engagement letter as "within 14 days of the date of the invoice", in the build phase that also sets the fee and when it is earned, and the finance view then sets each invoice's due date from that sentence.

 **Built 16 September 2026 (Phase 132e).** Clause 4 of the engagement letter now reads "payable within 14 days of the date of the invoice", and the bracket is gone. The back end reads it: an invoice is due on its date plus 14 days, and if the term were blank it would show no due date rather than assume one — both are tested. The operating procedures say the same.

## Do we need a compliance role separate from Admin?

 **Built and switched off, 12 September 2026 (Phase 129).** A data protection officer role is defined in the back office, scoped to the compliance registers and the record of disclosures and nothing else, and it cannot be assigned to anyone while it is off. Your answer decides whether and when it is switched on, who holds it, whether it may read the audit log, and whether the one-person notice comes off.

 **Added 12 September 2026 by CRM 8 of 10. For Lakhbir. Nothing is blocked** — the compliance registers are built and every one of them is held by Admin today.

 **The question.** The back office now keeps the rights-request register, the complaints log, the breach register, the identity store for people named in documents clients upload, customer due diligence and the retention list. Only the Admin role can open any of it. Admin is a super user — it also creates staff, changes roles and reads the audit log — and it is not a data protection officer. Should compliance be a role of its own?

 **What we think. Confidence: medium.** Not yet. With one Admin login and no data protection officer on the system, a sixth role would be a second login for the same person. It becomes worth building the day someone other than you — the data protection officer, or a compliance assistant — needs to keep these registers without being able to create staff or change anybody's role.

 **One thing that is not what it looks like.** The procedures say the data protection officer decides and you countersign. **In the system that is one person**: one Admin login types both names. Every register page says so, in those words, so nobody reads a typed countersignature as a second pair of eyes.

 **An example.** A stranger writes asking what we hold about them. Today you log it, classify it, decide it and record the data protection officer's name as the decider — all as Admin. With a compliance role, the data protection officer would log in, decide and record it themselves, and could not also add a sales person or read the whole audit log.

 **Our recommendation.** Keep it with Admin until the data protection officer is appointed and has a login; then build a compliance role that holds the registers and nothing else, and let the countersignature be yours as Admin.

 **What your answer unblocks.** Whether the next build adds a sixth role — which is your decision, not the build's — and whether the known limitation on every register page can come off.

How confident are you?                   High / Medium / Low

If you disagree with our recommendation, say so here:

......................................................................
```

 **Read 15 September 2026 (Phase 132c).** The box says "For now, no - just me": no separate compliance role yet, which is our recommendation. Nothing is built. Compliance stays with Admin, the known limitation stays on every register page, and this is revisited when the data protection officer is appointed and has a login. The Developer Spec now records it as decided.

## If money laundering supervision applies to us, what risk-rating scale do we use for a client, and which kinds of claimant must a check cover?

 **Added 12 September 2026 by CRM 8 of 10. For Lakhbir, to put to the solicitor. Nothing is blocked** — customer due diligence is built and switched off until "Is AML supervision legally required for this business?" is answered.

 **The question.** The AML/KYC position paper says what to identify and verify for five kinds of claimant: a limited company, a partnership of GPs, a sole-trader dentist, a charity, and a property owner who is an individual. It names no risk-rating scale, no ownership percentage at which someone counts as a beneficial owner, and nothing for an LLP, a trust or an ordinary partnership. If we are in scope, what scale do we rate a client on, what threshold do we use, and what do we do for the other kinds?

 **What we think. Confidence: low.** A three-level scale — low, standard, high — with the contingent fee named as a factor, and the usual 25% threshold for beneficial ownership, are the common shapes. But the paper says neither, and a scale we invent is a rule nobody wrote.

 **An example.** A care home group trading through an LLP asks us to start. The system lets us record it only as "another kind of claimant", with a note, and a risk rating only as words — because nothing tells it what "high" means.

 **Our recommendation.** Ask the solicitor for the scale, the threshold and the extra claimant types in the same letter as the question about whether supervision applies at all. Nothing is built on a guess in the meantime.

 **What your answer unblocks.** A risk rating that is a choice rather than free text, a threshold the check applies, and claimant types beyond the paper's five — none of which needs any other change.

How confident are you?                   High / Medium / Low

If you disagree with our recommendation, say so here:

......................................................................

A build phase checks what switching customer due diligence on would stop, and switches it on only if it records the checks without refusing work nobody can yet rate. The rest is still for the solicitor: the risk-rating scale, the ownership threshold (25% is usual), and what to do for an LLP, a trust or an ordinary partnership.

 **Checked 16 September 2026 (Phase 132e) — and left off, because switching it on would hold up work.** Switching customer due diligence on would not refuse a claim for want of a risk rating: a rating is never among what a check needs, and it stays free text. But it would stop every claim moving past the engagement stage until a finished check is on record for that client — what was identified, how it was verified, the evidence, the beneficial ownership position, the authority to bind and the date. The instruction was to leave it off if it would stop work, so it stays off, and a test pins both that and what switching it on would require. **Your policy still stands and can be followed today:** a check can be opened and completed for every client with the switch off. Turning it on is one dated record on the compliance page, with no code change, once you are content that no claim should pass engagement without one. Nothing here answers whether money laundering supervision legally applies to us; that stays with the accountant and the solicitor.

## Which of the Stage 2 documents are mandatory, and at which stage?

 **Added 12 September 2026 by the CRM follow-on.** For Lakhbir. Nothing is blocked while it waits: the check it decides is built and switched off.

 **The question.** The CRM requirements ask for the documents that are mandatory at each stage. The Stage 2 upload list names ten documents a client may send, and marks some as needed only in some cases — "where any spend was grant-funded" — but it never says which must have arrived before a claim may move on, or at which stage. The build can now refuse to move a claim out of data gathering until named documents have been received through the client's upload. It ships switched off until you say which documents, and at which stage.

 **What we think.** Confidence: medium. Four of the ten can be made a condition today, because the fact that says whether each one applies is already recorded: the fire risk assessment on every claim; the grant or NHS premises funding agreement where the claim's facts record grant funding; the sale or purchase contract, with any s.198 election schedule, where they record an election; and the lease where the property is leasehold. The other six — the operation and maintenance manuals, the asbestos register, the pre-contract enquiry replies, the contractor's cost breakdown, the insurance reinstatement valuation and the Building Regulations completion certificate — depend on things nothing records yet, such as a refurbishment or a building older than 2000, so we would keep asking for them without making them a condition. Data gathering is the stage that collects them, so that is where we would check.

 **An example.** A leasehold GP surgery, with no grant and no election recorded. With the check on as we recommend, its claim could not leave data gathering until a fire risk assessment and the lease had each arrived through the client's upload and passed the receipt strip. Asking the client for them would not count. And if nobody had yet recorded whether any spend was grant-funded, the claim would wait until somebody did: not knowing does not pass.

 **Our recommendation.** Switch the check on with those four, at data gathering. If any of them should be required at a different stage — before the survey, say — or another of the ten should be required too, tell us which: each is a one-line change.

 **What your answer unblocks.** Row 29.5 of the CRM disposition table — the mandatory documents per stage — which is built and switched off; and a survey that no longer starts without the papers it needs.

## Should we keep the files clients upload, or keep deleting them once read?

 **Added 21 September 2026 by Phase 140. For Lakhbir, with the data protection officer.** Nothing is blocked. Keeping the files is built, tested and switched off. Turning it on is one line, a key on the host, and a daily clean-up.

 **The question.** You asked to "see what the customer has uploaded". Today nobody can open the file itself. When a client sends a file, we read it, keep what we need from it, and delete the file straight away. That deletion is a promise: our privacy notice and our retention rules are written on it. Should we keep the original files, and if so, for how long and who may open them?

 **What we think.** Confidence: medium. Both sides are real. Keeping the file is what staff expect: a surveyor's query, a disputed figure or an HMRC enquiry is easier with the page in front of you. Deleting it is safer: a lease or a set of accounts can name people, and a file we do not hold cannot leak, be asked for under a rights request, or be kept too long. Turning it on changes the privacy notice (it must say we keep the file and for how long), the retention schedule (a new period for the file itself), and the rights procedure (a client can ask for the file). One more thing the officer should know: our own records say an earlier question about keeping raw files was answered on 2 September 2026 as "keep for the defence period", but the running system never switched to it and still deletes. This question settles it for the files clients send us.

 **An example.** Elverton Private Hospital Limited uploads its 12-page lease. Today the Files folder shows "Lease 1 — The client — 21 Sep 2026 — Read and then deleted — here is what we took from it: We read 12 pages. We kept 30 parts of its text. We took out 2 parts we must not keep." Switched on for 90 days, the same card also shows **Open the original** to Admin until 20 December 2026. That night the file is deleted, and the card goes back to "Read and then deleted". The file is kept encrypted, never where a web address can reach it, and every opening is written in the audit log.

 **Our recommendation.** Keep it switched off until two things are done: the officer approves a period and the privacy notice and retention schedule are changed to say so. Then switch it on for 90 days, Admin only. 90 days covers the survey and the report on a typical claim without holding a person's papers for years. If you want the file kept for a possible HMRC enquiry, that is six years, and it should be a separate decision.

 **What your answer unblocks.** One line in the build (`KEEP_ORIGINALS` in 09-app/backend/src/web/originals.ts: switched on, with the number of days); an encryption key set on the host; a daily run of the clean-up (`node src/cli.ts sweep-originals`); and, if you want Sales to open originals too, a change to who may open them, with its own test. The privacy notice and retention schedule changes are for the officer and the solicitor.

......................................................................

The data protection officer approves the period:   Yes / No / Not yet

How confident are you?    High / Medium / Low
