## Is our fee now a flat 3% of the allowances identified — with no minimum and no bands?

 **Added 12 September 2026 by Phase 129. For Lakhbir.** Nothing has been switched or rebuilt on the strength of it — which is why it is asked. It also replaces an ask the board was making twice, about whether the minimum survived 1 September.

 **The question.** Three answer boxes in this section now read as a new fee. Under _"Is the small end of the market worth serving at all?"_ there is a table headed "Your fee @ 3%". Under _"The multi-site discount was capped at 15%…"_ the box says "Looking to offer a flat fee of 3% for 1 or more sites - avg market rate is 5% - 7%". Under _"The rate card's fee bands were written against tax saved…"_ it says "no banding - flat fee of 3%". None of the three is dated or signed. The fee base decided on 3 September is **5% of the capital allowances identified**, and the engine, the rate card, the business plan and clause 4 of the engagement letter are all built on 5%. So: is the fee now a flat 3% of the allowances identified, on every job and any number of sites, with no bands, no multi-site discount and no minimum?

 **What we think.** Confidence: medium that this is what the three boxes mean, and none that it is final, because it changes the price rather than a detail of it. On the minimum, your answer of 1 September is already on record and we are not asking it again from scratch: _"No minimum — smallest job is a £0 purchase price, and the fee is a set percentage."_ Read plainly, that retires a minimum **fee** as well as a minimum **job size**, and a flat 3% with no bands is consistent with it.

 **An example, from your own table.** A £500,000 purchase with allowances at 35% is £175,000 of allowances. At 3% the fee is **£5,250**; at the decided 5% it is **£8,750**. What the job costs us does not change, so every break-even purchase price in the pricing papers rises — we will re-run the job-economics model and the business plan on 3% the day you confirm, rather than print an estimate of them here.

 **Our recommendation.** Confirm it or correct it in one line. If it is right, the engine's fee rate, the rate card, the business plan model and clause 4 of the engagement letter are rebuilt on 3%, the £5,000 / £7,500 floor comes out of both minimum-fee papers, and the fee-band and multi-site questions above close as answered. If 3% was a thought rather than a decision, say so and nothing moves.

 **What your answer unblocks.** The fee every client is quoted; the rate card going to Saddique; the minimum-fee and rate-card tasks on the board, which both asked whether the minimum survived 1 September and now point here; and the break fee paper, whose figures are costed against a 5% fee.

The 3% is a decision, and the rebuild goes to a build phase: the engine's fee rate, the rate card, the business plan model, the break-fee and minimum-fee papers, and clause 4 of the letter. ## Should the rates rulebook and its Scottish income tax addendum go to the tax adviser in this review, or wait for a separate one?

 **Added 12 September 2026 by Phase 129. For Lakhbir.** First asked on 29 August, on the tax adviser's review pack, and never answered.

 **The question.** The tax adviser's review covers rulebooks A to F. Two more documents carry questions only a tax adviser can answer: **Rulebook I**, the rates and reliefs table that every figure the engine prints comes from (six questions), and its **addendum on Scottish income tax** (four). The review pack assumes both are out, although neither document says so of itself. Do the ten go into this engagement, or wait for a separate rates review?

 **What we think.** Confidence: medium. Put them in. Every relief figure a client sees is an allowance multiplied by a rate from Rulebook I. When the Scottish rates were checked on 25 August, both ends of the old range were wrong. And on 12 September a switch was built, and left off, that makes the Scottish rate follow where the taxpayer lives rather than where the property is — it waits on exactly this review.

 **An example.** A Scottish-resident dentist who owns a practice in England claims £100,000 of allowances. Valued on the property's nation, at the rest-of-UK top rate of 45%, the relief is **£45,000**. Valued on where the dentist lives, at the Scottish top rate of 48%, it is **£48,000**. The rule moves that one figure by £3,000, and nobody qualified has confirmed the rule or either rate.

 **Our recommendation.** Put the ten into this engagement. At the rate our own estimate uses for the review — five to six days for forty-five questions — ten more is roughly a day, and the adviser should correct that rather than take it from us.

 **What your answer unblocks.** Ten entries on the tax adviser's review pack, created the day you answer; the Scottish main-home switch; and a signature under every rate the reports print.

## Who wrote the answer under the stamp-duty question?

 **Added 14 September 2026 by Phase 131. For Lakhbir.** Nothing is switched on because of that answer, and nothing will be until this is settled.

 **The question.** Under _"When a building is bought, is the stamp duty part of the price we apportion?"_, in the tax adviser's section, both parts of the answer box have been filled in. Part 1 says the transaction tax "is included in the consideration/capital cost being apportioned, to the extent attributable to the qualifying asset". Part 2 says legal and other incidental acquisition costs "can follow it in where they are directly attributable … and are capital expenditure". **There is no name, no date and no confidence**, and no chartered tax adviser has been appointed. **Who wrote it, and on whose authority?**

 **What we think.** Confidence: **high that it is not yet a signature we can act on**. This document runs on the rule that a reading which makes claims bigger is switched on only by a qualified adviser's answer. On 12 September the answer was left unactioned for that reason, and both switches stayed off. It may well be right: it reads like a professional's answer, and Part 2 describes exactly the "directly attributable" figure the second switch was built to capture. But an answer with no author could be an adviser's, your own reading, or text from another source, and each means something different for what we may do with it.

 **An example.** A trading company buys a care home for £2,000,000 and pays £90,000 of stamp duty. With Part 1 switched on, the claim is apportioned from £2,090,000 instead of £2,000,000 — about 4 to 5% more on every purchase claim we will ever produce. **Turning that on because of an unsigned paragraph is the exact failure the switches exist to prevent.**

 **Our recommendation.** Tell us who wrote it and where it came from. **If it is a qualified adviser's**, ask them to add their name, the date and their confidence in the box, and the switch can be considered. **If it is your own reading, or from somewhere else**, say so: we will keep the text, mark it in the box as unsigned input for the adviser to confirm or correct, and both switches stay off.

 **What your answer unblocks.** Whether the stamp-duty answer can be treated as answered, and with it the single largest systematic under-claim we know about — or, if not, a clear note in that box so the tax adviser reads it as a draft rather than an answer. **Read 15 September 2026 (Phase 132c).** Partly answered: the box says the answer under the stamp-duty question will not be signed by a tax adviser, and marks "Another source" with a question mark. Acted on, as our recommendation said: that text is kept, and a note under the stamp-duty question now marks it as unsigned input for the tax adviser to confirm or correct. Both stamp-duty switches stay off. It would still help to know where the text came from.

```

## May we keep using the price book's thirteen region names while the book itself is quarantined?

 **Added 14 September 2026 by Phase 131. For Lakhbir, to put to the solicitor if you are unsure.** Nothing is blocked. The names are in use today, in the rate library the surveyor will work in.

 **The question.** On 9 September the surveyor's rate library was keyed on a real region. So that there would be one regional grouping rather than three, the build took **thirteen region names** from the Architects' and Builders' 2024 price book — one of the three books quarantined under _"Where did the Spon's price books on our disk come from, and may we put their rates in a client report?"_. **No figure was taken.** A test fails if any number appears in the list, and which council sits in which region comes from Office for National Statistics geography, published free under the Open Government Licence. **May the names stay while the books' provenance is unresolved?**

 **What we think.** Confidence: **medium that the names are not the book's to own, low on the law**. Eleven of the thirteen are ordinary region and country names that the Office for National Statistics also uses: South East, South West, East of England, East Midlands, West Midlands, North East, North West, Wales, Scotland, Northern Ireland, and "Yorkshire and Humberside", which the ONS spells "Yorkshire and The Humber". The other two, **Inner London** and **Outer London**, are the book's way of dividing London, and that division is in ordinary public use: the London Government Act 1963 lists the Inner London boroughs. A list of names is not the rates the quarantine exists to keep out. But the list was chosen because it is the book's, and we have not established how we came by the book.

 **An example.** A Manchester property resolves to "North West" and a Cardiff property to "Wales" — the same names whether they came from the book or from the ONS. **London is where the source shows.** The ONS has one region called London; the book has two, priced about five per cent apart, which on a £4m build-up is £200,000 of replacement cost. That split is the one part of the vocabulary only the book gives us.

 **Our recommendation.** **Keep the names**, and record against the region list that it holds no figure and that the names are public geography. If you or the solicitor would rather the book played no part at all, the fallback is one line: use the ONS spellings and treat London as one region — already one of the answers offered to the surveyor under _"Which London boroughs count as Inner London?"_.

 **What your answer unblocks.** Whether the rate library's regions stay as built or switch to the ONS list; and one sentence in the surveyor's register, which today says only that the names were taken. **Read 15 September 2026 (Phase 132c).** The box says "maybe discuss with solicitor", the "ask the solicitor" answer. Acted on: the point is added to the solicitor's matters in FOR THE SOLICITOR, THE DPO AND THE DEVELOPER. Meanwhile the thirteen region names stay in use, holding no figure, and the surveyor's register says so. If the solicitor would rather the book played no part, the fallback is the ONS names with London as one region.

## Switch on automatic site area from the land boundaries, without waiting for Ordnance Survey?

 **Added 14 September 2026 by Phase 131. For Lakhbir.** No licence stands in the way of the site-area half any more. Building footprints still wait on the Ordnance Survey email.

 **The question.** The land boundary connector is built, with fetching switched off. It was held back on the belief that Ordnance Survey's permission might be needed even for internal use. HM Land Registry's published terms for its boundary polygons were read on 13 September 2026, and say the polygons are "Available for use and reuse under the Open Government Licence" and that "Use under the OGL is free". Commercial use is allowed, with no account, payment, rate limit or retention limit. Two attribution statements are required: HM Land Registry's and Ordnance Survey's (licence number AC0000851063). **May we switch the connector on for calculating a site's area internally, now?**

 **What we think.** Confidence: **high on the licence, medium on the value**. The polygons show only an "indicative extent" of freehold titles, and leaseholds are not included. Title plan boundaries are general boundaries, so a site area from them is graded below the top grade and can never carry a figure to sign-off on its own: it is a sanity check, not a measurement. **Building footprints are a different dataset** — Ordnance Survey premium data, where the open point is whether a footprint area we compute and keep is storable — and that is asked in the Ordnance Survey email, which is ready to send. A boundary plan inside a client report is permitted with both statements, but that is a separate decision and stays switched off.

 **An example, on our Cardiff test case.** The building is 1,346 m² of floor area over three floors. For that building the connector should return a site of 1,000 to 5,000 m², and a footprint of 430 to 560 m² once footprints are available. **Any site area below 50 m² or above 100,000 m² is refused as a units error.** The building-fits-its-site check needs the site to be at least 1,346 ÷ 3, about 449 m², so a boundary that gave 400 m² would be flagged rather than used.

 **Our recommendation.** Set the connector's permission to **internal calculation only**, and record who decided and when. Keep boundary maps out of reports, put both attribution statements in the sources note, and leave footprints until Ordnance Survey replies. Switching the connector on still needs the small, logged test lookup asked of the data protection officer in section five.

 **What your answer unblocks.** Automatic site area, and the building-fits-its-site check, once a developer has checked the connector's five format assumptions and written the fetcher. **Read 15 September 2026 (Phase 132c).** The box says "Yes": set the land boundary connector for internal calculation only, and boundary maps stay out of client reports. A build phase sets that permission in the connector, records who decided it and when, and puts both attribution statements in the sources note. Fetching stays off: switching it on still needs the test-lookup answer from the data protection officer in section five, and a developer to write the fetcher. Building footprints still wait on Ordnance Survey.

## How many hours should a surveyor have to tell us about a breach, and where?

 **Added 14 September 2026 by Phase 131. For Lakhbir, with the solicitor and the data protection officer deciding one part.** Nothing is blocked today: no surveyor is appointed, and that is why it can be written in now.

 **The question.** A draft clause for the surveyor's appointment terms is in the supplier breach-duties document. It gives the surveyor **24 hours** to tell us about a breach, by email to the privacy mailbox **and** a telephone call to you, in two versions: one for a surveyor acting as our processor, one for a surveyor acting as an independent controller. **The number of hours and the telephone number are yours.** Which version applies is for the solicitor and the data protection officer.

 **What we think.** Confidence: **medium**. As a processor, the surveyor already has to tell us "without undue delay", but the law gives no number. As an independent controller, the law obliges them to tell us nothing at all, so we might never learn that our client's documents were exposed. 24 hours is shorter than the 48 the only named extraction-model candidate publishes and Microsoft's 72, and a one-person firm can act on a weekend notice with a telephone call. The address stays in square brackets **because the privacy mailbox has not been shown to be live**: an emailed breach notice nobody reads still starts our own clock, which is why the telephone limb is there.

 **An example.** A surveyor's laptop holding a client's lease is stolen at **18:00 on a Friday**. With 24 hours, we hear by **18:00 on Saturday**, and our own decision starts from a known time. "Without undue delay" has no date. As an independent controller with no clause, the surveyor need not tell us at all.

 **Our recommendation.** **24 hours**, and a telephone number you will answer at a weekend. **Write no email address into any terms until the privacy mailbox is proven live.** Let the solicitor and the officer choose the version, and delete the other.

 **What your answer unblocks.** The breach clause in the surveyor's appointment terms; and two of the things still waiting on you on the supplier breach-notification task. **Read 15 September 2026 (Phase 132c).** Partly answered: 24 hours is confirmed, which is what the draft clause already says. Still needed: the telephone number a surveyor rings for a breach, marked "?" — and your answer on the contact number says there may be no phone line, so please say whether there will be one for this. Which version of the clause applies, processor or independent controller, stays with the solicitor and the data protection officer.

## Who decides a rights request or a data protection complaint when the officer is unreachable, or is the subject of it?

 **Added 14 September 2026 by Phase 131. For Lakhbir, with the data protection officer.** Nothing is blocked today. It applies from the first request or complaint that arrives while the officer is away, or is about him.

 **The question.** On 1 September you were named deputy for a breach decision. Rule D3 of the officer's instrument confines that route to four things — the decision to report a breach to the regulator, the decision to tell the people affected, fixing the time we became aware, and opening the breach entry — "nothing else". It says in terms that rights-request decisions and data protection complaints are not delegated by it. **So nobody is named to decide a rights request or a data protection complaint when the officer cannot be reached, or a complaint about a decision the officer took himself.** Who decides?

 **What we think.** Confidence: **medium**. The clocks run regardless: a rights request has a month, and a data protection complaint must be acknowledged within 30 days and decided without undue delay. The complaints pack's only safeguard for a complaint about the officer is your countersignature, as a second look, plus telling the person of their route to the Information Commissioner. There are two honest answers: name someone with no commercial interest in the outcome, or record that you decide, with the countersignature noted as being the same person, exactly as was done for breaches.

 **An example.** On day 1 a person complains that the officer wrongly refused their request to erase their data. **The officer is the subject of the complaint.** The 30-day acknowledgement runs from day 1, and the outcome is owed without undue delay. Today nobody is named who may decide it. If you decide it, you also countersign it: one person, twice.

 **Our recommendation.** Name someone with no commercial interest in the outcome if you can. **Otherwise record that you decide**, with the countersignature noted as the same person, as a known limitation — and, in the same letter, tell the person that they may take the complaint to the Information Commissioner.

 **What your answer unblocks.** The open point in the complaints pack's section on who decides; and the same gap in the officer's readiness checks. **Read 15 September 2026 (Phase 132c).** The box holds only "?", so it is not an answer and the gap stays: nobody is named to decide a rights request or a data protection complaint when the officer is away or is its subject. ## Where a client has no accountant or tax adviser, who confirms the claim fits their return before we report and invoice?

 **Added 14 September 2026 by Phase 131. For Lakhbir, with the tax adviser on the wording about the amendment window.** Nothing is blocked today. But a client with no accountant can never get past the reporting stage.

 **The question.** Clause 1 of the engagement letter says that where the client has an accountant or tax adviser, that person signs the filing position. Where they have none, we may introduce our own chartered tax adviser — but that option is in square brackets and **not available**, because none is appointed. Otherwise the client remains responsible. Stage 8 of the operating procedures, and the reports stage in the application, still ask for **the accountant's confirmation** that the claim fits the return before a report is released and an invoice raised. **For a client with no accountant, who gives that confirmation?**

 **What we think.** Confidence: **medium**. There are three honest options: (a) accept the client's own written confirmation, which matches what clause 1 already says; (b) wait until our own tax adviser can be introduced, which may be months; or (c) refuse clients with no accountant at engagement. Option (a) keeps the promise the letter already makes, provided the client is told the facts they are confirming.

 **An example.** A sole-trader dentist buys a practice for £500,000 and has no accountant. The claim identifies **£112,500** of allowances, so our fee is **£5,625**. The tax adviser signs off the figure, and the client accepts the report. The gate then waits for an accountant's confirmation that nobody can give. **The claim never leaves reporting, £5,625 is never invoiced**, and the client is never formally told the last day they can amend their return.

 **Our recommendation.** Until a tax adviser can be introduced, **accept the client's own written confirmation** of three things: that they will file the amendment themselves; the last day to amend, stated by us; and that they remain responsible for the filing position. Record it with its source — "the client's own confirmation", never as an accountant's — and ask the tax adviser to word the amendment-window sentence.

 **What your answer unblocks.** Stages 8 and 9 of the operating procedures for a client with no accountant, and the reports gate in the application for that client. **Read 15 September 2026 (Phase 132c).** The box says "The client": where a client has no accountant or tax adviser, the client's own written confirmation is accepted, as we recommended. A build phase changes stages 8 and 9 of the operating procedures and the reports gate in the application to accept it, recorded as the client's own confirmation and never as an accountant's. The tax adviser still words the sentence about the last day to amend.

```
ANSWER
......The client................................................................
Client with no accountant - who confirms before we report and invoice?
   The client, in writing, as recommended  /  Wait for our own tax adviser  /  Decline such clients

How confident are you?    High / Medium / Low

Answered by:                                     Date:

If you disagree with our recommendation, what should we do instead?


......................................................................

 **Built 16 September 2026 (Phase 132e).** Stages 8 and 9 of the operating procedures and the application's reports gate now accept, for a client with no accountant or tax adviser, the client's own written confirmation of three things: that they will file the amendment; the last day to amend, as we state it; and that they remain responsible for the filing position. It is stored as the client's own confirmation, a separate record that can never be read as an accountant's. If any of the three is missing, "not sure" or never given, the gate stays shut, and a client who has an accountant still goes the accountant's way. Only Admin and Sales may record it. **The last-day sentence is marked for the tax adviser's wording.**

## Should we keep the Microsoft 365 audit log beyond six months, and is it acceptable that nobody can see who opens the breach register on monday.com?

 **Added 14 September 2026 by Phase 131. For Lakhbir, and the data protection officer once appointed.** Nothing has been switched on, and nobody has signed in to either admin console.

 **The question.** Two decisions, both about whether anyone could say afterwards who opened what. **Microsoft 365:** the standard audit log comes with the Business plans and keeps records for **180 days**; Microsoft says auditing **is not switched on by default** on Business Basic, Standard or Premium; the premium log, which keeps OneDrive records for a year, needs E5 or an add-on; and **which plan our Microsoft organisation is on is not recorded anywhere**. **monday.com:** our account is on the **Pro** plan; the audit log, which records sign-ins and who did what, is **Enterprise only**; what Pro has is each board's activity log, which records changes to a board — **not who signed in, opened a board or downloaded a file**. **Do we keep the Microsoft log beyond 180 days, and do we accept the monday.com gap for the breach register boards?**

 **What we think.** Confidence: **medium**. An audit log cannot be produced after the event: one that was switched off, or has aged out, holds nothing, and it is the only record the breach decision's four facts could draw on. Exporting the Microsoft log costs time, not money. Upgrading monday.com to see who opens two boards is a large spend for a small gap, and the gap can be stated honestly instead.

 **An example.** A work laptop is lost on **1 March**, and the breach is found on **15 October**. With auditing on, records from before about **18 April** have already gone at 180 days — the loss itself, and the six weeks after it. With auditing never switched on, which is the default on a Business plan, there is nothing at all. **And had someone opened the breach register board on monday.com in that time, nothing would show it.**

 **Our recommendation.** **Check the Microsoft plan and switch auditing on now** — it is one command, set out in Appendix C of the Cyber Essentials readiness pack — and record the date it was confirmed. **Export the Microsoft log monthly to a restricted folder**, so it outlives 180 days. **Record the monday.com gap as a known limitation** for the breach register boards, rather than upgrading.

 **What your answer unblocks.** Evidence for the four facts a breach record turns on; and an honest statement of the breach register's access limits. **Read 15 September 2026 (Phase 132c).** The box holds only "?", so it is not an answer and nothing has changed: Microsoft auditing is not confirmed as on, and nobody can see who opens the breach boards. Asked again: which Microsoft 365 plan we are on, whether to switch auditing on now and export the log monthly, and whether to record the monday.com gap as a known limitation. Checking the plan and switching auditing on are yours, in the admin centre.

## Once we are VAT-registered, should the fee-versus-benefit figure shown to a client who cannot recover VAT include the VAT?

 **Added 14 September 2026 by Phase 131. For Lakhbir, and the accountant confirms.** Nothing is blocked. We are not registered, and nothing is charged VAT today.

 **The question.** Once registered, our fee carries VAT at 20%. A dental practice doing clinical work only, a GP surgery or a care home generally cannot recover VAT, so for them the VAT is a real cost. **When we show such a client how our fee compares with their tax benefit, should the fee figure include the VAT?** This is about disclosure only. Whether to cap, warn about or adjust the fee is asked separately under _"Do we cap the fee, warn about it, or adjust the percentage?"_, and nothing here answers it.

 **What we think.** Confidence: **medium**. Registration is compulsory once taxable turnover in the last 12 months goes over **£90,000**, or is expected to in the next 30 days alone. At a 5% fee, that is **£1.8 million of allowances identified** in a year, and one large claim can bring it forward with little notice. A comparison that leaves out a cost the client really bears understates the fee to exactly the clients least able to absorb it.

 **An example.** **£400,000** of allowances identified gives a fee of **£20,000**, plus £4,000 VAT: an invoice of **£24,000**, which is 6% of the allowances for a client who cannot recover it. A limited company at the 26.5% marginal rate gets **£19,080** back in the first year for a period before April 2026 (main rate 18%): the fee is **£920** more than that without VAT, and **£4,920** more with VAT it cannot recover. For a period from April 2026 (main rate 14%) it gets **£14,840**, and the gaps are **£5,160** and **£9,160**. A partly exempt practice whose taxable work is 15% of its supplies gets back about £600 and pays about £23,400.

 **Our recommendation.** **Show the VAT-inclusive fee to any client who cannot recover the VAT**, and the VAT-exclusive fee to one who can. Have the wording ready by the time fees reach 75% of the threshold in any 12 months: £67,500, or about £1.35 million of allowances identified.

 **What your answer unblocks.** The fee-versus-benefit wording for exempt healthcare clients, due before registration becomes compulsory.

```
ANSWER
.........TBC - as we may hit the VAT threshold in year 1.............................................................
For a client who cannot recover VAT, show the fee comparison:
   Including VAT  /  Excluding VAT  /  Both

How confident are you?    High / Medium / Low

Answered by:                                     Date:

If you disagree with our recommendation, what should we do instead?


......................................................................
```

 **Read 15 September 2026 (Phase 132c).** The box says "TBC - as we may hit the VAT threshold in year 1". It does not choose, so it is recorded as not yet answered, but it makes the answer more urgent. Registration is compulsory once taxable turnover passes £90,000 in 12 months; at a 3% fee that is £3 million of allowances identified, and our recommended point to have the wording ready (75%) is £67,500 of fees. Asked again: include VAT, exclude it, or show both, for a client who cannot recover VAT? The accountant confirms.

 **16 September 2026 (Phase 132e).** Still not answered, and it now reaches one more place. The new £1,000 no-claim test compares the client's relief with our fee, and for a client who cannot recover VAT the fee they really bear is 3.6% of the allowances, not 3%. Whether that test counts the VAT is asked under "Is the £1,000 no-claim test measured on five years of relief or over the life of the claim — and before or after VAT?"; the answer here would normally decide it.

## Now the fee is a flat 3%, how large may a multi-site discount be, who approves it, and do the old uplifts survive?

 **Added 16 September 2026 by Phase 132e. For Lakhbir.** Nothing is blocked: every quote is the flat 3%, and no discount is quoted until this is answered.

 **The question.** Your answer under "Is our fee now a flat 3% of the allowances identified — with no minimum and no bands?" says "A further multi-site discount can be applied depending on site/opportunities". It does not say how far a discount may go, or who may agree one. And the old banded rate card carried two add-ons — complexity uplifts of up to 1.5 percentage points, and a fixed £1,500 where a client declines automated register lookups. **(a) What is the largest multi-site discount? (b) Who approves one? (c) Does a flat fee still carry the uplifts and the £1,500 supplement?**

 **What we think.** Confidence: **medium**. A ceiling matters because a discount comes straight out of our margin, and the engagement gate tests the quoted fee before any discount, so a large enough discount can take a job below cost without anything noticing. The old rule, "the effective rate never falls below 2.5% without your approval", was written for the bands; at 3% it would be crossed by any discount above about 16.7%. The uplifts make a flat fee no longer flat, and the £1,500 was a first estimate, never measured.

 **An example.** Six care homes with £3.24 million of allowances between them: the fee is £97,200, or £16,200 a home. At 120 jobs a year a care home costs us about £6,150 loaded, so each home earns about £10,050. A 20% discount leaves £6,810 a home. The engagement gate's line for a care home at 120 jobs is £7,687.50 of fee, so a discount above about 52.5% takes each home below the line, and above about 62% each home loses money. At 25 jobs a year the cost is about £8,050 and those points come much sooner.

 **Our recommendation.** (a) Set the ceiling so that a discounted fee still clears the engagement gate's line for that sector at the volume we are actually doing. (b) Keep approval with you until there is a record of discounts given. (c) Drop the complexity uplifts, so the fee stays flat, and keep the manual-handling supplement only once its cost has been measured.

 **What your answer unblocks.** Quoting any multi-site discount; the rate card going to Saddique as an approved version; and the engine's discount setting, which holds your ceiling and approver as blanks today.

## Is the £1,000 no-claim test measured on five years of relief or over the life of the claim — and before or after VAT?

 **Added 16 September 2026 by Phase 132e. For Lakhbir; the tax adviser reviews the wording.** Nothing is blocked: the outcome is built, on five years and before VAT, and marked for review.

 **The question.** You decided under "Below what size of claim do we advise a client not to claim at all?" that we advise against claiming where the client's tax relief after our fee is below £1,000. Building it needed two choices your answer did not make. **(a) Is the relief measured over the first five years, or over the whole life of the claim? (b) For a client who cannot recover VAT, is our fee counted with the VAT?**

 **What we think.** Confidence: **medium**. The engine works out relief over five years; that is the longest period it calculates, and relief further out is less certain and worth less today. Year one alone would fire on nearly every claim on writing-down allowances. The documents said "over the life of the claim", which is larger for slow-running pools, so fewer clients would be advised not to claim. On VAT, a client who cannot recover it really pays 3.6% of the allowances.

 **An example.** £20,000 of special-rate plant for a company paying 25%. Our fee is £600, or £720 with VAT. Over five years the relief is £1,330.48, so £730.48 after our fee, or £610.48 after the fee with VAT: below £1,000 either way, and we would advise against claiming. Over the life of the claim the relief is £5,000 (25% of £20,000), so £4,400 after our fee: well above, and we would recommend claiming.

 **Our recommendation.** (a) Keep five years, and say so in the words the client sees. (b) Count the VAT where the client cannot recover it, to match our recommendation under "Once we are VAT-registered, should the fee-versus-benefit figure shown to a client who cannot recover VAT include the VAT?". The client still chooses either way.

 **What your answer unblocks.** The no-claim wording going to the tax adviser as settled, and the no-claim report and client journey, which today carry both phrasings with a note.

......................................................................

## How likely is our open-ended free enquiry support to cost us, and how badly?

 **Added 16 September 2026 by Phase 132e. For Lakhbir.** Nothing is blocked. The risk is on the register, unscored, and must not be read as low.

 **The question.** You decided to keep free support on our valuation and apportionment if HMRC opens an enquiry, with no time limit and no limit on hours. The risk register now carries that as a risk, but its method needs two judgements nobody has made: **how likely it is to happen in the next 12 months (1 to 5), and how much it would hurt (1 to 5) — once before any controls, and once assuming they work.**

 **What we think.** Confidence: **low**, because there are no facts yet: no claim has been filed, so there is no record of how often HMRC opens enquiries on our claims or how long one takes. Two things make it worse than it first looks. The promise sits alongside clause 13, so on a reduced claim our fee goes down while our cost goes up. And no professional indemnity policy is held, so nothing yet covers it.

 **An example.** A claim for £500,000 of allowances earns a fee of £15,000. An enquiry cuts the allowances to £400,000: we refund £3,000, and we also pay the surveyor and the tax adviser for every hour of the enquiry. On the register's scale, "possible" (3) times "moderate" (3) is 9, Medium; "likely" (4) times "major" (4) is 16, Critical — and the register's own rule says a Critical residual risk means the activity should be paused until it is better controlled.

 **Our recommendation.** Score it before any controls as possible (3) and major (4), 12, High, because one long enquiry on a large claim could involve several specialists; and leave the residual score until the broker says whether cover responds. Record the time spent on every enquiry from the first, so the score can rest on facts.

 **What your answer unblocks.** The risk register's scoring and bands, which leave this risk out of their counts today, and the escalation rule, which fires at a residual score of 10.

```

After controls:     Likelihood (1-5) ......    Impact (1-5) ......   /  wait for the broker

## Should the surveyor see a client's chat?

 **Added 22 September 2026 by Phase 142. For Lakhbir.** Nothing is blocked: the chat is built, and the surveyor sees neither thread until you say otherwise.

 **The question.** Every client page now has a **Chat** with two tabs: **Team only**, which only Admin and Sales see (it is where the internal notes always were), and **With the client**, which the client reads and answers in their portal as **Messages**. Today the surveyor sees neither. Should they see one, both, or neither?

 **What we think.** Confidence: **high.** Neither, for now. The surveyor works from the survey job, which carries only the facts they need and never our estimate. Both threads are free text: a message can easily say "we think the building is worth about £2m" or "the client paid £1.4m", which is exactly the figure the surveyor must not see before they commit their own. If the surveyor needs to talk to the client, it can go through Sales, or we can add a third thread for the survey job later.

 **An example.** Sales writes on Team only: "Our range for Corvale is £180k to £240k — push the surveyor to visit this week." If the surveyor could read Team only, they would see our range before committing their figures, and their independent figure would no longer be independent. The same goes for a client writing "We paid £1.2m for the building" on With the client.

 **Our recommendation. Neither thread, as built.** If the surveyor needs a channel, we build a separate "survey job" thread that goes through the same checks as the survey job itself.

 **What your answer unblocks.** Nothing is waiting. If you answer "yes" to either thread, it is one line in the rule table (`client_message.read` or `internal_note.read`, adding the surveyor on an assigned job), plus a check that the survey proof still passes.

## When someone buys a building, can they add their own alteration costs on top of an agreed figure?

 **The question.** A seller and a buyer formally agree that the boiler in the building is worth £20,000. The buyer then spends £5,000 knocking a hole in a wall to get the new plant in. **Is the claim £20,000 or £25,000?**

 **What we think.** £25,000. Our reading is that the agreed figure caps what passes _from the seller_, and the buyer's own installation alterations are separate expenditure that sits on top of it rather than inside it. Confidence: medium. It is the single largest claim-increasing point in the whole system.

 **The director's provisional view (3 September):** _"Its £25,000. It the cost of the item + cost to install the item."_ — agrees with us.

 **A**** contradiction ****this ****qu****e****s****t****i****o****n**** ****u****s****e****d**** ****t****o**** ****c****a****rr****y****,**** ****n****o****w c****l****os****ed****.** Until 12 September 2026 the client-facing wording told every client that their own alterations "sit on top of any election cap rather than inside it" — the £25,000 reading — while the arithmetic did the £20,000 one. **T****he**** s****e****ntence**** ****no****w**** ****f****o****llows**** ****the ****sw****itch.** Off, the client is told the alterations were kept inside the cap and that the point is unconfirmed; on, the original sentence is said and the arithmetic matches it. You are answering the number only.


 **Our recommendation.** Confirm or reject it explicitly. If you confirm it, we switch the branch on and the wording changes with it. If you reject it, nothing changes.


 **What it unblocks.** The largest single claim-increasing correction we hold.

## Where a seller never claimed, does the pooling requirement apply at all?

 **The question.** Some sellers are barred from claiming outright — residential landlords, charities, pension funds, NHS bodies. If they could never have pooled the expenditure, was there ever anything to pool? Our system refuses these claims anyway.

 **What we think.** If the seller was never _entitled_ to claim, the pooling condition should not engage on a purchase from them. Confidence: medium-low. This is a real statutory argument, not a stretch, but we would not act on it alone.

 **The director's provisional view (3 September):** _"If there has never been a claim on the property for whatever reason and now the same property is entitled to a claim. This will mean that we can claim."_

 **Why we have not simply banked that.** It is framed on whether a claim was **ever made**. The question is whether the seller was ever **entitled** to make one. Those come apart, and the gap is the whole answer: a taxable seller who simply never got round to claiming is a completely different case from a charity that could never have claimed at all. We believe only the second is capable of engaging the argument. **If that distinction is real, it is what we build into the client questionnaire — so please say so directly.**

 **The example, and it is not a rare transaction.** A dental practice buys its building from a housing association. Under the current system we tell them there is no claim. If we are wrong, we are turning away everyone who bought from a charity, a housing association or an NHS body — which in healthcare property is a meaningful share of the market.

 **Our recommendation.** Answer the entitlement version of the question, and tell us whether we should be asking the client "could the seller have claimed?" rather than "did they?".

 **What it unblocks.** A whole class of purchases currently refused, and one question in the client flow.

```
ANSWER
...If the buyer buys the property from a non-tax payer, then they they are they totally eligible without ever worying if
previuos owner ever claimed...................................................................
Your answer: If the buyer acquires the property from a person or organisation that was not entitled to claim 
capital allowances, the fact that the previous owner never claimed should not by itself prevent the buyer from 
claiming. However, the buyer must still satisfy all other statutory conditions for its own claim. We should 
therefore ask whether the seller was entitled to claim, rather than simply whether the seller actually claimed.


How confident are you?    High / Medium / Low

Answered by:                                     Date:

If you disagree with our recommendation, what should we do instead?


...................................................................... 
```

 **Read 15 September 2026 (Phase 132c).** The box holds an unsigned answer: where the seller was not entitled to claim, the fact that it never claimed does not by itself stop the buyer, and we should ask whether the seller was entitled rather than whether it claimed. That matches the entitlement framing we asked for. It is not a tax adviser's answer, so it is recorded as a draft; these purchases are still refused and the client question is unchanged. Still needed: the tax adviser's answer.

## Is there a third route to fixing the value that we are missing?

 **The question.** Where the value of fixtures has to be fixed between buyer and seller, we offer two routes: a joint election, or an application to the tribunal. There appears to be a third — two written statements from past owners — and on our reading it carries **no time limit at all**. Our system does not offer it.

 **What we think.** We believe the route exists and is available where the owner in between was never able to claim. Confidence: low. We have read the provision and we may be misreading its scope.

 **The director did not answer this one. It is wholly yours.**

 **The example.** A client bought in 2019 from a developer who bought from a charity. The two-year window closed long ago and we currently tell them they are out of time. If the third route is real and has no time limit, **that client is not out of time at all** — and the difference is between a nil report and a claim.

 **Our recommendation.** If you confirm it, we will need to add two new questions to the client flow that we do not ask today, so tell us what facts you would need captured. If you reject it, say whether a closed window should return a firm "no" or stay open pending statements — at the moment we return an undetermined result rather than a nil, precisely so that we are not silently deciding this against the client.

 **What it unblocks.** Every case where the two-year window has closed, and two new client questions.

 **Note added 14 September 2026 by Phase 131.** The answer box asks "what is the 3rd route?", so here it is. The third route is **two written statements under section 187A(8) of the Capital Allowances Act 2001**: (a) a statement by the person who bought from the past owner (or the lessee) that the requirement to fix the value by election or tribunal "has not been met and is no longer capable of being met"; and (b) a statement by the past owner of the disposal value they actually brought into account. Under section 187A(6)(b), holding both satisfies the requirement, **but only where that in-between buyer was not entitled to claim allowances on the fixture** — and the section sets no time limit on it (read on legislation.gov.uk, 14 September 2026). The tax adviser's review pack first drafted its version of this question on the assumption that no such route exists. Rulebook A, at section 9 question 6, now sets out both versions for you to choose between. The route is built behind a switch that is off.

```
ANSWER
...........what is the 3rd route?...........................................................
Your answer:A statement from the person who acquired the fixture from the past owner (or the relevant lessee) 
confirming that the requirement to fix the disposal value by election or tribunal has not been met and is no longer 
capable of being met; and
A statement from the past owner confirming the disposal value they actually brought into account.

This route can satisfy the value-fixing requirement where the intervening owner was not entitled to claim 
allowances on the fixture. The provision does not specify the same two-year election/tribunal time limit for this 
statement route


How confident are you?    High / Medium / Low

Answered by:                                     Date:

If you disagree with our recommendation, what should we do instead?


...................................................................... 
```

 **Read 15 September 2026 (Phase 132c).** The box first asks "what is the 3rd route?" and then sets out the two written statements under section 187A(8), which restates the note above rather than confirming it. So it is not an answer, and nothing changes: a closed window still returns an undetermined result. Still needed from the tax adviser: does the route exist on these facts, and if so, which facts must we capture from the client?

## Does the two-year clock run from our client's purchase, or an earlier one?

 **The question.** Where there was an owner in between — a developer, a charity, a housing association, a property company that never claimed — **whose** purchase starts the clock?

 **What we think.** Our reading is that it runs from the acquisition by the purchaser _from the past owner_, not from our client's own acquisition. Confidence: medium. HMRC's own manuals disagree with each other here: one page states the statutory rule and another glosses it as running from the new owner's acquisition, and the question our system originally asked was a paraphrase of the gloss. **This reading is now BUILT AND LIVE, and that is a correction to what this question used to say.** Since Phase 50 the flow asks whether an owner sat in between, asks for that owner's purchase date, runs the clock from it, and **refuses to substitute the client's own date where the answer is not known** — it reports the window as not determinable instead. **It was one of three readings in the whole system shipped behind no switch.** Since 12 September 2026 it has a switch, set to keep this reading live, and it is disclosed to the tax adviser in FOR THE ACCOUNTANT, Section D. 


 **The director's provisional view.** _"2 year clock starts from completion date."_ — **This is the third time this half of the question has been answered.** The date point is correct, it was given before and it is already recorded: completion, not contract signature. But the question asks **whose purchase**, not which date.

 We are recording that plainly rather than counting it as answered, because the difference is not academic. **If it runs from the earlier purchase, a whole class of acquisitions arrives already dead** — before our client did anything at all — and we have to say so in the first conversation rather than after taking a fee.

 **Our recommendation.** Answer it as put: our client's purchase, or the earlier one. **Note what a rejection now costs, because it has changed twice.** Earlier drafts said a "the earlier purchase" answer would need the intervening-transaction question built; that question has been built and is running. And until 12 September 2026 a rejection meant a revert. **N****o****w**** the**** o****t****her reading is built too, behind**** a switch ****t****h****at i****s ****o****ff**: rejecting us is one line, and the clock then runs from the client's own completion date. The in-between-owner questions stay in the flow either way.


 **What it unblocks.** Whether a class of purchases is claimable at all, and what we are entitled to say at the first conversation.

```
ANSWER

Your answer:Capture the previous owner's acquisition of the property: who they bought it from, the date of that 
purchase, and whether that previous owner was entitled to claim capital allowances on the relevant fixtures

Answered by:                                     Date:

If you disagree with our recommendation, what should we do instead?


......................................................................

## In supported living, is a shared lounge claimable?

 **The question.** A supported-living scheme has self-contained flats and shared areas. Which of the shared areas can be claimed?

 **What we think.** Not the inside of a resident's own flat. Beyond that we genuinely do not know, and we have deliberately taken the narrow position.

 **The director's provisional view (3 September):** _"Supported living is different altogether, so yes its claimable and it's the whole property – it should be treated as normal. The only change of factor is a private HMO which we are not facilitating."_

 **⚠ This answer is flagged, not filed, and we have not acted on it. Three reasons:**

1.  **It is broader than the question.** We asked about a shared lounge where the flats are genuinely self-contained. The answer is "the whole property". **A corridor serving only flats, a communal laundry and a staff office are three different cases**, and the answer does not distinguish them — nor does it distinguish any of them from the inside of a resident's own flat, which the dwelling-house restriction excludes.

2.  **It contradicts a correction we already shipped.** On 20 August we _removed_ shared lounges, kitchens and resident bathrooms from the claimable base and from the client-facing copy, on the strength of HMRC's published guidance. That fix went in precisely because the wider position had been assessed as an over-claim.

3.  **It is the sector our own risk register names as our highest mis-selling exposure.**

 **We are not going to reverse a shipped over-claim correction on an internal view, in the sector we have identified as riskiest.**

 **Our recommendation.** Answer it case by case, because the cases differ: the shared lounge · a corridor serving only flats · a communal laundry · a staff office. **And if you think the wider reading is right and our August correction was wrong, say so directly** — that is a real answer and we will act on it.

 **Where the build has got to (12 September 2026).** The narrow position is live, behind a switch set to keep it. If you tell us a shared lounge or kitchen outside self-contained homes is a common part, that reading is built and tested and turning it on is one line. **It covers a shared lounge or kitchen only**; a corridor, a laundry or a staff office would be new work.

 **What it unblocks.** The claimable base in supported living, and the client-facing wording that describes it.

 **Note added 14 September 2026 by Phase 131.** One tribunal decision is often cited on this, and **it does not decide our question**. _Tevfik v HMRC_ [2019] UKFTT 0600 (TC), read in full on 13 September 2026, was about **houses in multiple occupation**, not supported living with self-contained flats. It adopted HMRC's Brief 45/10 — a communal kitchen and lounge are part of the dwelling-house, while a lobby, corridors and stairs are not — and the claim then failed because the taxpayer did not prove the expenditure. It does not decide whether a shared lounge outside self-contained flats can be claimed, which is what we are asking. **Its passing words that mains and service installations are not a dwelling-house would make claims bigger, and we do not rely on them.** Take a £120,000 central boiler serving twelve flats and a staff office: on those words alone all of it might qualify; on our current rows only the staff office's share does.

```
ANSWER
....If a home is a supported housing home, then the full house is claimable
for HMO's only the communal areas such hallways, shared lounge so no worth pursuing as value is less..................................................................
Your answer:


How confident are you?    High / Medium / Low

Answered by:                                     Date:

If you disagree with our recommendation, what should we do instead?


...................................................................... 
```

 **Read 15 September 2026 (Phase 132c).** The box holds an unsigned answer: in supported housing "the full house is claimable". That is the view recorded above from 3 September, which is flagged and not acted on because it would reverse the 20 August over-claim correction. It also contradicts the unsigned answer under "Where exactly is the line around someone's home?", which says keep the narrow position. The narrow position stays live. Still needed: the tax adviser's answer, case by case.

## Can a high-care scheme fall outside the "home" restriction entirely?

 **The question.** Where care is intensive and delivered on site, can the building fall outside the dwelling-house restriction altogether — so that the shared-areas ceiling has nothing to stand on?

 **What we think.** HMRC guidance accepts that specialised supported housing may not be a dwelling-house at all. If that is right, the effect is not marginal: **it lifts the ceiling entirely rather than raising it.** Confidence: low. We have built no branch for this, so at present we simply do not offer it.

 **The director gave a related view on 29 August** — that it applies where there is a commercial agreement in place. We recorded it with a reservation: a commercial agreement is _evidence_, but the published guidance appears to reason from **how much privacy residents actually have**, which is a question about the building and the care regime rather than about the paperwork.

 **Our recommendation.** Tell us what the test actually turns on, and what evidence would support it — registration, the care contract, or what is physically delivered on site. If the test is evidential rather than contractual, that changes which question we ask the client and which document we request.

 **What it unblocks.** The largest single uplift available in supported living, and a client question that does not exist today.

## When a landlord and tenant are connected, does a contribution reduce the claim?

 **The question.** A property company pays for a boiler in the building its own operating company trades from — same people owning both. The rule blocks the **payer** from claiming. Does it also reduce what the **occupier** can claim?

 **What we think.** No. You cannot deduct the same money twice, and if the contributor is barred from claiming contribution allowances then the occupier's own spend should stand in full. **Our system reduces it anyway.** Confidence: medium.

 **The director's provisional view (3 September):** _"Use your understanding."_

 **We are treating that as an answer that was not given, and we want to be explicit about why.** "Our understanding" here **is** the reading that makes claims bigger. It is exactly the class of reading this entire pack exists to avoid acting on alone. It has not been built and the engine continues to reduce the occupier's claim.

 **The example.** Property company plus operating company is one of the commonest structures in dental groups and care homes — arguably the default in both of our starting markets. **If our reading is right, we are under-claiming structurally rather than occasionally.** That is a reason to want your answer, not a reason to assume it.

 **Our recommendation.** If you confirm it, tell us what evidence of connection we should require, and confirmation that the contributor is not themselves claiming on the same spend. We would rather build the evidence requirement at the same time as the branch.

 **What it unblocks.** A structural under-claim across both opening markets.

.No — if the contributor is unable to claim capital allowances or otherwise obtain tax relief for the 
contribution, the contribution falls within the exception to the normal deduction rule. The occupier's qualifying 
expenditure is therefore not reduced by that contribution. Evidence should establish the connected relationship, 
who actually incurred/owns the expenditure, the nature and amount of the contribution, and that the contributor 
is not claiming or obtaining tax relief on it......................................................................
Your answer:

Answered by:                                     Date:

If you disagree with our recommendation, what should we do instead?


...................................................................... 
```

 **Read 15 September 2026 (Phase 132c).** The box holds an unsigned answer: "No" — where the connected contributor cannot claim or get relief, the occupier's claim is not reduced, with evidence of the connection, who incurred the spend and that the contributor is not claiming. That is the reading that makes claims bigger, so the engine keeps reducing the occupier's claim until a tax adviser confirms it. Recorded as a draft. Still needed: the tax adviser's answer.

## When a building is bought, is the stamp duty part of the price we apportion?

 **The question.** A trading company buys a care home for £2,000,000 and pays £90,000 of stamp duty on the purchase. We work out how much of what they spent was plant by apportioning the consideration. **Do we apportion £2,000,000, or £2,090,000?** And if the tax comes in, do the solicitor's fees and the other incidental costs of acquiring come in with it, or stay out?

 **What we think.** We do not have a settled view, and we would rather say that than dress a preference up as a reading. Confidence: low. What we have is one piece of outside evidence pointing one way — **a competitor's real, published report puts the transaction tax inside the apportionable figure and leaves the legal fees out**, deliberately, on the face of the report, without explaining the line it draws between the two. We would rather have your reasoning than copy their line.

 **What it is worth.** The understatement is the tax divided by price-plus-tax — **about 4 to 5% of every purchase claim we will ever produce**, and it does not average out across a portfolio. On the competitor's own worked job it is 4.76%. On the example above it is £90,000 more consideration to apportion, on every purchase, for good.

 **A**** contradiction ****this ****qu****e****s****t****i****o****n**** u****s****ed ****t****o ****c****a****rry,**** ****no****w**** ****clos****e****d.** Until 12 September 2026 our client-facing wording told every client outside England that "acquisition costs added to the base" were their local transaction tax, while the arithmetic added nothing. **That sentence ****now follow****s ****the sw****i****tch**: off, it names the tax and says it is not added; on, it names it as what is added. You are answering the arithmetic only. (The buyer's own alteration costs question had the same shape and was closed the same day.)


 **Where the build has got to, so that you are answering a switch and not a proposal.** As of 7 September 2026 the first half of this is **built, tested and switched off**, exactly like the other prepared readings here. The amount is captured by a question the client only sees when the switch is on; a shared module adds it to the figure before apportionment on a purchase and only on a purchase; and it never estimates a tax, so if no amount is given nothing is added. **The second half is built ****to****o****,**** ****a****s ****of**** ****12**** ****Sep****t****emb****er ****2026**, behind its own switch, also off: a question the client only sees when that switch is on asks for the legal and other costs directly attributable to acquiring the property, and a stated amount is added on a purchase only. The two switches are independent, so you can answer the halves differently.


 **One thing about the name of the tax, since it is not the same tax everywhere.** It is Stamp Duty Land Tax in England and Northern Ireland, Land Transaction Tax in Wales and Land and Buildings Transaction Tax in Scotland. The engine already knew all four and resolves the name from the client's own answer, so a Scottish client is never asked about SDLT. Please answer for the transaction tax generally rather than for the English one, unless you think the answer actually differs between them — in which case that is worth a line of its own below.

 **Our recommendation.** Answer both halves explicitly rather than leaving either. Being in neither state is the problem: today we tell some clients one thing and compute another. If you confirm part one, we flip one boolean and every purchase claim rises by about 4 to 5%. If you reject it, we correct the client-facing wording instead — which is work that has to happen either way, and is the half a rejection does not remove.

 **What it unblocks.** The single largest systematic under-claim we know about. (The misstatement to clients outside England was removed on 12 September 2026.)


 **Note added 14 September 2026 by Phase 131.** Both parts of the answer box below have text in them, with no name, date or confidence. It has not been acted on, and both switches stay off. Who wrote it is asked of Lakhbir in section one, under _"Who wrote the answer under the stamp-duty question?"_. **If it is yours, please add your name, the date and your confidence. If it is not, please confirm or correct it as you would a draft.**

```
ANSWER
......................................................................
Part 1 - is the transaction tax inside the consideration we apportion?
The transaction tax (SDLT/LTT/LBTT, as applicable) is included in the consideration/capital cost being apportioned, 
to the extent attributable to the qualifying asset.

Part 2 - do legal and other incidental acquisition costs follow it in?
Legal and other incidental acquisition costs can follow it in where they are directly attributable to acquiring
 the qualifying asset and are capital expenditure for the relevant allowance. They should not all be automatically 
included simply because they were incurred as part of the purchase.

How confident are you?    High / Medium / Low

If you disagree with our recommendation, what should we do instead?

## When a grant-funded purchase is also capped at a past owner's figure, does the grant come off before the cap or after it?

 **The question.** A trading company buys a care home for £2,000,000, and a public body's grant pays for 40% of it. An earlier owner had claimed allowances on the fixtures, so what our client can claim on them is capped at the value fixed when that owner sold — here £120,000. Two things now reduce the claim: the grant, and the cap. **Does the grant come off the spend first, with the cap then applied to what is left — or is the cap applied first, with the grant's 40% then taken off the capped figure?**

 **What we think.** Grant first, then the cap — and that is what the system does today, in every sector. Money a public body contributed is not treated as spent by our client at all, so the grant reduces the spend; the cap is then a ceiling on what may be claimed out of that reduced spend. Read that way, while the reduced spend still sits above the ceiling, the grant changes nothing. Confidence: medium. The other reading — that the grant met its 40% share of every pound, including the pounds that sit under the ceiling — is not unreasonable, and it gives a smaller claim. **It is now a switch.** Since 10 September 2026 the other reading is built, tested both ways and switched off, in all five sectors: switched on, the cap is applied first and the grant's share comes off the capped figure, and the client is told so in a flag raised only where a cap actually bound. It asks the client nothing new.


 **An example.** On the facts above, care homes today: qualifying expenditure of £400,000–£800,000 without the grant, and £240,000–£480,000 with it. Both are well above the £120,000 ceiling, so the client is shown **£120,000 either way, and the grant changes nothing.** Read the other way, the claim is £120,000 less 40% — **£72,000**. At the tax rates the estimate uses, that is a saving of £22,800–£31,800 today against £13,680–£19,080 on the other reading. Dental, GP practices and private hospitals give the same £120,000 on these facts. Supported living's own figure is smaller, so the gap there is £75,000 against £72,000. The same question arises where a joint election fixed the value of the fixtures instead of an earlier sale.

 **Why it is worth your time even though it looks narrow.** It is the one question in this section where our current reading is the **favourable** one and the other reading takes money off the client. We found it while checking our own client-facing wording about grants, not because anybody raised it, and we would rather put it to you than keep a favourable order nobody has confirmed.

 **Our recommendation.** Confirm the order we use, or tell us it runs the other way. We would rather have it firmly than favourably: if you think today's order is arguable rather than safe, say so and we will switch to the other. **The other reading is already built and switched off**, so your answer is a one-line change rather than a proposal — though unlike most of the switches it would move live figures down the moment it is turned on, so we would agree the changeover with you. In supported living it combines with the grant-tracing question rather than replacing it: that one decides how much of a grant comes off, this one decides when.


 **What it unblocks.** The figure shown to every client, in every sector, who bought a grant-funded building that an earlier owner had already claimed on — and the switch that decides it.

 **One thing the switch deliberately does not reach, and it is a question in its own right.** Two other switched readings add spend **on top** of a capped figure: the buyer's own alterations needed to install the plant (ACC-A3 in FOR THE ACCOUNTANT) and installation and commissioning spend (ACC-A10). **Neither order takes a grant off that added spend** — today it is never grant-reduced, and the new switch leaves it alone so that it moves a figure only where a ceiling binds. Both of those readings are switched off, so nothing a client sees depends on this today. **If you confirm either of them, please also tell us whether a grant that funded those works should come off them** — on a £60,000 commissioning line funded 40% by grant, that is £24,000 in or out of the claim.

```
ANSWER
Before the cap
......................................................................
Does the grant come off before the cap, or after it?


How confident are you?    High / Medium / Low

Answered by:                                     Date:

If you disagree with our recommendation, what should we do instead?


......................................................................
```

 **Read 15 September 2026 (Phase 132c).** The box holds an unsigned answer: "Before the cap" — the grant comes off first, then the cap. That is the order the system uses today in every sector. It is not a tax adviser's answer, so it is recorded as a draft; nothing moves, and the other order stays switched off. Still needed: the tax adviser's confirmation that this order is safe rather than merely arguable.

## When the money was spent after the accounting year end a client gives us, can we roll that year end forward to find the return the claim belongs in?

 **The question.** Whether a claim can still reach a refund depends on whether the tax return for the year the money was spent in can still be amended. That is Rulebook A, section 4: the annual investment allowance and first-year allowances are made for the period in which the expenditure is incurred. We ask each client for their most recent accounting year end (or their next one) and for the date the purchase or the works completed, and we work out the year of spend from the two — the first year end on or after the completion date, on the same day and month. Where that year ends before the year end the client gave, we already use it. Where the money was spent after the year end they gave, we still use the year end they gave. May we roll it forward in that second case too?

 **What we think.** In law, yes: the period of expenditure is what counts, whichever side of the year end given it falls. What we cannot know is the facts. Rolling forward assumes the business kept twelve-month accounting years with the same year end through years the client has not told us about, and a client who gives a year end from long ago may simply have given the wrong one. Confidence: high on the law, medium on whether the dates can be trusted.

 **An example.** A company gives its year end as 31 March 2019 and says the purchase completed on 1 June 2025. Worked from 31 March 2019, the deadline to amend was 31 March 2021, so today it is out of time and the client is shown writing-down allowances over future years, with no refund. Rolled forward, the money was spent in the year to 31 March 2026, whose return can be amended until 31 March 2028 — in time, so the client would be shown a route that can include a refund of tax already paid. The relief itself is the same either way; what changes is whether it can come back as cash for an earlier year.

 **Our recommendation.** Leave it off until you confirm it. It can only make a claim look better, and the case where it matters is one where the client's own dates do not sit together. If you confirm the reading, we switch it on. If you would rather we asked the client to confirm their year end in that case instead, say so.

 **What it unblocks.** One switch, which decides whether a client who spent the money after the year end they gave us is shown a refund route. The rest of the correction — money spent before the year end given — is already live, because it can only close a window, never open one.

```
ANSWER
Roll the year end forward
......................................................................
Where the money was spent after the year end given, may we use the year it was spent in?
Yes  /  No  /  Only once the client has confirmed their year end

How confident are you?    High / Medium / Low

Answered by:                                     Date:

If you disagree with our recommendation, what should we do instead?


......................................................................
```

 **Read 15 September 2026 (Phase 132c).** The box holds an unsigned answer: "Roll the year end forward". That is the half of the correction that can only make a claim look better, so its switch stays off until a tax adviser confirms; the half that can only close a window is already live. Recorded as a draft. Still needed: the tax adviser's answer, or a preference to ask the client to confirm their year end instead.

## Does the cap on loss relief against other income apply to losses set against the same business's own profits?

 **Added 14 September 2026 by Phase 131.** It changes when a sole trader or partner gets relief, not how much in total, so it is here among the questions that change money.

 **The question.** Section 24A of the Income Tax Act 2007 caps certain reliefs an individual takes against general income, including a trade loss carried back. The cap is **£50,000 or, if more, 25% of adjusted total income**. Section 24A(7)(b), read on legislation.gov.uk on 14 September 2026, takes out of that cap any such relief "so far as made from profits of the trade or business to which the relief in question relates". Our rates rulebook and both personal tax summaries state the cap. **None applies the carve-out**, because it makes relief bigger. **Should they?**

 **What we think.** Confidence: **high on what the words say, medium on applying them without you**. A loss carried back and set against the previous year's profit from the same practice looks like exactly what (7)(b) removes from the cap. But a reading that enlarges relief is switched on only on your answer.

 **An example.** A dentist's 2026-27 claim creates a **£120,000** trade loss, carried back to 2025-26. In 2025-26 the practice made **£150,000**, and the dentist had **£40,000** of other income (not savings or dividends). Adjusted total income is **£190,000**, so the cap is the greater of £50,000 and £47,500: **£50,000**. Capping the whole deduction, £50,000 is relieved in 2025-26, taxable income falls to £140,000, and £70,000 is carried forward. With the carve-out, all £120,000 is set against the practice's £150,000, and taxable income falls to £70,000. The extra **£70,000** relieved now is worth about **£33,770** of 2025-26 income tax at rest-of-UK rates, in three slices: £14,860 at 45% (£6,687); £25,140 across the band where the personal allowance is withdrawn, at an effective 60% (£15,084); and £30,000 at 40% (£12,000). Class 4 National Insurance is ignored. Under the capped reading the £70,000 is not lost — it waits for later profits — so the difference is **when** the client is relieved.

 **Our recommendation.** Apply the carve-out as the statute reads, and show the capped line and the carved-out line separately, so a reader sees both. Leave it unapplied until you confirm.

 **What it unblocks.** The loss sections of the sole-trader and partnership tax implication summaries, the cap in the rates rulebook, and the open question on each of those three documents.

```
ANSWER
......................................................................
Does s.24A(7)(b) take relief against the same trade's profits out of the cap?    Yes / No - yes

Show capped and carved-out lines separately?                                     Yes / No - yes

How confident are you?    High / Medium / Low

Answered by:                                     Date:

If you disagree with our recommendation, what should we do instead?


......................................................................
```

 **Read 15 September 2026 (Phase 132c).** The box holds an unsigned answer: "yes" to both — relief against the same trade's profits is outside the cap, and the capped and carved-out lines are shown separately. It matches our recommendation, but it is not a tax adviser's answer, so the carve-out stays unapplied in the rates rulebook and both tax summaries, as recommended until confirmed. Recorded as a draft. Still needed: the tax adviser's answer.
