## In supported living, does a landlord's contribution come off before the common-parts share, or after it?

 **Added 15 September 2026 by Phase 132.** It changes how much a supported-living client can claim, and it can only make that claim smaller, so it is here among the questions that change money.

 **The question.** When a landlord pays towards the works, the other four sectors take that contribution off the qualifying spend (Capital Allowances Act 2001 s.532). Where the landlord and the occupier are connected, a separate switched reading decides what happens instead (s.536); that is asked above, under "When a landlord and tenant are connected, does a contribution reduce the claim?". Supported living has never asked whether a landlord contributed, and has never deducted one, so its figure today is gross of any contribution. Supported living is also the sector where we claim only a share of the building — the common parts, the shared areas outside the residents' own flats. **When a landlord has contributed, does the contribution come off the spend before that common-parts share is applied, or is the share applied first and the contribution taken off what is left?**

 **What we think.** Contribution first — or, which gives the same figure, the contribution spread evenly across the whole building. Where the contribution is not earmarked to any part of the works, that is the natural reading of s.532: the landlord's money met a share of every pound, and the common parts carry their share of it. It is also the order the care-homes deduction already uses. Confidence: **medium**. A contribution earmarked to the shared areas may be a different case, because then the landlord's money never met the flats at all. That is the same kind of point as in "If a grant paid for something we could never claim anyway, does it reduce anything?" — where a deduction lands, not whether it is made — and as the order question in "When a grant-funded purchase is also capped at a past owner's figure, does the grant come off before the cap or after it?".

 **It is now a switch.** On 14 September 2026 a landlord-contribution deduction was built for supported living and switched off. Switched on, the flow asks whether the landlord contributed to the works and what share, and a known share comes off the spend before the common-parts share is applied — the care-homes order. "Not sure", or no answer, deducts nothing. It can only make a claim smaller. It stays off because the order has never been put to an adviser, and no rulebook question asks it.

 **An example (illustrative).** A supported-living scheme spends **£400,000** on the works. The landlord contributed 30%, which is £120,000. The common-parts share the engine applies is 25%. **Contribution first:** £400,000 less £120,000 is £280,000, and 25% of that is **£70,000** of qualifying expenditure. **Common-parts share first, with the contribution treated as funding the whole building pro rata:** 25% of £400,000 is £100,000, less 30% of it (£30,000), which is **£70,000** — the same. **Common-parts share first, with the contribution earmarked to the common parts:** £100,000 less up to £120,000 is **nil**. So the order matters only where the contribution is not spread evenly across the building — and a landlord paying for the shared areas is exactly the case that creates. On these facts that is £70,000 of qualifying expenditure against nothing.

 **Our recommendation.** Confirm the pro-rata order for a contribution that is not earmarked, and tell us whether a contribution earmarked to the shared areas should come off the common-parts share directly. We would then switch the supported-living deduction on for the unearmarked case only, and add a question to the flow asking whether the landlord's contribution paid for the shared areas in particular.

 **What it unblocks.** Switching on the landlord-contribution deduction in supported living — the one sector that does not yet take a landlord's contribution off the claim — and the question in the client flow that would ask whether that contribution was earmarked to the shared areas.

```

......................................................................

How confident are you?    High / Medium / Low

Answered by:                                     Date:

......................................................................
```

## One thing we want to raise before you find it

 **Several of our corrections make claims bigger, not smaller, and we are flagging that deliberately rather than letting it surface later.**

 **Eight**** of the ****elev****en above are claim-increasing by direction** — seven of ten until the stamp-duty question joined them on 7 September 2026, and it is claim-increasing too. **A firm that only ever finds arguments in its own favour is a firm to be suspicious of, and we know that.** We would rather be told we are wrong about them than have it go unmentioned.


 One of them is different from the other six and you should know which. Six depend on a fact that no client question currently captures, so they are inert twice over — the switch is off _and_ nothing could feed the branch. **The grant-tracing one is not.** It keys off a field the questionnaire captures today, so it is the only one of the seven that would change a live client figure the moment its switch flipped.

## The other twenty-nine for the tax adviser — worth answering, but nothing stops without them


 These refine what we already do. Answer them at whatever pace suits, after the ten.

### Deadlines and timing

 **Where a client has already sold the property, does anything survive for them?**

 **Partnerships — confirm the amendment window and how the benefit reaches individual partners.**

 We understand the claim goes in the partnership return. **This is not a corner case for us** — GP and dental partnerships are a large part of the market.

 **Note added 14 September 2026 by Phase 131.** What we now read the law to say, so that you are confirming rather than researching. A partnership return can be amended **only by the partner who made and delivered it**, and **not more than twelve months after the filing date** (Taxes Management Act 1970 s.12ABA); HMRC then amends each partner's own return. Overpayment relief cannot rescue a claim that was never made, or expenditure never put in a pool, so the twelve months is the only way back into a past year. A partnership that still owns the property can still put the expenditure into its current return's pool: writing-down allowances from now, no refund for the old year. **An example.** A four-partner GP practice spent **£400,000** on a fit-out in 2024-25 and claimed nothing. Its filing date was **31 January 2026**, so the return can be amended until **31 January 2027**. If the practice comes to us in **February 2027**, the refund route is gone, but the £400,000 can still go into the **2026-27** pool if the practice still owns the property. **Please confirm four things:** (a) that never-pooled expenditure is outside overpayment relief (the question below this one is answered "shut", unsigned); (b) that one company partner moves the whole return onto the company deadline (s.12AA(5B) and (5C)); (c) that current-period pooling stays open after the window closes; and (d) whether the engagement letter should warn that repayments also reach former partners, in their profit shares for the year amended.

 **Note added 15 September 2026 by Phase 132 — what the engine now does while (b) is open.** All five sector flows now ask whether any partner or LLP member is a company. Where one is known to be, the engine uses the company deadline — the first anniversary of the period end plus twelve months, so 24 months — wherever that is **earlier** than the individual date, because that can only close a window: a 31 December 2024 year end closes on 31 December 2026, not 31 January 2027. Where the company date is **later** — period ends from 1 February to 5 April, so the usual 31 March year end among them: 31 March 2024 would stay open to 31 March 2026 rather than 31 January 2026 — it still uses the individual date; applying the company date there is built behind a switch, off, **and your answer to (b) decides it**. Where nobody knows whether a partner is a company, the window counts as open only if it is open on both dates. No figure has moved up.

 **Where the amendment window has closed and relief comes through writing-down allowances, can it still put cash back — through payments on account, or through a loss carried back?**

 **Added 12 September 2026 by Phase 129.** **The question.** Our house rule, from Rulebook A, is that "refund" language belongs only to amending a return inside its window and to an open enquiry. Where the window has closed and the client still owns the property, relief comes through writing-down allowances on the pool, and we say "reduced tax over time, not a refund". Two situations may put cash back on that route anyway, and the rule as written covers neither: **(a)** the lower bill lets an individual or partner **reduce payments on account** already made or due for the year; **(b)** the allowance creates or enlarges a **loss that is carried back** against the previous year's profits.

 **What we think.** Confidence: medium on (a), low on (b). (a) is less tax for the current year, not a refund of an earlier year's — so "less to pay this year, and instalments already paid may come back" is honest, and "a refund for past years" is not. (b) is a genuine repayment of an earlier year's tax, and the engine already treats it as one on this route. That is a reading nobody has confirmed.

 **An example.** (a) A sole-trader dentist has £28,000 of writing-down allowances (£200,000 on the pool at 14%). At 40% that is £11,200 less tax for the year, so they can claim to reduce the two payments on account for the year by £5,600 each — or have that money back if already paid. (b) A company with £10,000 of taxable profit claims the same £28,000, making an £18,000 loss; carried back one year against profits taxed at 25%, that is a **£4,500 repayment** of last year's tax.

 **Our recommendation.** Let (b) be called a repayment of an earlier year's tax, with the loss conditions stated. Describe (a) as a lower bill for the year that may reduce instalments, never as a refund. Tell us if either is wrong.

 **What it unblocks.** How the cash figure is described on that route in all five assessments and in the tax implication summary. If (b) is wrong, the engine's treatment of a carried-back loss on that route is corrected.

```
ANSWER
......................................................................
(a) Payments on account reduced:  a lower bill, not a refund?   Yes / No - Yes
(b) Loss carried back:  a repayment of an earlier year's tax?   Yes / No - Yes

How confident are you?    High / Medium / Low

Answered by:                                     Date:

If you disagree with our recommendation, what should we do instead?


......................................................................
```

 **Read 15 September 2026 (Phase 132c).** The box holds an unsigned answer: "Yes" to both — reduced payments on account are a lower bill, not a refund, and a carried-back loss is a repayment of an earlier year's tax. It matches our recommendation, but it is not a tax adviser's answer, so it is recorded as a draft and neither the wording nor the engine's treatment of a carried-back loss changes on it. Still needed: the tax adviser's answer.

 **In a Scottish sale, which date starts our clocks — conclusion of missives, the date of entry, or registration of the disposition?**

 **Added 14 September 2026 by Phase 131.** Scotland is not served, and nothing is blocked until the question about Scotland's trigger is answered. **The question.** Our fixed-value clocks and the two-year window for a joint election are anchored to exchange and completion. A Scottish transaction has no exchange or completion: it turns on **missives**, a **disposition** and a **date of entry**. Which of these is "the time of the transaction" for those clocks? The jurisdiction matrix put this to a tax adviser on 2 September 2026, and it was never written into this document.

 **What we think.** Confidence: **low**. The Capital Allowances Act carries its own Scottish wording for interests in land, so the rules themselves apply in Scotland without a separate rulebook. But the dates our clocks run from are written in English conveyancing terms, and we have not defined their Scottish equivalents. This is a question about which date the statute bites on, and that is tax advice, not a build task.

 **An example, with illustrative dates.** An Edinburgh care home is bought. Missives are concluded on **10 March 2028**, the date of entry is **1 May 2028**, and the disposition is registered on **14 August 2028**. Depending on which date starts the two-year window, it closes on **10 March, 1 May or 14 August 2030** — a spread of five months in which a client would be told either "in time" or "out of time".

 **Our recommendation.** Tell us which date. If it depends on something, such as missives concluded subject to a condition, say what it depends on. Until then, a Scottish purchase date is treated as not established.

 **What it unblocks.** The clocks for a Scottish client, and the one rulebook question in the jurisdiction matrix's sizing of Scotland.

 **When a landlord grants a new lease for a premium and no joint election is made, which item of the disposal table applies — and is a section 198 election available at all?**

 **Added 12 September 2026 by Phase 129**, found while checking a gloss in our own rulebook against the Act. **The question.** Two of our rulebooks disagree about what item 9 of the section 196 Table is, and our software follows the wrong one. One says item 9 is a tenant paying a premium for a new lease with no joint section 183 election, and on that basis says a section 198 election is available on a lease. The Act, read on 12 September 2026, says item 9 is **"Permanent discontinuance of the qualifying activity followed by the sale of the qualifying interest"**, and section 198(1) applies only to items 1 and 9 — both sales. The lease reading appears to come from HMRC's manual at CA26800, which says a section 198 election can be made where an incoming lessee has paid a premium at or above market value, **without naming any Table item**.

 **What we think.** Confidence: **high** that item 9 is not a lease premium — the Table says so in terms. **Low** on the part that matters: what, if anything, carries CA26800's lease case into section 198. We cannot find the route in the section text, and we would rather ask than guess.

 **A worked example.** A landlord owns a care home freehold and has claimed allowances on its fixtures. It grants a 125-year lease to an operator for a premium of £1,200,000, at market value. No joint section 183 election is made. **Our software today** calls this item 9, starts a two-year fixed-value clock from the grant, and tells the operator a section 198 election is available. **On the section text** the landlord has not sold its interest and no election has moved ownership of the fixtures to the operator, so no Table item seems to be triggered by the grant — and section 198, which needs item 1 or 9, does not seem to be available.

 **Our recommendation.** Correct both rulebooks and the software to the Table — item 9 is a discontinuance followed by a sale, sitting with item 1 under section 198 — and treat a lease granted for a premium with no joint election as **"route not established"** until you tell us what CA26800's lease case rests on. The correction is being built switched off, so nothing changes for a client until you answer.

 **What it unblocks.** Which statutory route the software offers a tenant who paid a premium for a new lease; which date starts the two-year clock on those facts; and two rows of our elections rulebook.

```

......................................................................
(a) Is item 9 of the s.196 Table ever a lease premium?          Yes / No - No
(b) On a new lease granted for a premium with no joint s.183
    election, which Table item applies, if any?    Do not assign item 9. The applicable statutory route needs to be 
confirmed against the incoming-lessee provisions (particularly s.183/s.184) and HMRC's CA26800 guidance.....................
(c) Is a s.198 election available on those facts, and under
    what provision?         HMRC guidance says yes, but the precise statutory route should be confirmed by the
 qualified tax adviser before the software is switched on.                       ....................

How confident are you?    High / Medium / Low

Answered by:                                     Date:

If you disagree with our recommendation, what should we do instead?


......................................................................
```

### Three that are judgement rather than law


 **Where ownership history cannot be established, how far back is it reasonable to look before we treat the position as unknowable?**

 **We want a written stopping point we can apply consistently** — a number of years, or a test — not a case-by-case judgement. Consistency is the control here.

```
ANSWER

search the available ownership/title history back far enough to establish the relevant acquisition and disposal 
chain needed for the capital allowances rules. If that chain still cannot be established after all reasonably 
available sources have been checked, mark the history “unable to establish” and escalate rather than assuming it 
is unknowable after a fixed period.......................................................................
Your answer:

Answered by:                                     Date:

If you disagree with our recommendation, what should we do instead?


...................................................................... 
```

 This is about what a client should be shown rather than what the law says, **so your instinct as a practitioner is the answer we want.**

 figure entirely. The report should clearly label it as an indicative/unconfirmed range, explain what causes the

 **Read 15 September 2026 (Phase 132c).** The box holds an unsigned answer: where the position is unknown, show a range with a stated floor of nil, labelled as indicative, explaining the uncertainty, and never presenting the upper end as what the client is entitled to. It is not a tax adviser's answer, so it is recorded as a draft and nothing changes. Still needed: the tax adviser's answer.

 **Is "roughly break-even" enough profit for the relief to bite on?**

 **The question.** The first six screens of our assessment ask a client how the business is doing this year, and offer four answers: profitable, loss-making, roughly break-even, and not sure. Our engine has a coarser question with three: comfortably profitable, "no — around break-even, or loss-making", and not sure. **When a client picks "roughly break-even" on the short form, which of the engine's three answers is that?**

 **What we think.** Confidence: low, and it is a judgement about a business rather than a reading of a statute, which is why it is here rather than above. Our own engine's wording bundles break-even with loss-making, which points at "no". Against that, the short form deliberately offers break-even as a separate option beside loss-making, so a client who picked it was distinguishing itself from a loss — and "no" makes us print "insufficient taxable profit confirmed", which is more than a break-even business has confirmed.

 **An example.** A dental practice with £4,000 of taxable profit is handed a £180,000 allowance. Read as "no", we say the profit gate is shut, show no near-term cash figure and point at the loss carry-back route. Read as "not sure", we say the timing is not established and keep asking the accountant. The entitlement is the same on both readings and the client's own figure does not move; what changes is what we tell them about when the money arrives, and whether we go on asking them a question they have already answered.

 **Our recommendation.** Tell us which of the three it is, and if the answer is "it depends on the size of the deduction" say so — that is a real answer and we would build the comparison rather than the constant. Until you do, the build treats it as **not answered at all**, which behaves exactly like "not sure": nothing is asserted, no gate opens, and the question stays on our own missing-facts list. That is the safe reading and it is deliberately the one that costs us work rather than the one that costs the client accuracy.

 **What it unblocks.** Nothing stops without it. It decides whether a client who told us they are roughly break-even goes on being asked about their profits by our staff — so it is a question about how much we pester people, and about one line of a mapping that is otherwise settled.

 **Now a switch — 10 September 2026.** The reading this question points at — break-even counts as not enough taxable profit — is built in the back end behind a switch that ships off, with both positions tested, so your answer becomes a one-line change rather than a build; until then nothing changes.

```
ANSWER
......................................................................
Which of the three is "roughly break-even"?    Yes / No / Not sure - Not sure

Does it depend on the size of the deduction? - Yes


How confident are you?    High / Medium / Low

Answered by:                                     Date:

If you disagree with our recommendation, what should we do instead?


...................................................................... 
```

 **Read 15 September 2026 (Phase 132c).** The box holds an unsigned answer: "Not sure" which of the three, and "Yes", it depends on the size of the deduction. We said that is a real answer and would mean building a comparison rather than a constant. But it is not a tax adviser's answer, so nothing is built and "roughly break-even" stays treated as not answered, with its switch off. Still needed: the tax adviser's answer.

# Section three — for the accountant

 **Twelve questions for you and one for whoever does your own compliance, and none of them blocks anything.** This is preparation for the referral channel, which is meant to be the main way clients reach us. Until now the only accountant-facing document ran the other way: it told them what we do. **Nothing asked them anything.** 

 **Two honest caveats.** These have not been tested on a practising accountant — they are a first draft, and the first accountant you show them to will improve them. And **the second question below cannot be asked until the fee question in the first section is settled**, because what you are offering changes the answer.

### Does the channel work at all — the four that decide it

 **Would you refer a client to us, and what would stop you?**
The blunt opener, asked first and asked cold. **Do not lead with the pitch.** The reasons an accountant will not refer are usually specific and unglamorous, and they are the only thing worth knowing on a first call.

......................................................................

 **Do you want a referral fee — and what does your professional body require you to tell the client?**
Members of the accountancy bodies generally have to disclose commission and referral arrangements to their client. **So this is not only a commercial question: it decides what has to appear in writing in front of the client, and in whose document.** Some firms will prefer no fee at all precisely so that the disclosure conversation never arises. Ask which they are and take the answer at face value.

### And one to ask whoever does your own compliance

 **Do we need anti-money-laundering supervision to do this?**
Capital allowances advice provided by way of business may fall within the definition of a tax adviser for money-laundering purposes, and a tax adviser must be supervised. **Operating unsupervised where supervision is required is a criminal offence rather than a compliance gap**, so this is worth a direct answer rather than an assumption. Our own reading is that it probably does apply.

supervised.....................................................................

# Section four — for the chartered surveyor

 **Eleven questions decide something we cannot work around. Fourteen more refine what we already do.** You can answer all of this cold; you do not need to read anything else first. **The seventh was added on 5 September 2026**, and is now the eighth — whether we are counting site overheads twice — and it was not new, it was buried inside a question about which price books to buy, where it was easy to answer the first half and never reach it. **The eighth was added on 7 September 2026**, and is now the ninth — our own worked example states the same building's rebuild cost twice, 9.6% apart, and the corroboration we claim in that pack is computed off the smaller of the two. **The ninth and tenth were added on 9 September 2026**, and are now the tenth and eleventh — whether our five sector percentages are anywhere near right, and what counts as too far apart when we test ourselves against a real claim. Neither is new either: both were written down in June, in a document that was superseded before anybody was asked, and they were found when it was finally reconciled line by line.


 **We are not asking you to build this with us.** It is built. We are asking you to look at what we built and either say "that's sound" or "that bit is wrong, here's why". **You are being asked to check work, not to do it** — which is what should make this affordable.

 **One thing we do that may seem odd.** When we ask you to price a job, we will give you the building, the room list and the method, and we will **deliberately withhold the number our own system produced** until you have given us yours. That is not us being cagey. It is because a pre-filled number anchors the person checking it, and the only honest evidence we will ever have about whether our estimates are any good is the gap between yours and ours before you have seen ours. **The system enforces it structurally — our figures are not in the data your session receives, not merely hidden on screen.**

 **Two things have changed since this pack was written, and both help you.**

 First, the director answered nine of the eighteen himself on 3 September. Those answers are recorded below. **Please answer as if they were not there.** Several of them answer a slightly different question from the one asked, and we would rather say so than quietly bank them.

 Second — **a complete real client deliverable from a trading competitor came into our hands on 3 September** and it answers three of our hardest questions outright, with a worked example. We are not treating it as authority: it is one report, on a holiday let rather than a healthcare property, and no figure in it is a rate we would adopt. **What we want from you is whether one firm's practice is the profession's practice.** That turns three "what should we do" questions into "is this right", which should be faster for you and is certainly sharper.

---

## Can you value a building you have not stood in?

 **The big one. Everything else hangs off it.**

 **The question.** Where does a desk estimate stop being defensible? Is there a size of building above which somebody has to attend, a complexity threshold, or a category of plant you would never accept a figure for unseen?

 **The director's provisional view:** _"Yes, this is a desktop valuation/survey."_ **That is our commercial intent, not an answer.** The question is about **your** liability, not our preference.

 **Why it matters more than it looks.** We have **no minimum job size** — that was decided on 1 September — so **there is no site visit to fall back on.** If a desk estimate is defensible, a job costs an hour and we can serve the whole country. If it is not, every job carries a travel day and the small end of the market disappears.

 **Our recommendation.** Answer it as a liability question and give us a threshold we can code — a floor area, a building type, or a named category of plant. **"It depends on the building" is true and unusable.** If you would never sign a desk-only valuation, say that plainly; it is a real answer and it tells us we have one product rather than two.

 **What it unblocks.** Whether there is a cheap desk-only product at all, the minimum job size, and the pricing of every job we take.

```
ANSWER
A desktop valuation can be defensible for straightforward, well-documented properties where the available 
plans, photographs, room schedules, specifications and cost information are sufficient to identify and value 
the relevant assets. There should not be an arbitrary floor-area threshold on its own. However, complex properties, 
unusual specialist plant, materially incomplete evidence, or circumstances where condition/installation cannot 
reasonably be established remotely should require a site inspection before a surveyor signs off the valuation.......................................................................
Your answer:


How confident are you?    High / Medium / Low

Answered by:                                     Date:

If you disagree with our recommendation, what should we do instead?


...................................................................... 
```

 **Read 15 September 2026 (Phase 132c).** The box holds an unsigned answer: a desktop valuation can be defensible for straightforward, well-documented buildings, with no floor-area threshold on its own, while complex or poorly evidenced ones need a visit. The question asked for a threshold we can code, and this gives none. It is not the surveyor's answer, so it is recorded as a partial draft and nothing changes. Still needed from the surveyor: a codeable threshold, or "never desk-only".

## When can a video walkthrough replace a visit?

 **The question.** What would you need to see, in what order? What can a video never show — things behind panels, above ceilings, things you have to touch? Would you sign off a valuation resting partly on one, and with what caveat?

 **The director's provisional view:** _"yes"_ — recorded, but the substance is what we need.

 **Our recommendation.** If the answer is yes, tell us what the capture script has to contain and we will build it into the client's instructions. **"I would not sign off on one" is a real and useful answer** — it would settle the question rather than leaving it half-open, which is where it has been for a month.

 **One thing to know.** We removed video from the client-facing booking screen because it was being offered as a sales channel. **If video works for survey purposes it comes back as a post-engagement step against your diary**, not as a way for a prospect to book. Different screen, different calendar, different owner.

 **What it unblocks.** The smallest client we can take, and whether we serve the country or a region.

```
ANSWER

is prepared to rely on video, the capture must follow a defined script and show the relevant areas, access points, 
plant, services and installations in sufficient detail to identify and assess them. It should also record areas 
that cannot be inspected, including concealed or inaccessible items.

The surveyor should decide whether the video evidence is sufficient for each property and retain the right to 
require a physical visit where the evidence is inadequate. Any valuation relying materially on video should state 
that limitation and the extent of the inspection/evidence relied upon......................................................................

## Should the site-visit decision be sized on our own estimate, which you may not see, or on the price paid, which you do?

 **Added 12 September 2026 by Phase 129.** Found while writing the instruction sheet that goes with our draft policy on when a video walkthrough can replace a visit.

 **The question.** The draft policy decides how much evidence a job needs — desktop only, desktop with video, desktop with video and targeted capture, or a site visit — from two things: **the value at stake** and **how complex the job is**. The value is measured on **our own first estimate of qualifying expenditure**. But that estimate is withheld from you until you have committed your own figures, so that your numbers are independent of ours. **Those two rules pull against each other**, and we would rather you chose the way out than we did.

 **What we think.** Confidence: **high** that the conflict is real; **low** on the right answer. As a stopgap the draft now sends you only the basis a job is instructed on — never the value band, the complexity score or the estimate. That keeps the estimate from you, but it is imperfect: a simple job sent for a site visit is probably a large one, so the basis still hints at the value, and you cannot run the grid yourself without the band.

 **A worked example, on our illustrative care home.** Bought for £1,850,000. Our first estimate of qualifying expenditure is about £621,000, and you do not see it. Suppose the value bands were set at £250,000 and £750,000 — illustrative only, the real thresholds are yours. On our estimate the job sits in the middle band, and as a purchase apportionment it is complex, so the grid says **desktop with video and targeted capture**. That is all you receive. **Measured on the price paid instead** — £1,850,000, which is on the title you already hold — the band is read against thresholds set on price, nothing withheld feeds the decision, and you can apply the grid yourself.

 **Our recommendation.** Measure the value axis on **the price paid for a purchase, or the invoice total for a fit-out** — both already released to you — and set the thresholds on that basis. It removes the conflict entirely. What it costs: price is a blunter measure of what is at stake than qualifying expenditure, because the share of a price that qualifies varies by sector and by building. If you would rather keep our estimate, the stopgap stands and you receive the basis only — and Lakhbir, who owns the rule that keeps our estimate from you, confirms that the hint it leaves is acceptable.

 **What it unblocks.** Whether you can apply the site-visit grid yourself, and whether an instruction can tell you the tier rather than only the evidential basis.

```
ANSWER
......................................................................
Your answer:

Measure the value axis on:
   our estimate (you receive the basis only)  /  price paid or invoice - Price paid or invoice
   total  /  no value axis - complexity alone decides

How confident are you?    High / Medium / Low

Answered by:                                     Date:

If you disagree with our recommendation, what should we do instead?


......................................................................
```

 **Read 15 September 2026 (Phase 132c).** The box holds an unsigned answer: measure the value axis on the price paid or the invoice total, our recommendation. It is not the surveyor's answer, so it is recorded as a draft; the stopgap stays, and the surveyor still receives only the basis a job is instructed on. Still needed: the surveyor's answer, and the thresholds set on that basis.

## Which valuation basis should we be using?

 **The question.** Rebuild cost today, open market value, or worth to the person using it? **The same building has materially different values depending on which you pick, and the choice sets the denominator that every percentage in our reports is divided by.**

 **The director's provisional view:** _"we would never make a capital allowance claim based on the structure of the property itself however more the fixtures and fittings and the price those items and equipment was purchased for."_ **That describes what we claim on, not the basis on which the building and land are valued.** Different question.

 **The competitor report answers it, in two halves, both printed on the face of the document:**

-  **Plant and building — replacement cost.** "the estimated cost of replacing the building and external works if work has commenced at the appropriate time to have the building available for occupation at the date of purchase"

-  **Bare site — open market value**, "taken at the date of purchase"

 The formula, attributed to the Valuation Office, is `PC × (A1+A2) / (A1+A2+B+C)` where A1 and A2 are main and special-rate plant at replacement cost, B is the building excluding qualifying plant at replacement cost, and C is the bare site at open market value.

 **Three things we would most like you to confirm:**

1.  **Is that the basis you would expect?** Replacement cost for A and B, open market value for C.

2.  **Two different bases in one formula.** A and B are costs; C is a market value. They are not commensurable quantities, and the formula divides one by a sum containing the other. **Is that genuinely the convention, or an artefact of one firm's practice? If it is wrong, it is wrong in the denominator of everything we produce.**

3.  **Depreciation.** Their report shows **no** obsolescence or age deduction — replacement cost, undepreciated. Ours defaults to _depreciated_ replacement cost, on the reasoning that it is the harder basis to attack. **Which would you expect, and does it differ for a bought building against a self-built one?**

 **Our recommendation.** We have built it so the basis can be switched, and we have deliberately not switched it. **This is not a price-book question — basis first, then rates.**

 **What it unblocks.** Every percentage in every report we produce.

 **Note added 14 September 2026 by Phase 131.** Point 3 above says "Ours defaults to _depreciated_ replacement cost", and that needs correcting. The basis label does default to depreciated replacement cost. But **both depreciation methods ship switched off, with the method set to none**, so today every figure we produce is **replacement cost new, undepreciated** — the same as the competitor's. Which basis to use is the question, and nothing has been chosen.

```
ANSWER
.Use replacement cost for the building and qualifying plant, with the bare site valued separately at open 
market value, subject to the surveyor confirming that this is the appropriate valuation convention for the particular 
capital-allowances exercise. The mixed basis is not automatically wrong simply because the inputs are different 
types of value; the surveyor should confirm the methodology and calculation.

For depreciation, do not switch on any depreciation method yet. Use replacement cost new/undepreciated as the 
current neutral position until the chartered surveyor confirms whether depreciation is appropriate, what method 
should be used, and whether the treatment differs between acquired and self-built properties......................................................................
Your answer:


How confident are you?    High / Medium / Low

Answered by:                                     Date:

If you disagree with our recommendation, what should we do instead?


...................................................................... 
```

 **Read 15 September 2026 (Phase 132c).** The box holds an unsigned answer: replacement cost for the building and plant, the bare site at open market value, subject to the surveyor, and no depreciation until the surveyor says. That matches today, where both depreciation methods are off. It leaves the convention and depreciation to the surveyor, so it is recorded as a partial draft and nothing is switched. Still needed: the surveyor's answer on all three points.

## Where exactly is the line around someone's home?

 **The question.** A resident's flat is their home and nothing inside it counts. A shared lounge probably is not their home. **A corridor serving only flats — we genuinely do not know.** And separately: do you measure the split, or apportion it by floor area?

 **The director's provisional view:** _"Carehome and supported living you can claim for everything, you do not have to restrict where you cannot claim for rooms, this rule only applies for private HMOs and we do not accommodate them."_

 **⚠ We have flagged that rather than acting on it, and you should know why.** It is broader than the question, and **it contradicts a correction we shipped on 20 August**, which removed shared lounges, kitchens and resident bathrooms from the claimable base on the strength of HMRC's published guidance. That correction went in because the wider position had been assessed as an over-claim — in the sector our own risk register names as our **highest mis-selling exposure**. The system still applies the narrow position.

 **Our own notes call this the single biggest risk in a care-home claim and record that we have not decided it. We are not going to decide it.**

 **Our recommendation.** Take these four separately, because we think they differ: **a shared lounge · a corridor serving only flats · a communal laundry · a staff office.** And tell us whether to measure or apportion — and if that depends on job size, roughly where the threshold sits, so we can build it into scoping and pricing.

 **If you think the wider reading is right and our August correction was wrong, say so plainly.** That is a real answer and it is the one that would move the most money.

 **What it unblocks.** The claimable base in two of our five sectors, and the wording clients read.

## Emergency lighting, and six other things we could not place

 **The question.** Seven items we expect in these buildings and could not fit anywhere in our own classification: **emergency lighting**, commercial kitchen extract, clinical flooring, security glazing at reception, drug storage outside a dispensary, standby generators outside care homes, and the ventilation family above.

 **This is a classification question, not an entitlement question.** For each item: is it a separate thing, or is it already inside something else on our list?

 **The director's provisional view:** _"We can claim for all fixtures and fittings, essentially whatever is not fixed in we can claim for — imagine turning a room upside down and shaking it, what falls out we can't claim."_

 Two problems with using that, and we would rather raise them than work around them. **First, the shake test as stated is inverted relative to how capital allowances actually work** — loose chattels fall out and generally _are_ claimable, while it is fixtures, which do not fall out, that the fixtures regime exists to bring into a claim at all. We are not asking you to referee the wording; we are flagging that it does not give us a rule we can code. **Second, it answers entitlement rather than classification**, which was the question.

 **Emergency lighting first, please.** It is in every building we will ever look at, and depending on the answer it is relieved at **14% or at 6%**. Then one line each on the other six. **All seven are currently marked in our system as guesses.**

 **What it unblocks.** Seven rows in the fixture taxonomy that are flagged as unverified today.

```
ANSWER
Treat the seven items as separate classification questions, not one catch-all category. In particular, 
emergency lighting should be classified as part of the building’s electrical/lighting system, which is an integral 
feature under CAA 2001 s33A, rather than creating a separate 14% category. HMRC confirms that an electrical system, 
including a lighting system, is an integral feature.

For the other six, do not automatically group them together:

Commercial kitchen extract: powered ventilation — assess as an integral feature, subject to the precise system/expenditure.
Clinical flooring: normally building/flooring unless it forms part of a qualifying integral-feature system.
Security glazing at reception: assess separately; do not automatically classify as plant.
Drug storage outside a dispensary: assess according to the actual storage equipment/system, not simply because it is used for drugs.
Standby generators outside care homes: separate plant item; assess independently from the building's electrical system.
Ventilation: keep as a separate family and apply the specific ventilation classification already being developed.......................................................................
Your answer:


How confident are you?    High / Medium / Low

Answered by:                                     Date:

If you disagree with our recommendation, what should we do instead?


...................................................................... 
```

 **Read 15 September 2026 (Phase 132c).** The box holds an unsigned answer: emergency lighting belongs to the lighting system, an integral feature, not a separate 14% item; kitchen extract as ventilation; clinical flooring normally building; security glazing, drug storage and standby generators assessed on their own; ventilation kept separate. It is not the surveyor's answer, so the seven taxonomy rows stay flagged as unverified. Recorded as a draft. Still needed: the surveyor's answer.

## Are we counting site overheads twice in every job?

 **Added 5 September 2026.** This was already being asked — but only in the second half of a question about whether to buy a set of price books, where it is easy to answer the first half and never reach it. **It is a bigger question than the one it was buried in, and it decides whether a number in every job we produce is wrong.** It is asked here on its own.

 **The question.** When we build up a replacement cost, we add a flat percentage for preliminaries — site overheads, the cost of running the job rather than the materials in it. **Different cost sources treat preliminaries differently. A published building-cost index conventionally holds them OUTSIDE the unit rate. A Valuation Office replacement cost holds them INSIDE it.** So whether our uplift is correct, or is a double count, depends on which source the underlying rate came from — **and we apply the uplift regardless.**

 **What we think.** Confidence: **low, and deliberately so** — this is a convention we cannot settle from inside the business. Our own working note already suspects it is a double count where the base rate came from a published index, and separately suspects that a flat percentage is the wrong instrument and that a scale banded by job size would be more defensible.

 **An example, with the real number.** Our uplift is **18%**, applied flat to the whole build-up. On a £400,000 reconstruction cost that is £72,000 added. **If the base rate already contained preliminaries, that £72,000 is invented — in every sector, on every job, in the same direction: upwards.** It is not a rounding matter and it is not confined to one sector.

 **Our recommendation.** Tell us the convention, in three parts. **(a)** Where the base rate came from a published cost index, should we add a preliminaries uplift? **(b)** Where it came from a Valuation Office replacement cost, should we not? **(c)** Is a flat percentage the right instrument at all, or should it band with job size — and if so, roughly where do the bands fall? **If the answer is that it is a double count, we remove it and every estimate we produce comes down.** That is a correction that makes claims **smaller**, it needs nobody's permission but yours, and we would rather make it than defend it.

 **What it unblocks.** The accuracy of every cost build-up we produce. **The gate that removes the uplift is already built and switched off, so your answer is a one-line switch rather than a piece of work** — and since 12 September 2026 the board item says so, waiting on your signature rather than marked as blocked. It sat for a while behind a data-licensing decision it does not depend on: the licence question is about which price book to buy, and this question is about how to use whichever one we end up with.


```
ANSWER
......................................................................
Your answer:

(a) Uplift on a published-index rate?          Yes / No - Yes — only if the published rate expressly excludes 
preliminaries.
(b) Uplift on a Valuation Office replacement cost?   Yes / No - No, if the VO replacement-cost figure already 
incorporates preliminaries.
(c) Flat percentage, or banded by job size? with the bands and percentages set/validated by the chartered surveyor
 rather than using a blanket 18%...........................


How confident are you?    High / Medium / Low

Answered by:                                     Date:

If you disagree with our recommendation, what should we do instead?


...................................................................... 
```

 **Read 15 September 2026 (Phase 132c).** The box holds an unsigned answer: (a) add preliminaries to a published-index rate only if that rate excludes them; (b) add none to a Valuation Office figure that includes them; (c) band by job size, with bands set by the surveyor. (c) gives no bands. It is not the surveyor's answer, so the 18% uplift and its switched-off gate stay as they are. Recorded as a partial draft. Still needed: the surveyor's answer.

## Our rebuild cost is 8.9% above an insurance reinstatement figure — what has to be made like-for-like first, and is that corroboration?


 **Narrowed 12 September 2026 by Phase 129.** This question used to ask which of two rebuild costs in our specification was right. **We traced both before asking, and that half no longer needs you.** The element build-up sums exactly to **£2,841,400** — mechanical £412,000, electrical £348,000, lifts £96,000, sanitary £128,000, fittings £164,000, fire alarm and emergency lighting £61,400, frame £486,000, envelope £604,000, finishes £330,000, external works £212,000 — and it is the figure our apportionment actually uses. The **£2,567,720** has no working anywhere in our files: it is exactly £2,232,800 plus a 15% fee uplift, on a base that appears nowhere else, from a version of the build-up that has since been corrected. **So £2,841,400 stands and £2,567,720 is a leftover we are removing.** Every figure in this example, the insurance figure included, is illustrative — it is not a real building.

 **The question.** When we check our replacement-cost build-up against an insurance reinstatement valuation, **what has to be put on the same basis before the two are compared, and how far apart can they then be before the check stops being corroboration?**

 **What we think.** Confidence: **low**. As our pack compares them, the two are not like-for-like. The insurance figure includes demolition and professional fees; ours includes neither. The insurance figure is dated 2023; our build-up uses 2026 rates. We suspect the right comparison adjusts for all three before a percentage means anything, and we do not know where the line then sits.

 **A worked example, with the real numbers.** Insurance reinstatement valuation **£2,610,000** (2023, includes demolition and fees). Our build-up **£2,841,400** (2026 rates, no fees, no demolition). As printed, ours is **8.9% higher** (£231,400). Put back the 15% fee uplift our model used to carry and ours becomes **£3,267,610 — 25.2% higher**, before demolition is added at all. **The same check reads as close or as far apart depending entirely on the basis**, and until this week the pack printed 1.6%, computed off the leftover figure.

 **Our recommendation.** **(a)** Tell us the adjustments to make before comparing — fees, demolition, date, and anything else. **(b)** Tell us the divergence, after those adjustments, beyond which the check is no longer corroboration. **(c)** Until you answer, we take the word "corroborates" out of the pack and print the raw divergence with the basis differences stated beside it. That makes our claim smaller, not bigger, and needs nobody's permission.

 **What it unblocks.** Whether the only independent check on our own cost build-up means anything. It belongs in the same conversation as _"What counts as too far apart when we test ourselves against a real claim?"_

                           anything else: .................................

After those adjustments, the check stops being corroboration
beyond:  +/- .10...%

## Are our five sector percentages anywhere near right?

 **Added 9 September 2026.** Found while reconciling a June document that everyone had stopped reading. **It is now the tenth question in this section that decides something rather than refines it (the ninth when it was added), and it may be the one that matters most, because it is the only number a client ever actually sees.**


 **What we think. Confidence: low, and we want to be very clear that it is low.** For each sector we hold a percentage range, and we multiply the client's spend by it to produce the figure on the screen. They are:

|  Sector |  On a purchase |  On a new build |  On a refurbishment or fit-out |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
|  Care homes |  20% to 40% |  25% to 35% |  60% to 90% |
|  Dental |  15% to 30% |  30% to 40% |  50% to 90% |
|  GP and primary care |  15% to 30% |  30% to 40% |  40% to 70% |
|  Private hospitals and clinics |  25% to 45% |  35% to 45% |  50% to 85% |
|  Supported living |  10% to 25% |  10% to 25% |  10% to 25% |

 **Where they came from, said plainly: us.** They are our own reading of how much of a building's cost is typically plant and fixtures in each sector. **They have never been checked against a real completed project.** The software that holds them says so about itself, in terms — it calls them _assessed bands, not independently calibrated_ — and that qualifier travels with every figure they produce. **But the qualifier is ours, and it is not a substitute for somebody who has actually done these jobs telling us whether the numbers are sane.**

 **An example, with the real arithmetic.** A care home is bought for £3,000,000. We tell the client their qualifying expenditure is somewhere between **£600,000 and £1,200,000** — that is the 20% to 40% band, and it is the first number they ever see from us. At a 25% tax rate that is the difference between about £150,000 and £300,000 of relief. **If the true band for care-home purchases is 15% to 30%, every figure we have ever put on a screen is a third too high, and we will have been telling people so since launch.** If it is 30% to 50%, we are quietly losing clients who look at our number and decide it is not worth the trouble.

 **Our recommendation. Tell us the two numbers per sector, or tell us the shape is wrong.** We are not asking for precision. **A band you would defend is worth more to us than a point estimate you would not**, and "the purchase band for care homes is far too wide to be useful" is a completely acceptable answer — it would tell us the band needs to be conditioned on something we are not currently asking the client about.

 **Three things worth knowing before you answer.** **(a)** The refurbishment bands are much higher than the purchase bands because a fit-out is mostly plant, where a purchase is mostly building. Confirm that reasoning as well as the numbers. **(b)** Supported living is the same band on all three triggers, which is almost certainly wrong in shape rather than in level — nobody has separated them. **(c)** These are used at the very front of the funnel, before any survey, on nothing but a sector, a trigger and a spend figure. **They are a screening estimate and they are labelled as one.** The question is only whether the screen is honest.

 **What we deliberately have not done.** We have not tuned them to match the one competitor deliverable we hold. Fitting our own bands to a single outside job would look like corroboration and would be nothing of the kind.

 **What it unblocks.** Nothing is technically waiting on it — which is exactly why it has sat unasked since June. **What it changes is whether the number on the front page of our website is defensible**, and that is not a small thing to be unsure about at launch.

```
ANSWER
.The shape is wrong, and here is why: sector and trigger alone are not sufficient to produce a defensible 
qualifying-expenditure percentage. Purchase, new-build and refurbishment/fit-out need to be conditioned on factors 
such as building type, specification, specialist clinical equipment, extent of M&E, fit-out scope, and whether the
 expenditure is actually qualifying plant/fixtures rather than building expenditure. HMRC's rules are asset-based
 rather than sector-percentage based.

In particular, the current assumption that refurbishment is inherently 50–90% qualifying is too broad to use as an 
unqualified screening rule, and supported living should not use the same percentage for purchase, new build and 
refurbishment without further segmentation.

Do not replace the existing bands with another set of invented percentages. Have the chartered surveyor provide 
evidence-based indicative ranges, ideally split into meaningful property/specification sub-types. Until then, label 
the front-end result as an uncalibrated screening estimate and do not present it as a defensible expected claim......................................................................
Your answer:

  Care homes         purchase ....% to ....%   new build ....% to ....%   refurb ....% to ....%
  Dental             purchase ....% to ....%   new build ....% to ....%   refurb ....% to ....%
  GP / primary care  purchase ....% to ....%   new build ....% to ....%   refurb ....% to ....%
  Private hospital   purchase ....% to ....%   new build ....% to ....%   refurb ....% to ....%
  Supported living   purchase ....% to ....%   new build ....% to ....%   refurb ....% to ....%

Or: the shape is wrong, and here is why ..............................

How confident are you?    High / Medium / Low
Answered by:                                     Date:

We said "the shape is wrong" is acceptable. It is not the surveyor's answer, so the bands stay and are recorded as a draft. Still needed: the surveyor's ranges.

## What counts as too far apart when we test ourselves against a real claim?

 **Added 9 September 2026. For the chartered surveyor AND the tax adviser — it needs both, and it is short.** Also found in the June document, and it has been open ever since without anybody being asked.

 **What we think. Confidence: medium on the method, low on the numbers, and there is a problem with our anchor.**

 We keep one real, completed claim as a permanent test case — we call it the golden case — and every time we change the software we re-run it and check we still get the same answer. **That is the only thing standing between us and quietly breaking the calculation.** Two questions come out of it and neither has ever been put to anybody.

 **First: how close is close enough?** Our June draft proposed **within 10% on the cost components, and within 0.03 on the apportionment multiplier**. Those numbers were written by us, with nothing behind them. Too tight and every honest improvement to the model looks like a failure; too loose and a real defect slips through unnoticed.

 **Second, and it is the uncomfortable one: our anchor may be circular.** One of the figures the model uses — the professional-fees percentage, a flat 15% — **has no cited source, and our own notes record that it may have been back-solved to reproduce the golden case in the first place.** If that is what happened, then testing the model against that case partly tests it against itself. **We would rather tell you that than have you find it.**

 **A worked example of why the tolerance matters.** On the golden case the qualifying figure is about **£1,036,000**. A 10% tolerance means anything from **£932,000 to £1,140,000** passes the test — a spread of £208,000, which at a 25% tax rate is about **£52,000 of relief**. **A change that moved the answer by £50,000 of client money would pass our own regression test silently.** State that plainly and 10% starts to look generous.

 **Our recommendation, three parts.** **(a)** Give us a tolerance you would defend — or tell us the single-percentage approach is wrong and it should be banded by job size. **(b)** Tell us whether a case with a parameter fitted to it can be a regression anchor at all, or whether we need a second case that nothing was ever fitted to. **(c)** **Commit, if you are willing, to letting each completed claim become a new test case.** That is the part that compounds: one anchor is a spot check, and ten real jobs is a genuine safety net. It costs you nothing beyond the work you have already done, and it is the single cheapest thing on this list.

 **One more thing to confirm while you are here, because it belongs in the same conversation.** Our sign-off routing is: **an invoice-based claim goes to the tax adviser only; a purchase apportionment goes to the surveyor for a desktop review, escalating to a site visit above an agreed value or where entitlement is contested; and nothing is filed without the matching signature.** That is written down in several places and **nowhere does it ask either of you to confirm it as a routing.** Does it match how you would actually want to work?

 **What it unblocks.** Nothing stops without it. **What it changes is whether “the tests still pass” means anything**, which is the sentence every future phase of this build will rest on.

```
ANSWER
......................................................................
Your answer:

  Tolerance on the cost components:  +/- .5...%  (or: banded, as follows ....)
  Tolerance on the multiplier:       +/- ..0.01..

  Can a case with a parameter fitted to it be an anchor?
                                Yes / No / Only alongside a second case - only alongside a second case

  Will you let each closed claim become a test case?      Yes / No - yes

  Is the sign-off routing right?    Yes / No, it should be ................Yes, subject to the tax adviser and 
surveyor confirming their respective scope and escalation triggers in writing.

How confident are you?    High / Medium / Low

Answered by:                                     Date:

If you disagree with our recommendation, what should we do instead?


......................................................................
```

 **Read 15 September 2026 (Phase 132c).** The box holds an unsigned answer: ±5% on costs and ±0.01 on the multiplier; a fitted case only alongside a second case; each closed claim becomes a test case; the sign-off routing is right, subject to each professional confirming their scope. Letting closed claims become test cases is the surveyor's commitment to give, not ours. Recorded as a draft; nothing changes. Still needed: the surveyor's and the tax adviser's answers.

## The other fourteen — worth asking, but nothing stops without them


 **Is our room list right?**

 The plain-English list is built and will be in front of you on the call. **Please mark it up as we go rather than taking it away — the annotations are the answer.** We want to know what is missing, not whether it can be longer.

## Does a desk review cost the same on a private hospital as on a dental practice?

 **Added 8 September 2026 by Phase 100, while costing the small end. Board item 12955448913.**

 **What we think.** We have costed two ways of producing a claim. On the **site-visit** route a surveyor produces the valuation and attends the property, and we have assumed that costs more for a bigger building: **£1,500 for a dental practice, £2,200 for a GP surgery, £3,200 for a care home, £3,500 for a private hospital.** On the **desk** route you would read, challenge and sign a pack we produce, without attending — and there we have assumed **a flat £600 for every sector**, on the basis of two to three hours of specialist time at published rates of £100–£250 an hour.

 **The flat £600 is almost certainly wrong and we do not know in which direction.** It came from a paper that used a dental practice as its reference throughout, and the figure has since been read as if it applied to everything.

 **A worked example of why it matters.** A £2.7m care home identifies roughly £810,000 of allowances. Our fee is 5% of that, so £40,500 — comfortable either way. But a **£240,000 care home** identifies about £72,000, so the fee is £3,600. On our numbers a desk job costs £2,645 all-in at year-one volumes and a site visit costs £5,390. **So that job pays if it is done from the desk and loses about £1,800 if somebody drives to it.** If the true desk cost on a care home is not £600 but £1,500, that job stops paying altogether — and we would not know, because nothing in our model would have changed. **Revised 17 September 2026: those fees are at the old 5%. At the flat 3% they are £24,300 and £2,160, so the small care home no longer pays even from the desk — it loses about £485, and about £3,230 with a site visit.**


 **Our recommendation.** Give us a rough multiplier rather than a precise quote. Something as coarse as _"a desk review of a care home is about twice the work of a dental practice, a hospital about three times"_ is enough — we will hold it as an estimate and mark it as one. A single number per sector is better still.

 **What your answer unblocks.** It sets the smallest job we can profitably take **in each sector rather than only in dentistry**, which is the thing that decides whether we say yes or no to a small enquiry. It is also the input to the routing rule: which jobs go to the desk, which need somebody to attend, and which we should decline rather than run at a loss.

 **One thing this is not.** It is not the question about whether a desk estimate is _defensible_ — that one is already on your agenda and it is a judgement about your own liability, not about price. This is only about cost, and it assumes the answer to that one is yes.

```
ANSWER
..Price is dertermined by purchase price/cost work - not by type of business or desk survey or f2f....................................................................
Your answer:

(a) Roughly how much more (or less) does a DESK review-and-sign cost
    than the same thing on a dental practice?

        dental practice      1.0   (the reference)
        GP surgery           .....
        care home            .....
        private hospital     .....

(b) Or, if you would rather give money than multiples:

        dental practice      GBP .....
        GP surgery           GBP .....
        care home            GBP .....
        private hospital     GBP .....

(c) Is there anything about a building that changes desk cost MORE than
    its sector does - age, number of buildings on the site, whether we
    have drawings, anything else?



Date:                    Name:

```

 **Read 15 September 2026 (Phase 132c).** The box holds an unsigned view: "Price is determined by purchase price/cost work - not by type of business or desk survey or f2f". The question asks a surveyor what a desk review costs by sector, and this answers from our side, so it is provisional. The desk and site-visit costs stay as estimates. Still needed from the surveyor: multiples or amounts, or confirmation that cost follows the price rather than the sector.

## Which London boroughs count as Inner London?

 **Added 9 September 2026.** For the chartered surveyor. **This is the only thing standing between us and a proper regional figure for a London property** — everywhere else in England and Wales is already resolved.

 **The question.** The price book you may end up telling us to use divides the country into thirteen regions, and two of them are **Inner London** and **Outer London**, priced differently. It does not say which boroughs belong to which. **Which definition should we use?**

 **Why it is not obvious.** There are two published definitions of Inner London in ordinary use and they are not the same list. The older statutory one is twelve boroughs plus the City of London. The statistical one used for national accounts adds **Haringey** and **Newham**. So a property in Haringey is Inner London on one reading and Outer London on the other.

 **What it is worth.** The book prices Inner London at **1.05** and Outer London at **1.00** — about **five per cent on the whole cost plan**. On a £4m build-up that is £200,000 of replacement cost, before anything else is applied to it.

 **What we have done in the meantime, and why.** Nothing. A London postcode currently resolves to its council and then **refuses to produce a region at all**, saying this question is open. **We deliberately did not default to Outer London**, even though it is the obvious fallback, because it is the cheaper of the two and a silent default that happens to suit us is exactly the kind of thing this project does not do. A refusal on a screen is honest; a 1.00 nobody chose is not.

 **Our recommendation.** Tell us which list, or tell us that the distinction is not one you would rely on for this purpose and we should treat all of London as one region. **Either is usable.** What we cannot do is guess.

 **What your answer unblocks.** Rate figures you give us on a London job would be offered back to you on your next London job, correctly grouped. It is also one of the two inputs the regional cost factor needs — the other is a price book nobody has bought yet.

```
ANSWER
.That list plus Haringey and Newham.

For this regional cost model, I would use the current ONS Inner/Outer London geography rather than the older
 statutory list. ONS currently treats 14 local authorities as Inner London: the City of London, Camden, Hackney,
 Hammersmith & Fulham, Haringey, Islington, Kensington & Chelsea, Lambeth, Lewisham, Newham, Southwark, 
Tower Hamlets, Wandsworth and Westminster.

This is preferable to mixing the statutory definition with a cost model because the purpose here is regional
 cost classification, not a legal definition of Inner London. The London Government Act's statutory definition is 
different, which is exactly why the basis should be stated explicitly......................................................................
Your answer:

Which definition of Inner London?
   The twelve boroughs and the City   /   That list plus Haringey and Newham
   /   Treat all of London as one region   /   Other (say which)

If "Other", list the boroughs you would count as Inner London:


How confident are you?    High / Medium / Low

Answered by:                                     Date:


...................................................................... 
```

 **Read 15 September 2026 (Phase 132c).** The box holds an unsigned answer: "That list plus Haringey and Newham", the current ONS list of fourteen. It is not the surveyor's answer, so it is provisional. A build phase adds that list to the region lookup behind a switch that stays off, so London keeps refusing a region until the surveyor confirms. No figure moves either way today, because no regional cost factor is loaded.

 **Built 16 September 2026 (Phase 132e), switched off.** The list was checked at the Office for National Statistics first: its council-to-Inner-and-Outer-London lookup and its regional geography lookup both give exactly the fourteen named here. **A correction to the note above:** against the older statutory list, ONS adds Haringey and Newham and also leaves out Greenwich, which is Outer London on the ONS list. The fourteen are in the region lookup behind a surveyor switch that is off. Off, London is refused exactly as before, compared field by field; on, the fourteen read as Inner London and the other London boroughs as Outer London. The list is recorded as an unsigned draft, not the surveyor's answer, and the entry is on the surveyor's register. **No figure moves either way:** no regional cost factor is loaded.

## Does WordPress replace the rest of the system, or sit beside it?

 **The question.** Everything built so far — the estimate engine, the eleven rulebooks, the document controls — is TypeScript and browser JavaScript. WordPress runs PHP.

 **Where it stands.** This was answered on 1 September with the larger of the two readings: **WordPress is the whole back end and front end.** So everything built is rebuilt, wrapped or dropped — not connected. That is roughly 19,500 lines of non-test code, 8,500 lines of tests and 1,063 assertions to re-home.

 **Updated 12 September 2026 by CRM 10 of 10 — the size in the price has moved a long way.** The paragraph above counts roughly 19,500 lines of non-test code and 8,500 lines of tests from before the CRM was built. Counted again on 12 September 2026, the back end alone is 85 source files and 39,520 lines, with 17,843 lines in its test folder — and **the CRM is 37 of those source modules and 21,829 of those lines**, 55 of the database's 87 tables and 236 of its 242 triggers. **If a Node process can run beside WordPress, the CRM adds almost nothing to the job.** If everything must be rebuilt in PHP, the CRM is now the larger half of what is rebuilt, and most of its safeguards live in those triggers, which a rebuild would have to reproduce and prove one by one. **That makes the one-sentence confirmation worth more, not less.** Count again rather than quote these figures; the Developer Spec's section 5.5 says how they were counted.

 **Our recommendation.** Treat it as answered, but **get it confirmed in one sentence before pricing**, because the two readings are genuinely different quotes and this one is the expensive reading. If a Node service can run alongside — which is one of the two hosting questions above — much of the rebuild disappears.

 **What it unblocks.** The scope, the price and the stack. **Nothing can be quoted until it is confirmed.**

```
ANSWER
WordPress does not replace the existing TypeScript/Node system; it sits alongside it. WordPress should provide 
the website/content layer, while the existing application, estimate engine, CRM, rulebooks and document controls
 continue to run as the application/backend services, communicating with WordPress through defined APIs.

The existing safeguards and business logic should not be rebuilt in PHP merely because WordPress is being used.
 The developer should confirm the proposed architecture, hosting arrangement, API boundaries and responsibility 
for each component before pricing the build.......................................................................

## Can the client-document folders be excluded from the nightly whole-site backup?

 **Rewritten 12 September 2026 by Phase 129, for a document that is kept** — the 2 September decision — and to add the holding folder, which is the case no backup schedule can fix.

 **The question.** One question to the host, about two folders. Every uploaded document first lands, unredacted, in a **holding folder** for up to an hour while the names we have no right to hold are stripped out. It then moves to a **document store**, where it is kept for six years once the data protection officer approves that period, and deleted at the end of it or earlier on an erasure request. Can both folders be left out of every backup, snapshot and copy the host takes?

 **What we think.** The holding folder matters most, and it has only one acceptable answer. A copy of it keeps an unstripped file — including content we have written down we have no lawful basis to hold — for as long as the host keeps copies, instead of for an hour. The document store matters less, but it still matters: a copy taken just before we delete a document keeps it alive while our deletion record says it is gone.

 **The example.** An accounting period ends on 31 March 2027 and our report is delivered on 15 June 2027. The document is kept to 15 June 2033 and deleted that day. If the host took a copy that morning and keeps copies for 30 days, the document survives until 15 July 2033 — a month in which our record is wrong. For the holding folder, a file uploaded at 01:40 and caught by a 02:00 nightly copy is kept for 30 days instead of one hour.

 **Our recommendation.** Send the redrafted email attached to "Ask the host: can client-document folders be left out of the backup?" and get the answer in writing. **"We keep backups for 30 days" is not a yes.** If the holding folder cannot be left out, it cannot live on that host and uploads go to storage we control directly. If only the document store cannot be left out, that may be workable, but the data protection officer decides, and every deletion record must then say when the backup copies expire.

 **What it unblocks.** The client-document upload page, and one risk on the data protection assessment.

## Will the data protection officer permit a transient read of two register name fields?

 **The question.** We would like to check, where a public register can tell us, that the person who signed the engagement letter represents the business they signed for. Two free sources cover it: the Companies House officers list, and the care regulator's nominated individual.

 **Our own control refuses it today, and that is the control working correctly** — those fields are not among the two permitted name exceptions, so they are classified as unrecognised and refused rather than waved through.

 **Our recommendation, and the shape of the ask matters more than the answer.** **Ask only for permission to read, compare in memory and discard — with no write exception at all.** The output we need is a yes/no and a source tag, not a name. **Do not let it get broadened into "add officer names as a third exception"**: that is a much larger ask, it will probably be refused, and it will take the feature down with it.

 **One thing to get right or the flag becomes a liability.** "Not found" is the **expected** result for most legitimate signers — practice managers, financial controllers, bookkeepers and operations managers appear on no public register anywhere, free or paid, and that is most of the dropdown. It must be recorded as "not found", never as "unverified", never as a risk score, and **the client must never see any of it.**

 **What it unblocks.** One soft anti-fraud check that blocks nobody.

The hosting account is understood to be shared with several unrelated businesses. A trusted circle drawn around an unknown set of people is not a trusted circle, and the risk rating we have recorded for the audit log depends on that set being small and known.
