## Does the backup risk need prior consultation with the Information Commissioner?

 **Added 15 September 2026 by Phase 132a — a second place the same article applies, so that both are decided here.** Article 36 has now been read for the assessment covering people named in documents clients upload. On that assessment's own findings, consultation is not triggered today: nothing is processed yet, and the public notices page is a safeguard not yet in place rather than one that cannot be. It could be triggered if a document naming someone we cannot contact is kept before the page is live — a lease naming a witness with no address, for example. **Our recommendation:** make "the notices page is live" a condition of keeping any such document, which avoids the question. Whether the risk that remains is high is the officer's judgement. **What it unblocks:** the assessment's condition on publishing, its linked risk in the risk register, and the first upload we keep.


 **Rewritten 12 September 2026 by Phase 129, for a document that is kept.** The example and what it unblocks, below, are unchanged.

 **The question.** Our host may take copies of the whole website that we cannot pick apart. Client documents go to two places: a holding folder, where each file sits unredacted for up to an hour, and a document store, where it is kept for six years and then deleted. **If the folders cannot be left out of those copies, the holding folder's one-hour limit becomes however long the host keeps copies, and every deletion from the document store — at six years, or on request — leaves copies we cannot reach.** Before we accept that as a residual risk, is it a **high** risk we cannot reduce — in which case the law requires us to consult the Commissioner before starting the processing at all, rather than to record it and go ahead?

 **What we think.** Confidence: **low**, and this is one where we would rather be told we are wrong. Our reading is that consultation bites only where the risk is high **and stays high after mitigation**, so the answer depends on what the host will actually agree to — and that is open in this same section. **The two folders are not the same risk.** If the holding folder can be left out, what remains is a copy that outlives a deletion by the length of the host's backup cycle, and we think that can be brought down by stating the cycle and saying so in every deletion record. If the holding folder cannot be left out, there is no mitigation available on that host at all.

 **An example, and it is not hypothetical.** A client uploads a lease. That night it is copied into the backup. Three months later the client asks us to delete it. We delete the live file, honestly, and the backup still holds every nightly copy taken in between. **We would have to tell the client the truth: we have deleted it from the system and we cannot reach the copies.** That is the conversation this question decides whether we ever have to have.

 **Our recommendation.** **Ask the host first, then decide this.** If both folders can be left out, there is nothing to consult about. If only the document store cannot, assess the residual risk in writing; we expect it not to be high. **If the holding folder cannot be left out, move uploads off that host rather than consult** — and if the business chooses to stay, consult before the first client document is accepted, not after, because a consultation duty discharged late is not discharged. **We are not asking the data protection officer to run a consultation; we are asking whether the threshold is crossed.**

 **What it unblocks.** The client-document upload page, which is on the board as blocked on exactly this and has been since 1 September. It also decides whether the retention rule we are asking the DPO to approve is enforceable at all — **a six-year deletion rule against a backup nobody can delete from is a rule about the live copy only, and it should say so.**

## May the upload strip run after the file is already stored, or must it run before?

 **The question.** When a client uploads a document, we take out of it the personal data we have no basis to hold — names of people who are not our client, and anything else the rules for that document type say to remove. **The order matters.** Today the file lands in storage first and is stripped afterwards. **Between those two moments the unstripped file exists, on our systems, containing personal data about people who have no relationship with us.** Is that acceptable if the window is short and the file is not readable by anything else in the meantime — or must nothing be written to durable storage until the strip has run?

 **What we think.** Confidence: **medium**. Our own design says the strip is the boundary and nothing is promoted past it until it has run, and the upload handler was built to that order and tested. **But the file still has to physically arrive somewhere before anything can read it and strip it**, so "before storage" cannot mean "never written down" — it can only mean **written to a quarantined place that nothing else can reach, stripped there, and only then promoted**. That is what is built. **The question is whether that satisfies the rule, or whether the quarantined write is itself the problem.**

 **An example.** A care-home fire risk assessment is uploaded at 14:00. It names the responsible individual and two staff. The strip runs at 14:00:02 and removes them. **For two seconds, and in whatever the storage layer keeps afterwards — logs, temporary copies, a backup that happened to run — the unstripped file existed.** If the answer is that the two seconds are fine, we carry on. If the answer is that they are not, **the fix is not a code change, it is a different upload path**, and the developer needs to know before building it and not after.

 **Our recommendation.** Confirm that a fail-closed quarantine — written where nothing else can read it, stripped there, promoted only on success, and deleted with evidence on the failure path too — is the correct boundary, and that the moment of arrival is not itself the thing that has to be prevented. **And tell us what that means for the backup**, because a nightly copy that catches a quarantined file has just made the two seconds permanent. **That connects this question to the one above and they should be answered together.**

 **What it unblocks.** Client uploads, and the shape of the upload path the developer builds. The quarantine described above is already built; since 12 September 2026 its board item waits on your signature rather than being marked as blocked on this question. **It is also the difference between a control that works and a control that works except on the night the backup runs.**

## May company names stay on conveyancing uploads if we strip the individuals?

 **Built and switched off, 12 September 2026 (Phase 129).** The narrow version is built: on a conveyancing upload, a company or LLP that is a party keeps its name and Companies House number, and every individual — and any party whose legal form is not known — is stripped to a role such as [SELLER]. Sole traders, partnerships and public bodies are stripped too. Switched off, nothing changes. Keeping an individual who is themselves the party is not built; your answer decides it.

 **The question.** The documents that matter most to a claim — the sale contract, the pre-contract conveyancing replies — have a parties clause, and **the parties clause is load-bearing.** Who sold to whom, and whether the seller was within the charge to UK tax, is the question entitlement turns on. So we cannot strip the parties clause and keep the document useful. **May we keep the names of the companies in it, and strip only the individuals — the solicitors, the signatories, the witnesses, a proprietor who held personally?**

 **What we think.** Confidence: **medium to high on the principle, low on where the line falls.** A company name is not personal data, so keeping it should not need a basis at all. **The difficulty is that in this sector it very often is a person's name**: a sole trader's practice, a family property company, a proprietor holding personally. **A rule that says "keep companies, strip individuals" is easy to write and hard to apply, because the document does not always tell you which it is looking at.**

 **An example.** A contract names "Fairview Dental Care Ltd" as seller — clearly a company, clearly keep. The next line names "Dr A Patel" as the proprietor of the freehold, and "Dr A Patel" is both the personal data of a living individual **and the fact the whole entitlement chain turns on.** Strip it and the claim cannot be evidenced. Keep it and we are holding the name of someone who may never become our client.

 **Our recommendation.** Permit **company names and the entity's registered number to be retained on these two document types**, strip every named individual by default, and where an individual's name is the party to the transaction, **keep it — but as a party to our client's own transaction, on the same basis as everything else we hold about that transaction, and record it as such** rather than treating it as an incidental name that slipped through. **We would rather have a narrow permission with a stated reason than a broad one nobody can apply.** And if the answer is that we may not keep an individual party's name, say so plainly, because **that decides that we cannot process those two document types at all** and the two extraction specifications behind them should be abandoned rather than left gated.

 **What it unblocks.** Two client-upload document types the pipeline cannot handle today, both of them blocked on this and on nothing else. **It is also a condition inside the impact assessment on the document pipeline** and should be answered at the same time as that is approved.

## Does the permission gate apply to a council-area lookup with no personal data in it?

 **Added 8 September 2026.** For the data protection officer. **Nothing is blocked on this** — the thing it concerns was built and switched off, and it stays off until you answer.

 **The question.** We now derive which council area a property sits in, from the client's own postcode, using a published Office for National Statistics table that we hold locally. **May that run as soon as the client gives us the postcode, or must it wait behind the published privacy notice like our other lookups?**

 **What we think.** Confidence: **medium, and it is your call not ours.** Our existing rule is that free data lookups fire once the client has given permission, and permission there means a published privacy notice reachable from the address question. But **the reasoning behind that rule does not obviously reach this one.** The gate was justified on the grounds that those lookups process information about _identifiable people_ — the proprietor at HM Land Registry, persons of significant control at Companies House. This lookup processes none. It is the client's own postcode, mapped against a static public table, with **no external call, no third party's data, and no natural person anywhere in the product.**

 **A worked example.** A client types "CF10 3NB" at the address question. We look it up in a table on our own server and get "Cardiff". Nothing leaves our system, nobody else's data is touched, and the answer is used to pick a regional building-cost factor.

 **Our recommendation.** That this does **not** need the notice gate, and can run on the postcode alone — on the basis that it is computation over data the client has just given us, for the purpose they gave it, under the legitimate-interests assessment already written. **But we have deliberately not acted on that view.** The module ships inert and we would rather ask than assume, because the cost of asking is nothing and the cost of being wrong is a lawful-basis problem in a client-facing journey.

 **One thing to weigh either way.** If your answer is that the notice gate applies to everything derived from a client's address regardless of whose data it is, say so plainly — that is a cleaner rule to operate and we would rather have one rule than two.

 **Update, 9 September 2026 — the lookup is now wired in, and it is still switched off.** The council-area lookup has been connected to the thing that consumes it, and the table it reads from has been given somewhere to live. **Nothing about this question changed and nothing has been assumed.**

 **Two things are worth confirming to you because they are what you would ask.** First, **no second permission gate was built.** There is one gate in this system, the published-notice one, and this lookup does not add another — what exists is a single switch saying whether this lookup sits _behind_ that gate. Second, **either answer is one line.** Say it is gated and the switch flips and the lookup waits with everything else; say it is not and the switch stays as it is. There is a test asserting the existing gate is still shut.

 **And it costs nothing to leave open.** The table is a 75 MB file that is built by a free download and is not installed on any deployment, so the lookup is inert today whichever way you answer.

## May we ask our client to pass a transparency notice to a stranger named on their own paperwork?

 **Added 8 September 2026.** For the data protection officer. **This one has been depended on for ten days without existing.** A board item has cited it since 29 August as "open question Q31", and it was never written down anywhere — the same failure that was found on this board once before, where three items were marked blocked on a question nobody had drafted.

 **The question.** Two of our data sources give us the name of somebody who is not our client: a person with significant control at Companies House, and an individual proprietor on an HM Land Registry title. **Because we did not get their data from them, Article 14 says we must tell them we hold it** — within a month.

 Sometimes we have no way to reach them at all, and there is an exemption for that. Sometimes we have no way to reach them **but our client does**, because it is their company or their property. **May we discharge the duty by asking our client to pass our notice on?**

 **What we think.** Confidence: **low, and this is squarely yours.** The argument for is that it reaches the person, which is the point of Article 14, and it is less intrusive than us finding an address for a stranger. The argument against is that we cannot evidence delivery, we have handed a data-protection duty to someone with no obligation to perform it, and we have told our client that we hold data about a third party — which is itself a disclosure.

 **A worked example.** A client's care home is owned by a company with one person with significant control: their brother, who has no involvement in the claim. We hold his name, birth month and year. We have no address for him. Our client sees him every week. Do we (a) ask our client to pass him the notice, (b) publish and rely on the exemption, or (c) both?

 **Our recommendation.** Treat the client route as **available but not sufficient on its own** — use it where it exists, publish as well, and record which was done. **But we have deliberately not acted on that view.**

 **What your answer unblocks, and why the field was built before you answered.** The store that records this shipped on 8 September with three route values — direct, via the client, and not sent because there was no route. **The field is needed whichever way you answer**, which is why it was buildable now; what your answer changes is whether the middle value may ever be used. Any record already carrying it is findable in one call, so the answer will actually reach the data rather than sitting in a document.

## How do we identify a stranger who asks what we hold about them?

 **Added 8 September 2026.** For the data protection officer. **Nothing is blocked on this today** — but it becomes urgent the moment we hold a single one of these records, and the answer takes longer to design than a rights request gives us.

 **The question.** Our retention procedure sets a proportionate identity rule: **match the request against what we already hold.** That is a sensible rule and it works for a client.

 **It does not work for the two register populations.** For a person with significant control we hold a name, a birth month and year, and a nationality. **Every one of those facts is on a public register that anybody can read.** So a stranger emailing us can quote back, perfectly, everything we would use to authenticate them — and matching held data authenticates nobody.

 **What we think.** Confidence: **medium on the problem, low on the answer.** The problem is real and structural. The answer is genuinely difficult, because the alternatives are worse in different ways: asking for identity documents means collecting **more** personal data about someone whose complaint is that we hold their data at all, which is the wrong direction; and refusing to answer without it risks obstructing a valid request.

 **A worked example.** An email arrives: _"I am the person with significant control of X Ltd, my name is Y, I was born in March 1974. What do you hold about me?"_ Every fact in that email is on the Companies House register. **We cannot tell whether it came from that person or from anybody who looked them up**, and we have one month to answer.

 **Our recommendation.** A different rule for this population than for clients: **release nothing beyond confirming the categories and the source until identity is established by a route that is not public-register data** — for example a reply to the address on the register, if there is one. We reached the same conclusion from the other direction in the breach work, where the identity standard is one of four facts an Article 34 decision turns on. **This is written as a question rather than adopted, because a rule that under-serves a genuine data subject is as much a failure as one that over-releases.**

 **What it unblocks.** Any rights request from an RP-E1 or RP-E2 subject, and worked example BR-W7 in the breach procedure.

## Is the required "processing consent" tick at contact capture describing the right thing?

 **For the data protection officer.**

 **What we think.** The developer brief's funnel has, at the contact-capture step, a **required processing consent** tick, plus a **separate optional marketing opt-in**. The second one is right and should stay — marketing genuinely does run on consent, and it is genuinely optional. **The first one looks wrong to us in three ways at once**, and we have not changed it, because what it should say is your call and not ours.

 **Why we think it is wrong.** Nothing at that step actually runs on consent. The prospect's own contact details are processed to take steps at their request before entering a contract — **Article 6(1)(b)**, which is what the privacy notice already says. The public-register lookups run on **legitimate interests, Article 6(1)(f)**, with an assessment already written. So a tick labelled "consent" misdescribes both of them. **And a tick somebody must give in order to use the product is not freely given**, which means it is not consent even if we call it that — so we would be claiming a basis we do not have, in place of two we do.

 **Why it matters more than a wording nit.** Consent is withdrawable and an assessment is not. If we tell a prospect their data is processed on their consent, we have told them they can withdraw it and stop the processing — and then either we honour a withdrawal we never needed to offer, or we do not, and we have made a false statement in a transparency notice. **The weaker position is the one that looks more careful.**

 **A worked example.** A practice owner completes the funnel, gets a range, and three weeks later emails to say "withdraw my consent, delete everything". On the tick as drafted, that is a withdrawal of the basis we told them we were using. On the real bases it is an erasure request under Article 17 and an objection under Article 21, both of which we can refuse where we need the data for an actual or possible HMRC enquiry — and we would have to explain why the word on the form did not mean what it said.

 **Our recommendation.** Replace the required tick with a **plain statement and a link to the privacy notice** — no tick at all — saying what we do with their details and what we look up, on the same screen. Keep the marketing opt-in exactly as it is: separate, optional, unticked. **This is the same reasoning already agreed for the register lookups at the address question**, where the answer was a notice and a plain line rather than a box, and it should be the same answer here for the same reason.

 **What it unblocks.** The contact-capture screen. The developer has been told not to ship a required tick that uses the word consent until this is answered, so the screen is buildable but not finishable.

## The Article 14 review you asked for was to happen "when the name question clears". It has cleared — is that review due now?

 **Added 8 September 2026.** For the data protection officer. **Nothing on the board is blocked on this** — but a condition you own has a trigger that has already fired and nobody has recorded that it did.

 **The question.** The Article 14 assessment — the one about people named in documents clients upload — carries a condition in your name. It says the residual non-compliance for the group we could only ever reach by publishing is **recorded on the risk register and reviewed when the brand-name question clears**. That question cleared on 4 September 2026: the business is Caprebate, caprebate.com is registered and live with mail configured, and the decided address for the public notices page is [https://caprebate.com/notices/](https://caprebate.com/notices/). **So on the words of the condition, the review is due.**

 **What we think.** Confidence: **high on the fact, medium on what follows from it.** There are two honest readings and they lead to different places. **Reading A — the words.** The trigger was "the name clears", the name has cleared, so the review happens now. **Reading B — the intent.** What the condition was really waiting for was the moment publishing became _possible_, and it is not: the page is specified and drafted but there is no website to put it on. On that reading the true trigger is the page returning HTTP 200 and nothing is due yet.

 **A worked example of why it matters.** Suppose a client uploads a lease next month naming two individuals we have no address for. Under Reading A there is a dated review on file, taken after the name was settled, recording that the exemption is still relied on and why. Under Reading B there is nothing on file at all until the website exists — which could be months — and the only record of the position is a condition whose stated trigger fired and was ignored. **The difference is not the outcome; it is whether the file shows anybody looked.**

 **Our recommendation.** **Do the short review now, under Reading A** — it is what the condition says and it will take you minutes, because almost nothing has changed except the name. Then, if it concludes that the position is unchanged, **re-point the trigger explicitly at the notices page returning 200**, so that it fires again at the moment that actually matters. Re-wording it without holding the review first would quietly cancel a review your own condition has already called for.

 **What we deliberately did not do.** Phase 103a corrected the stale "blocked behind the name" attribution everywhere else in that assessment on 8 September 2026 and **left this one condition untouched**, because changing a condition's trigger is a different act from correcting a statement of fact, and it is yours rather than ours.

 **What it unblocks.** Nothing is waiting on it today. It closes a condition that currently reads as overdue on its own terms.

 **Note added 14 September 2026 by Phase 131: one data point from the regulator.** In its guidance on the right to be informed, read on 13 September 2026, the regulator's own example of a case where telling people individually is impossible has the organisation publish the information on its website and also place a notice in a local newspaper pointing people to it. That bears on whether a notices page on our own website, reached from the footer, is enough on its own for the people we can only reach by publishing. It does not answer the question; it is here so that you can.

## How long do we keep a member of staff's photograph after they leave?

 **What we think.** From 8 September 2026 a member of staff can put a photograph on their own account. It is a picture of a person's face, so it is personal data of a kind that is obvious to everyone who sees it and easy to forget in a retention schedule, because it does not live in an obvious "personal data" table.

 **What is actually built, so that the question is about a real thing.** The photograph is not stored in the database. It is written into the same hardened store that holds client documents — outside the web root, owner-read-only, no execute bit — and is served only through an endpoint that authorises each request. Nothing the person uploaded is kept: the file is decoded to pixels and a **new** one is written from those pixels, which drops every text chunk, colour profile and anything appended after the end of the image. Each person can remove their own photograph at any time and the file is deleted for real, with the deletion proved rather than assumed.

 **Here is the gap, and it is the whole question.** Disabling an account ends its sessions immediately, but **it does nothing to the photograph.** The file stays and the row stays, indefinitely, because nothing has been told what "indefinitely" should be. An administrator cannot delete it either — putting a face on somebody's account, or taking one off, is `self` for every role including the super user, deliberately, because editing the name and face on an account produces a record that reads as the person themselves.

 **An example.** A sales colleague leaves in March. Their account is disabled the same afternoon and every session ends. In September their photograph is still on the server, and the only person who could remove it no longer has a login.

 **Our recommendation.** Delete the photograph when the account is disabled, in the same transaction that revokes the sessions. It is the least surprising rule, it needs no scheduler, and a disabled account has nothing left to display a face on. If you would rather keep it for a period — because a disabled account is sometimes re-enabled a week later — say what that period is and we will build the sweep. **We have not built either, because building one would answer this.**

 **What it unblocks.** Nothing is stopped today. It closes the one thing in the staff settings screen that has no retention rule, and it wants an answer before real staff use it rather than after.

## Which receipt strip should each new document type carry before anyone may upload one?

 **Recommended answer drafted, 12 September 2026 (Phase 129).** A recommended strip for each of the fourteen document types is now recorded in the system, switched off, each with a one-line reason you can approve or amend. **Keep whole:** the engagement letter, our fee invoice, the survey report, the capital allowances report and claim documentation. **Remove the individuals and keep the figures:** the completion statement, a valuation, accounts, a tax computation, a contractor's invoices and plans. **Do not accept as uploads:** survey photographs, correspondence and "other". Until you answer, none of the fourteen can be uploaded or asked for — recommending a strip changes nothing on its own. **One correction to "What your answer unblocks" below:** an accepted type needs a strip of its own in the ingestion module and a migration that rebuilds the document-type table, not one line.

 **Added 11 September 2026 by CRM 4 of 10.** For the data protection officer. **Fourteen kinds of document are recorded in the system and switched off until this is answered.**

 **The question.** Every document a client uploads passes a receipt strip before anything read out of it is kept: a rule, for each kind of document, saying which parts may be kept and which are removed — signatures, correspondence, the details of anyone with no reason to be in our records. Eleven kinds already have one: the lease, the fire risk assessment, the asbestos register and the rest of the Stage 2 upload list, and planning documents. The CRM specification asks for fourteen more — the engagement letter, the completion statement, a valuation, accounts, a tax computation, our fee invoice, a contractor's invoices, plans and drawings, survey photographs, the survey report, the capital allowances report, claim documentation, correspondence, and "other". **None of them has a strip, so the system refuses an upload of any of them, and refuses to ask a client for one.** For each, what may be kept and what must be removed?

 **What we think.** Confidence: **medium**. The fourteen fall into three groups. **Documents we produce** — the engagement letter, our fee invoice, the survey report, the capital allowances report and claim documentation — name nobody who is not already our client or our own staff, and could be kept whole. **Financial documents from the client** — accounts, a tax computation, a completion statement, a valuation, a contractor's invoices, plans — name the client's own officers and advisers and sometimes a counterparty; the individuals could be removed as they are on a lease, and the figures kept. **Photographs, correspondence and "other"** can contain anyone and anything and cannot be stripped by section at all: a photograph of a care home's corridor may show a resident.

 **An example.** A client uploads their company's statutory accounts. Page 1 names two directors and the accountant who prepared them; page 3 is the balance sheet. Under a strip like the lease's, the three names are removed from what we keep, and the balance sheet — which says whether the company can use the allowances — is kept. A survey photograph of a day room has no parts to keep or remove: a strip that keeps "the findings" of a photograph keeps the whole picture.

 **Our recommendation.** **Allow the five we produce with nothing removed; allow the six financial and drawn documents under the lease's strip, individuals removed and figures kept; and do not accept photographs, correspondence or "other" as uploads at all** — survey photographs stay in the surveyor's own record, and a letter is summarised in a note rather than stored. If that is right, each accepted kind is one entry in the ingestion module and one line in one migration, and nothing else in the system changes.

 **What your answer unblocks.** Uploading, and asking a client for, any of the fourteen. Until then they are recorded, configurable and switched off, and the claim page says so.

```
ANSWER
......................................................................
Your answer:

Documents we produce - keep them whole?                          Yes / No

Financial and drawn documents - remove individuals, keep figures? Yes / No

Photographs, correspondence and "other" - refuse as uploads?     Yes / No


How confident are you?    High / Medium / Low


If you disagree with our recommendation, what should we do instead?

......................................................................
Who answered:                      Date:
```

## What must a referred client be told before we share their claim's progress with the firm that referred them?

 **Added 11 September 2026 by CRM 5 of 10.** For the data protection officer. **Nothing is blocked on this** — the capture is built with drafted words, nothing is sent, and the public referral form is switched off.

 **The question.** When an accountant refers a client, the referral paper says the accountant is kept informed at each stage — but only with the client's consent, because the client, not the accountant, is the person the progress is about. The system now records the client's answer against that firm, with the version of the words they were read, and queues a notice to the firm only while the answer is yes. **Are the words right, is consent the right basis, and what must the privacy notice say before the public referral form — which collects another firm's client's details — is switched on?**

 **What we think.** Confidence: medium. The words, drafted as version SHARE-PROGRESS-1, read: "May we tell {the firm} how your claim is progressing? We would tell them when the referral reaches us, when your indicative range is issued, when you engage us, when your report is delivered, when our invoice stage is complete, and — if the answer is that there is no claim — that there is none, and why. We would not send them figures, your documents or anything about the people in your business. You can change your mind at any time." We read this as **consent** — genuinely optional, withdrawable, and nothing else depends on it — which is different from the register lookups, whose basis is legitimate interests. Never asked shares nothing, exactly as a no does. For the form, what a practice sends us is collected about the client from someone other than the client, so we think Article 14 applies and the privacy notice needs a paragraph saying what an introducing firm passes to us, why, and how the client can object — reachable from the form itself.

 **An example.** Ledger & Co refers Meadow Dental Limited. On the first call the practice manager is read the words and says yes; it is recorded as given, with the time, who recorded it and the version. The one person named at Ledger & Co is told the referral is with us. Three weeks later the client signs the engagement letter and Ledger & Co is told "engaged" — and nothing else: no range, no figure. In May the client withdraws; the next stage, "report delivered", is kept as not queued, and nothing reaches the firm. Had nobody ever asked, every one of those notices would have been kept as not queued, and nothing would have been sent.

 **Our recommendation.** Approve the words as consent. Add the referral paragraph to the privacy notice at its next revision, and switch the form on only once the notice carrying it is published.

 **What your answer unblocks.** The words becoming a version the business may rely on; the public referral form being switched on once the notice is published; and a client giving or withdrawing the answer in their own portal, which is written into the compliance registers phase.

```
ANSWER
......................................................................
Your answer:

Are the words right?                                  Yes / Change them as follows

Is consent the right basis for sharing progress?      Yes / No — use:

What must the privacy notice add before the form opens?


How confident are you?                                High / Medium / Low

If you disagree with our recommendation, say so here:


Answered by:                                     Date:

......................................................................
```

## May the chartered tax adviser's own name be kept on the claim's sign-off record, or only their firm and credential?

 **Added 11 September 2026 by CRM 6 of 10. For the data protection officer. Nothing is blocked on this** — the sign-off record is built to keep the adviser's firm or role and their credential, and never a person's name, until you answer.

 **The question.** When the chartered tax adviser signs off a claim, the operating procedure says the sign-off is "logged with date and adviser name", and the defence pack's specification wants the adviser's name, credential and practising status. The claim record's own rule is that no third party's name is kept in it. Is the adviser who signs our client's claim a third party whose name stays out of the claim record, or a professional acting on the claim whose name may be kept beside their sign-off?

 **What we think.** Confidence: medium. The adviser is not a third party in the sense that rule was written for — a previous owner, a seller's director, a tenant — but a professional engaged on the work, and who signed is the point of the record: a defence pack that cannot say who signed it is weaker in an enquiry. Against that, the adviser is a person whose name would sit in a record kept for six years or more, on every claim they sign, and the firm and the credential are what an enquiry usually asks for — and those are already kept.

 **An example.** Ledger Tax Advisers LLP's chartered tax adviser signs off Meadow Dental Limited's claim on 1 September 2026. Today the record reads "Ledger Tax Advisers LLP, Chartered Tax Adviser, signed 2026-09-01, scope: the whole claim". With your yes it would also carry the adviser's own name.

 **Our recommendation.** Permit the name, on our legitimate interest in evidencing who signed a filing position, with the adviser told when we engage them that their name is kept on the sign-off record for as long as the claim's records are kept.

 **What your answer unblocks.** The sign-off record carrying the name the procedure and the defence pack ask for — one column and one guard to change — or, if the answer is no, the procedure and the defence pack's specification reworded to the firm and the credential.

```

......................................................................

## May we make a small, logged test lookup to prove a data connector works, and on what conditions?

 **Added 13 September 2026 by the Phase 130 sweep. For the data protection officer.** A board item has asked this since 4 September, and until today it was written down nowhere: the action plan pointed at "Will the data protection officer permit a transient read of two register name fields?", which is a different question, about checking who signed an engagement letter.

 **The question.** Each of the six data connectors that wait on a licence or a purchase is built with fetching switched off, and each has an activation checklist. Every checklist needs at least one live lookup against the real register before the connector is trusted, to prove the response has the shape the code assumes. Our own rules forbid exactly that: no bulk lookup, and no demonstration lookup. So, as written, a connector cannot be switched on without breaking the control that governs it.

 **What we think.** Confidence: medium. A single lookup made only to test a connector is still processing if what comes back names a person, so it needs a lawful basis and a record. But it is not bulk processing and it is not profiling. The legitimate interest is plain - not switching on a connector that misreads a register - and the intrusion is small if nothing is kept.

 **An example.** The land boundary connector is switched on once its licence is confirmed. To test it, we look up one title we already know the address of - a public building, or our own registered office once the company exists - compare the boundary that comes back with the documented format, record that the test ran and passed, and discard the response. No client's property is looked up, and no name is stored.

 **Our recommendation.** Permit it, in writing, on five conditions: (1) at most three lookups per connector each time it is activated; (2) never a client's property, and a subject chosen to return no personal data wherever the register allows; (3) the response is compared in memory and discarded - only pass or fail, the time and the connector are written down; (4) each test is logged with who ran it and why; (5) the same conditions apply when a connector is re-tested after the register changes its format. **Do not let it be broadened into an exception for demonstrating the product to clients:** that is a different purpose and a much larger ask.

 **What it unblocks.** Switching on any of the six connectors once its licence or purchase clears. It stops no building today.

```
ANSWER
......................................................................
Your answer:


How confident are you?    High / Medium / Low

Answered by:                                     Date:

## Does Google Analytics need to be on the website at all?

 **Added 14 September 2026 by Phase 131. For the data protection officer.** Nothing is blocked. The analytics work is parked until the website goes live, and this is the question it asks first.

 **The question.** The measurement plan specifies Google Analytics 4 for the assessment funnel: **ten events and nine parameters**, both closed lists, a never-send list, Google Signals off, no user ID, and the tag not loaded at all on any page after engagement. Its own open questions start by challenging all of that: _"does GA4 need to be in the picture at all?"_ The five numbers the plan tracks, grouped by four fields, could be counted from our own server logs. **Should the site use Google Analytics, or count the funnel itself?**

 **What we think.** Confidence: **medium**. At pilot volume the plan already says not to build a dashboard, and to read a plain counts table after each session. Using Google Analytics adds four things this business would otherwise not have: **a named recipient in the privacy notice**, with its purpose; **a transfer position** to record; **an entry in the cookie banner**, with the tag held back until the visitor consents; and **a failure surface** — the plan's own warning is that the likely failure is not malice but "somebody adding a helpful debug parameter eighteen months from now". Counting from our own logs removes all four, at the cost of Google's ready-made reports.

 **An example.** In a pilot week, forty people start the assessment and eleven reach an indicative figure. **With Google Analytics**, those numbers come from Google's reports, the privacy notice names Google, the cookie banner asks first, and visitors who decline are not counted. **From our own logs**, the same two numbers are a count of rows on our server, nobody else receives anything, and visitors who decline cookies are counted as well.

 **Our recommendation.** **Count from our own server logs at pilot.** Reconsider Google Analytics only when paid marketing starts; the go-live checklist already makes analytics a hard gate only before a paid-marketing launch. If you agree, the plan's Google-specific property settings fall away, while the event list, the wrapper that enforces it and the acceptance test still apply unchanged.

 **What your answer unblocks.** Whether the privacy notice, the list of who receives personal data and the cookie banner need a Google entry at all; and what the analytics task builds on the day the website goes live.

```
ANSWER
......................................................................
Your answer:

Google Analytics on the site?    No - count from our own logs  /  Yes, as the plan specifies  /  Not until paid marketing

If yes: anything to add to the privacy notice beyond Google's name, the purpose and the transfer position?

## Which model reads client documents — on whose account, hosted where, kept how long, and does it learn from them?

 **Added 15 September 2026 by Phase 132a — for the data protection officer and Lakhbir, when this is answered.** As built, a value read from a document goes to a person only when its confidence is below its threshold; a value at or above it is recorded with nobody looking. A switch that sends every value of one kind of document to a named reviewer is built, and it is off for all nine kinds. For example, a bed count read from a care home's fire risk assessment at 96% confidence would be recorded unseen. **Our recommendation:** before any model reads a real document, switch it on for fire risk assessments, which can carry residents' health information, and decide the other eight kinds in the same answer. Nothing is blocked today, because no model reads client documents.


 **Added 14 September 2026 by Phase 131. For the developer and the data protection officer, with Lakhbir as the account holder.** This blocks every real document. The pipeline is built, and no model is wired into it.

 **The question.** The document pipeline sends the permitted parts of an uploaded document to a model, which pulls structured facts out of them. Its impact assessment first recorded the model's identity, hosting, retention and training use as "unknown" and rated that High, then lowered the rating once it was found that no model is wired in at all. **The decision is still unmade.** The assessment calls it Gate A — "a vendor and a contractual retention term — not a fact to discover" — and it blocks all eleven document types. **Which provider, on an account in whose name, processing in which country, keeping submitted content for how long, and with what promise about training?**

 **What we think.** Confidence: **high that the question is real, none on the answer**. Four records cannot be completed without it: the list of who receives client personal data carries the provider as an entry that "does not exist"; the supplier breach-notification review records that none is chosen; the Article 14 assessment cannot answer two of its limbs, recipients and transfers; and the breach register cannot say who would owe us a notification. The controls on what is sent are already strong — seven kinds of section are never sent to a model under any setting, and anything that looks like a person's name is refused — **but those controls decide what leaves; only this answer decides where it goes.** The only provider any of our files names is Anthropic, and only as a guess in the impact assessment; its published data processing addendum, read on 13 September 2026, commits to written notice of a security breach "without undue delay, but in any event within 48 hours". Its retention and training terms were not read. Homedata, the address-lookup supplier, publishes no data processing terms at all. None of this is a choice.

 **An example.** A client uploads their lease. The signatories and the correspondence are dropped before anything is assembled to send, and the rent review clause and the landlord's fixtures clause are sent. Today nobody can say which company receives those clauses, in which country they are processed, whether they are kept for a day or a month, or whether they may be used to train a model — so the transparency notice cannot say it, and nobody who knows the terms could report a breach there.

 **Our recommendation.** Choose a provider only on these conditions, and write all five answers into the impact assessment's section 9 before one real document is sent: (1) a business account in Caprebate's name, not a personal or consumer account, with its administrators named and its notice address the privacy mailbox once that is live; (2) a data processing agreement; (3) no training on submitted content, by contract rather than by a setting; (4) the shortest retention the provider offers, stated in days; (5) processing in the UK or the EEA, or, if not, the transfer mechanism recorded. The developer proposes; the data protection officer approves; CAP REBATE LTD signs the contract, by a director. (Until 18 September 2026 this said Lakhbir signs the contract; he has no affiliation with the company.)

 **What your answer unblocks.** Gate A, and with it real documents through the pipeline; the provider's row in the list of recipients, the supplier breach-notification review and the Article 14 assessment; and the data protection officer's approval of the impact assessment, whose first open question this is.

## Which retention clock decides when a client's file is deleted, where two apply?

 **Added 14 September 2026 by Phase 131. For the data protection officer; the developer implements.** Nothing is deleted today: no retention period is approved.

 **The question.** Two periods can apply to the same client file: **the tax-enquiry period** — six years from the end of the accounting period, or longer where an enquiry window or an opened enquiry runs longer — and **the client-document period** — six years from the later of the period end and the date we deliver our report. The built rule, set out in Retention Determinations and tested, **deletes the file on the earlier of the two**. Now that the tax-enquiry period has been drafted, that rule cuts the client-document period short. **Which should govern?**

 **What we think.** Confidence: **medium**. Each period is the longest certain window for a different person who can act against us: HMRC, and the client. The report-delivery half of the client-document period exists for the client's own contract claim against us, which runs six years from our report. "Fail closed means sooner" is right for an unknown date, but not for two known ones: taking the earlier silently removes that half every time the report comes after the period end, which is the ordinary case.

 **An example.** A company's accounting period ends on **31 March 2027**, and we deliver our report on **15 September 2027**. The client-document period runs to **15 September 2033**; the tax-enquiry period ends on **31 March 2033**. Under the built rule the file is deleted after 31 March 2033 — **about five and a half months while the client could still bring a contract claim** the file would answer.

 **Our recommendation.** **For the client's document, the later of the two governs.** The cost is at most the months between the period end and our report. The alternative is to keep the rule and remove the report-delivery half from the client-document period, so the document stops promising a window the system does not keep.

 **What it unblocks.** Approving the client-document period consistently with what the system does; and one line in the developer's deletion-date calculation and its test.

```
ANSWER
......................................................................
Where both periods apply to a client's file:
   The later governs  /  The earlier governs, and the report-delivery half is removed

How confident are you?    High / Medium / Low

Answered by:                                     Date:

If you disagree with our recommendation, what should we do instead?


......................................................................
```

## Are the 20-year deliberate-behaviour window and the 15-year latent-damage longstop handled by legal hold rather than by the schedule?

 **Added 14 September 2026 by Phase 131. For the data protection officer, with the tax adviser.** Nothing is deleted today. But every six-year figure in the retention schedule rests on this.

 **The question.** HMRC can reopen a company's return up to **20 years** after the period end where behaviour was deliberate, and a negligence claim has a **15-year longstop**. The retention schedule keeps files for six years, on the footing that these rare outer cases are handled by a **legal hold** instead: an instruction on one specific file, placed when there is a reason, that stops its deletion until lifted. **Is that right?**

 **What we think.** Confidence: **medium**. Keeping every claim file for twenty years, to be ready for the small number that ever involve deliberate behaviour, is not proportionate under the storage-limitation principle. A longstop bars stale claims; it is not a period in which claims are expected. But this is the single assumption the six-year figure depends on, and it needs saying out loud.

 **An example.** A claim for the period ending **31 March 2027**. **On the schedule**, every such file is kept to **31 March 2047**. **With a legal hold**, it is deleted after six years unless a hold has been placed — for instance because HMRC has alleged deliberate behaviour, or a client has threatened a claim.

 **Our recommendation.** **Legal hold, not the schedule**, with the events that place a hold written down: an HMRC enquiry or allegation, a complaint or threatened claim, and a request from the tax adviser.

 **What it unblocks.** Both the tax-enquiry period and the client-document period at six years, as drafted.

```
ANSWER
......................................................................
The 20-year and 15-year outer cases:    Legal hold  /  On the schedule

Events that place a hold (add or remove):
   HMRC enquiry or allegation  /  complaint or threatened claim  /  tax adviser's request  /  other: ..........

How confident are you?    High / Medium / Low

Answered by:                                     Date:

If you disagree with our recommendation, what should we do instead?


......................................................................
```

## Which event starts our own six-year clock — delivering our report, or the accountant filing the return?

 **Added 14 September 2026 by Phase 131. For the data protection officer, with the tax adviser.** Nothing is deleted today.

 **The question.** Our own six-year clock, for a claim against us, is drafted to start from the later of the period end and **the date we deliver our report**. The alternative is **the date the client's accountant files the return** that uses it. **Which?**

 **What we think.** Confidence: **medium**. A client's contract claim against us runs from our breach, and in practice that is our report. The filing date is a fact we depend on someone else to tell us, and the field that would hold it is filled by nothing today. A clock that waits for a date nobody records is a clock that never starts.

 **An example.** We deliver our report on **15 September 2027**, and the accountant files the return on **31 March 2028**. From the report, the clock ends on **15 September 2033**; from filing, on **31 March 2034** — six and a half months later, and only if someone tells us the filing date.

 **Our recommendation.** **The later of the period end and our report delivery, as drafted.**

 **What it unblocks.** The client-document period's start date, and the field the developer has to fill for it.

## Should the lawful-basis assessment's retention rule name HMRC's discovery windows instead of the 12-month enquiry window plus a margin?

 **Added 14 September 2026 by Phase 131. For the data protection officer; the tax adviser supplies the windows.** Nothing is deleted today.

 **The question.** The retention rule in section 10 of the lawful-basis assessment keeps data until the later of the close of the **enquiry window** for the return and the close of any enquiry actually opened, plus a margin. But the window in which HMRC can really come back on a capital allowances claim is the **discovery** window: four years from the period end, six if careless, twenty if deliberate. **Should the rule name the discovery windows as the operative period, with the enquiry window as a minimum?**

 **What we think.** Confidence: **high**. As written, the rule produces a short period and quietly relies on the margin to carry the rest. Nobody reading it would know that the margin is doing four years of work.

 **An example.** A company's period ends **31 March 2027**, and its return is due **31 March 2028**. The enquiry window on an on-time return closes about **31 March 2029**. The careless-error discovery window runs to **31 March 2033**. **Under the rule as written, the margin has to carry the four years between them.**

 **Our recommendation.** **Yes.** Name the six-year careless-error window as the operative period, with the enquiry window as a minimum, and handle the 20-year deliberate window by legal hold.

 **What it unblocks.** The tax-enquiry period and its margin, as drafted.

How confident are you?    High / Medium / Low

## How is the signer of a statutory data protection letter described before the officer's appointment is signed?

 **Added 14 September 2026 by Phase 131. For the data protection officer and Lakhbir.** Nothing is blocked. A conservative rule is in force until this is answered.

 **The question.** The officer's instrument says the appointment "takes effect on signature and not before", and it is unsigned. The letters pack's current rule is: **sign for and on behalf of the controller, name the person dealing with the request, and do not use the title "Data Protection Officer"** until the instrument is signed and the officer's details are published and given to the Information Commissioner. The complaints acknowledgement now reads: "[Name], [until our Data Protection Officer's appointment takes effect: our director dealing with data protection / once it has taken effect: our Data Protection Officer], is dealing with it personally." **Is that the rule?**

 **What we think.** Confidence: **medium**. The controller is responsible for compliance whatever the officer's status, and naming a reachable person meets the duty to let people contact the officer. A letter that uses a title whose appointment has not taken effect describes someone as something they are not yet.

 **An example.** A rights request is answered in October 2026, before the instrument is signed. The letter is signed "for and on behalf of Caprebate", with the name of the person dealing with it, described as the director dealing with data protection — not as the Data Protection Officer.

 **Our recommendation.** **Confirm the rule.** The cheaper answer is to sign the instrument, whose remaining blanks need no research.

 **What it unblocks.** The signature block on every statutory letter template, and the complaints acknowledgement.

## How often is a person who has complained told how it is going, and is there an internal target for the outcome?

 **Added 14 September 2026 by Phase 131. For the data protection officer.** Nothing is blocked. An interim rule is in force until this is answered.

 **The question.** For a data protection complaint, the law makes telling the person about progress part of the duty, but sets **no cadence**, and sets **no outer deadline** for the outcome — only "without undue delay". The letters pack's interim rule is an update at least **every 28 days** while a complaint is open, and one immediately whenever the expected timescale changes. **Should that be the rule, and should there be an internal target for the outcome?**

 **What we think.** Confidence: **medium**. A duty with no date is a duty that slips, and "undue delay" is judged afterwards against a standard nobody set. The Information Commissioner's guidance (_What do we do when we receive a complaint?_, read 13 September 2026) sets no cadence either, and says an internal timeframe must never become a reason to take longer than a complaint needs.

 **An example.** A complaint arrives on **2 November**. With a one-month target the outcome is due by **2 December**; if it runs on, an update is owed by **30 November** and again by **28 December**. If it can be finished on **10 November**, it is finished on 10 November — the target is a ceiling, not a queue.

 **Our recommendation.** **An update at least every 28 days, and an internal target of one month from receipt for the outcome**, matching the month for a rights request so there is one number to remember. Neither is to be presented to anyone as a statutory deadline.

 **What it unblocks.** The progress-update entries in the complaints register, and one line in the complaint letters.

```

Internal target for the outcome:         One month from receipt  /  .......... /  none

How confident are you?    High / Medium / Low

Answered by:                                     Date:

If you disagree with our recommendation, what should we do instead?


......................................................................

## Will Caprebate ever charge a fee for a rights request?

 **Added 14 September 2026 by Phase 131. For the data protection officer, with Lakhbir.** Nothing is blocked. No fee letter is drafted.

 **The question.** The law lets a controller either charge a reasonable fee or refuse, but only where a request is manifestly unfounded or excessive. If a fee is charged, the month for answering runs from when it is paid. **Will we ever charge?**

 **What we think.** Confidence: **medium**. The burden of showing a request is manifestly unfounded or excessive is the same whether we charge or refuse. A fee produces a worse impression, and moves a deadline in a way that is easy to get wrong. Refusing with reasons is cleaner, and the person keeps their route to the Information Commissioner either way.

 **An example.** A person sends the same access request for the fourth time in two months, with nothing new in it. **Charging a fee:** the month starts again only when the fee arrives, and we need a ninth letter template to ask for it. **Refusing with reasons:** we write once, explain why, and tell them of their right to complain — with the same evidence either way.

 **Our recommendation.** **Never charge.** Where a request is manifestly unfounded or excessive, refuse it with reasons.

 **What it unblocks.** Whether a ninth letter template, asking for a fee, has to be drafted.

```

## Should the notice at the address question offer "answer the questions yourself instead"?

 **Added 14 September 2026 by Phase 131. For the data protection officer, with Lakhbir.** Nothing is blocked. The free register lookups at the address question stay switched off until the notice is published.

 **The question.** Free register lookups at the address question fire once the prospect has been informed by a published notice, on legitimate interests rather than consent. The new section 15 of the legitimate interests assessment, version 1.3, lists a safeguard it recommends but does not build: **a way for a prospect to decline the pre-filled lookups and type the answers themselves**, stated in the notice. **Should we offer it?**

 **What we think.** Confidence: **medium**. It strengthens the legitimate-interests position, because anyone who would rather not have registers searched for their property can simply say so in the moment. **It is not consent**: the basis stays legitimate interests, the figure is still given, and nothing is withdrawn from anyone who does not use it. It costs one link at the address question, and the questions the funnel already asks.

 **An example.** A care-home owner declines the pre-fill and types a floor area of **1,200 m²** and their bed numbers. No register is searched for the property, so the registered manager's details are never looked up at all. The estimate is built from their answers alone, and an "I don't know" goes to the cautious branch, exactly as it does today.

 **Our recommendation.** **Yes.** Build it, and switch it off with the register gate, so it goes live the moment the notice does.

 **What it unblocks.** The wording of the notice at the address question, which the officer approves before the lookups are switched on; and the list of safeguards in section 15 of the legitimate interests assessment.

```

......................................................................
Offer "answer the questions yourself instead" in the notice?    Yes / No

How confident are you?    High / Medium / Low

## Once the back office is live, which breach register is the register of record — the restricted monday.com board, or the one built into the back office?

 **Added 14 September 2026 by Phase 131. For the data protection officer, with Lakhbir.** Nothing is blocked today. Both registers are empty, and the back office is not deployed.

 **The question.** The law requires us to keep one record of every personal data breach, and today there are two places built to hold it. **The restricted monday.com board** exists and is live: a private board with 30 fields and a separate names annex. Read on 14 September 2026, each board has one owner and one subscriber, Lakhbir, and neither holds a single entry. It is the register the breach procedure describes. **The back office's own breach register** is built, switched off and not deployed, and holds nothing: an entry cannot be deleted, a correction to the time we became aware is kept beside the original rather than replacing it, it holds no names (only a reference to the restricted annex), and it does not read the board. **Once the back office is live, which of the two is the register?**

 **What we think.** Confidence: **high that two is the failure, medium on the answer**. Two stores for one statutory register mean an entry made in one is invisible in the other, and the officer can reach neither today. The back office's register is the stronger record: it refuses deletion, keeps every correction, and works out the 72 hours from awareness itself. But it only helps once it is deployed and the officer can sign in to it.

 **An example.** An incident on day 1 is logged on the board by Lakhbir. In month three the back office goes live, and an officer working there opens an **empty register**. When the regulator asks "show us your breach register", **there are two different answers**, and the one the officer can see is the wrong one.

 **Our recommendation.** **The back office becomes the register of record on the day it is deployed and the officer has an account there; until then, the board is.** On that day the board's entries (none today) are copied across with their original dates, the board is made read-only, and the breach procedure is amended in one change to say where the register now lives. **The names annex stays on its restricted board**, because the back-office entry holds only a reference to it, never a name.

 **What it unblocks.** The breach procedure's section on where the register lives; and the entry in FOR THE SOLICITOR, THE DPO AND THE DEVELOPER on where the breach register lives and who may write to it.

## When someone enquires on behalf of a company, is their contact data held on the contract basis or on legitimate interests?

 **Added 15 September 2026 by Phase 132a. For the data protection officer.** Nothing is blocked. But the officer approves the governance policies and the privacy notice together, and both now state one basis for every enquiry.

 **The question.** Since 15 September 2026 the privacy notice and the governance policies both put an enquiry made before engagement on the contract basis: steps taken at the person's request before a contract. That fits a sole trader or a partner, who would be a party to the contract. It fits less well someone enquiring for a company, because the contract would be with the company, not with them. **Which basis covers that person?**

 **What we think.** Confidence: **medium**. The regulator's guidance on the contract basis says it does not apply where the contract is with someone else. So for a person acting for a company, legitimate interests is the likelier basis, and the legitimate interests assessment would need to name them.

 **An example.** The finance manager of a group of three care homes emails for an estimate. Their name, work email and phone number are personal data; the contract, if the group signs, is with the company.

 **Our recommendation.** Say in the governance policies and in section 3 of the privacy notice that a sole trader or partner enquiring is on the contract basis, and someone enquiring for a company is on legitimate interests, under the existing assessment, which then names them.

 **What your answer unblocks.** Approving the governance policies' lawful-basis section and the notice's section 3 together, in words that match each other.

## Where will the healthcare property list come from, will it name anyone, and on what basis would we hold and use it?

 **Added 15 September 2026 by Phase 132c. For the data protection officer, with Lakhbir.** Nothing is blocked today: no such list exists in the system yet.

 **The question.** Under "Is it right that there is no existing client data to import?", Lakhbir confirms there is nothing to import, and adds that he "will be building a list of health care properties to import". That is a new source of data. A list of properties can name the people who own or run them — a dentist who is the practice's sole owner, a GP partner, an individual landlord — and if it is used to approach them, that is marketing. **Where will the list come from, will it name any individual, on what lawful basis would we hold it, and will anyone on it be contacted?**

 **What we think.** Confidence: **medium**. A list of business names, addresses and sectors, taken from sources whose terms allow commercial re-use, is low-risk. It stops being low-risk the moment it names individuals or feeds outreach. A named partner or sole owner is personal data. Electronic marketing to individuals, sole traders among them, generally needs their prior consent, while companies can be written to with a clear way to opt out. And our legitimate interests assessment covers lookups for a client who has asked us for a claim; it does not cover prospecting. What each register's terms allow its data to be re-used for is recorded, source by source, in the register licensing review.

 **An example.** A list of 400 dental practices is built from a public register. For 120 of them the owner shown is the principal dentist personally; the other 280 are limited companies at a practice address. Emailing the 120 to offer a claim, without their consent, is electronic marketing to individuals; holding their names for that purpose needs its own lawful basis and a privacy notice that says so. A letter to the 280 companies, with an opt-out, is on much firmer ground.

 **Our recommendation.** Build the list with business names, addresses and sectors only, from sources whose terms allow it, and keep individuals' names off it. If it will be used for outreach, decide the channel first — post, or email to companies, with a clear opt-out; nothing electronic to individuals without consent — and have the data protection officer approve a short assessment and the privacy notice wording before anyone is contacted.

 **What your answer unblocks.** The healthcare property list itself; any outreach from it; and a new source in the privacy notice and the record of processing.

## Lakhbir decides a breach as deputy for a company he has no affiliation with - what written authority does CAP REBATE LTD give him?

 **Added 18 September 2026 by Phase 133i. For the solicitor.** Nothing is blocked today: no breach has happened and the officer's instrument is not yet signed. But the first time the officer cannot be reached during a breach, the deputy's decision has to stand up.

 **The question.** On 18 September 2026 Lakhbir confirmed that he is Head of Business Development and has **no affiliation with CAP REBATE LTD**: he is not a director and does not act or sign for the company. He also confirmed that he **stays as the deputy who decides a personal data breach** when the data protection officer, Sadique Maskeen, cannot be reached. That decision is the company's: whether to tell the Information Commissioner within 72 hours (Article 33) and whether to tell the people affected (Article 34). Every such decision is then countersigned by the company, by a director. **What written authority must CAP REBATE LTD give a person outside the company before his decision on its behalf is valid, and what should it say?**

 **What we think.** Confidence: **medium**. A company can authorise someone who is not an officer or employee to take a defined decision for it, but the authority has to be given by the company and written down before it is used. We think a **board resolution, or a letter of authority signed by a director** (today Jalil Ahmed, the only director), is enough if it names Lakhbir Dhillon, limits the authority to the breach decision when the officer cannot be reached, says the company countersigns each decision, and says how and when it can be withdrawn. We also think he will need a written confidentiality undertaking, because deciding a breach means reading the register and possibly the names annex. The countersignature by a director then makes it a two-person check: the deputy decides, the company countersigns.

 **An example.** A laptop holding client files is stolen on a Friday evening and Sadique Maskeen is abroad and unreachable. Lakhbir decides on Saturday that the loss must be reported, and Jalil Ahmed countersigns for the company on Sunday. On Monday the Commissioner's office asks who took the decision. With a signed letter of authority dated before the breach, the answer is "our authorised deputy, under the board's written authority of [date], countersigned by a director". Without one, the company has let someone it says has no connection with it decide a statutory notification on its behalf, and has nothing to show that it did.

 **Our recommendation.** Draft a one-page letter of authority for a director to sign: the deputy's name, the one decision he may take and when (only when the officer cannot be reached within the time the 72-hour clock allows), the countersignature by a director, a confidentiality undertaking, access to the breach register only for that purpose, and withdrawal by the board in writing at any time. File it with the officer's instrument of appointment and name it in the breach procedure.

 **What your answer unblocks.** The deputy route in the breach register and incident procedure, the officer's instrument of appointment (which names the deputy), and who may be given access to the two restricted breach boards.

Anything else the deputy needs before he may decide (an agreement,
insurance cover, training)?

How confident are you?

Answered by:                                   Date:

```

## How long do we keep an enquiry sent from the website's form, if it never becomes a claim?

 **Added 18 September 2026 by Phase 133j. For the data protection officer.** Nothing is blocked today: the form is live only in the private test system, with invented data.

 **The question.** Since 18 September 2026 the website's "Request my estimate" form sends what a person types to the back end: their name, practice or company, email address, telephone number, property type and a message, and their tick agreeing to be contacted about the enquiry (not marketing consent). It is kept in its own list (`website_enquiries`). Sales then open it as a lead, which copies the person onto a contact, or close it. Today nothing deletes an enquiry, and no rule in the retention schedule names it. **How long should an enquiry be kept once it is closed, or once it has been opened as a lead, and what starts the clock?**

 **What we think.** Confidence: **medium**. A closed enquiry has done its job once it has been answered. One opened as a lead is kept as that lead and its contact, under the rules those already have, so the enquiry row itself need not outlive the lead's first contact.

 **An example.** A dental practice owner sends the form on 1 October 2026. Sales reply the next day and close it because the practice is leased and there is nothing to claim. Under our recommendation the row is deleted on 1 April 2027.

 **Our recommendation.** Delete a **closed** enquiry six months after it was closed. Delete an enquiry **opened as a lead** six months after it was opened, because the lead and its contact now hold what is needed. Delete an enquiry **never handled** twelve months after it arrived. The basis is legitimate interests: answering the enquiry the person sent. Add all three rules to the retention schedule and to the privacy notice's list of what we hold.

 **What your answer unblocks.** A deletion rule for `website_enquiries` in the retention register, and the privacy notice's line about website enquiries, before the form is used with real people.

contract)?

## How long do we keep client messages and the team chat?

 **Added 22 September 2026 by Phase 142. For the data protection officer.** Nothing is blocked today: the chat is live only in the private test system, with invented data. Nothing deletes a message until you answer.

 **The question.** Since 22 September 2026 every client page has a **Chat** with two threads. **With the client** holds what a client writes to us in their portal (and, once the mailbox is switched on, the replies they email to contactus@caprebate.com) and what we write back. **Team only** holds what our staff write to each other about a client — it is the same store as the old internal notes, so every note already there is in it. Both are free text, so both can hold personal data: a contact's name, a remark about a person, a figure. A file sent with a message is an ordinary document and keeps the document rules it already has. No message is ever edited or deleted by the system today, and **no rule in the retention schedule names either thread. How long should each be kept, and what starts the clock?** Both threads are now listed in the record of processing (the ICO registration content, sections 3.2 and 6), with the period marked "not set".

 **What we think.** Confidence: **medium.** A message is part of the client's file: it is how the claim was run, and it is what we would show HMRC or a complaint handler about what we told the client and when. So it should live exactly as long as the claim file — the same rule, not a separate number. A message on a company that never became a client has no claim file to follow, so it needs its own shorter period.

 **An example.** Corvale writes "Here is our lease" on 22 September 2026, and we reply the same day. Their claim closes in March 2027 and HMRC's enquiry window on that return closes in January 2029. Under our recommendation both messages — and our team's notes about Corvale — are kept until the claim file's date (January 2029 plus the margin the claim file rule sets), then deleted with it. A dental practice that writes once in October 2026 and never engages would have its messages deleted in October 2027.

 **Our recommendation.** **Messages on a company with a claim** — both threads — follow the claim file's rule, and are deleted with it. **Messages on a company that never had a claim** are deleted twelve months after the last message, the same as an enquiry that does not become a client. The basis is contract for a client's thread (running the engagement) and legitimate interests for the team thread and for a company that never engaged. Add both rules to the retention schedule and a line to the privacy notice: "messages you send us through your portal, and our staff's notes about your claim".

 **What your answer unblocks.** A deletion rule for `client_messages` and `internal_notes` in the retention register, and the job that deletes old messages. **Answere****d ****22 Sep****t****e****mb****e****r 2026 by Lakhbir: seven years.** Phase 142a built the job; Phase 142b switched it on at seven years, from the claim file's date or the last message. The privacy notice's line about messages now says seven years, in Sections 3 and 7 of the notice, marked DRAFT for the data protection officer, who is still asked to approve it.

        [X] another period: 7 years, from the claim file's date

Messages on a company that never had a claim — delete after:

        [ ] 12 months from the last message (recommended)
        [X] another period: 7 years from the last message

Should the team chat and the client chat follow the same rule?   Yes

Nobody has to decide anything about that: it is work for us to do, it is on the board as its own task, and it was removed from this document on 4 September because a task sitting among questions is one nobody answers. **Developers: the two new client questions are specified on that task, not here.**
